# WebLingo — Consolidated Architecture Specification

**Document:** `weblingo-architecture.md`  
**Status:** Consolidated architectural baseline through M0.6, including approved WL-T001–WL-T008 template composition and the WebLingo project name (12 November 2026)  
**Implementation status:** M0.6.1 solution scaffold generated (22 October 2026); renamed to WebLingo on 12 November 2026; M0.6.2–M0.6.7 implemented on 12 November 2026, a working compiler and register VM for the M0.6 subset, built and tested on Windows and Linux (§14, §15).  
**Convention:** **Approved** means explicitly accepted by the project owner. **Proposed** means an implementation recommendation requiring confirmation or validation. **Provisional** means deliberately undecided.

## 1. Executive overview

WebLingo is a JavaScript-inspired, native-oriented, cross-platform **server-side scripting language** designed to combine the low-friction deployment experience of PHP and classic ASP with modern language semantics, secure defaults, efficient execution, and first-class application development.

- Source scripts use **`.wlg`**; HTML/server template pages use **`.wasp`**.
- Page-oriented and application-oriented programming are equally first-class.
- The language is JavaScript-inspired but **does not promise ECMAScript compatibility**.
- The initial compiler, bytecode VM, runtime and hosting stack are implemented in **C#/.NET 10**.
- The long-term goal is to compile that C# implementation with the project's **independent Native C# compiler**, eliminating the .NET runtime dependency **without a C++ rewrite**. Native C# and WebLingo remain separate languages and runtime systems.
- The execution engine and platform services must be independent of IIS, ASP.NET Core, FastCGI, Kestrel, Windows and Linux.
- IIS is the priority deployment target; FastCGI and standalone HTTP hosting are architectural targets.

The approved project name is **WebLingo** (formerly WebScript). The intended project domain is **`weblingo.dev`**. The solution is `WebLingo.slnx`, project and namespace roots use `WebLingo`, and the CLI executable is `weblingo`. Scripts and modules are `.wlg` files, templates `.wasp` files; specification IDs and diagnostic codes have the prefix `WL` (`WL-BC002`, `WL1001`), and type names in source the prefix `WebLingo` (`WebLingoLexer`). They kept WebScript's `ws` (`.ws`, `.wsp`, `WS`, `Ws`) until the owner had them renamed, with no backward compatibility, on 14 November 2026. The technology of templates for web servers (§13) is **WASP**, Web Application Server Pages, by analogy to Active Server Pages, without "Active", which came from COM's ActiveX and Active Scripting, which WebLingo does not have: its pages are `.wasp` files, its command line `wasp`, in the project `WebLingo.Wasp.Cli` (the owner's decision, 15 November 2026, in place of WebLingo Pages, `.wlp` and `welp`, with no backward compatibility). Compiled bytecode is **WLBC**, WebLingo ByteCode, its files `.wlbc` files (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026, in place of WBC, before any such file was written).

### 1.1 Decision register

| ID / area | Decision | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Product direction | JavaScript-inspired, non-ECMAScript-compatible, native-oriented server scripting | Approved |
| Project naming | WebLingo, with `weblingo.dev` as the intended project domain; `.wlg` and `.wasp` files, `WL` IDs and codes, and `WebLingo` type names in source, in place of WebScript's `.ws`, `.wsp`, `WS` and `Ws`; WASP, Web Application Server Pages, the templates' technology, its command line `wasp`, in place of WebLingo Pages, `.wlp` and `welp`; WLBC, WebLingo ByteCode, the bytecode's format, its files `.wlbc`, in place of WBC (§1) | Approved (the names 14 November 2026; WASP 15 November 2026; WLBC 16 November 2026) |
| The request and the response in layouts and components | A layout's and a component's `param request;` and `param response;` are the host's, the request's own, unless the page gives others (§6.12) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Command lines | `weblingo` is strictly the scripts' runner (`run`, `check`, `compile`, `disasm`); `wasp` is the web handler: its pages, and serving applications, locally and under IIS (§8.4, §6.9) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| M0.14, hosting's design | A page's request and response are values its parameters take, `param request;` and `param response;`, instances of two built-in classes, `Request` and `Response`, read and changed through their members; a `.wlg` route handler is a module exporting a function for each HTTP method it answers, `get(request, response)`, any other method a 405; a request's path finds its file without the extension, `/` and a folder's path their `index`; a runtime error is a 500, its page showing the error in development and not in production; the host's core is a portable project of its own, `WebLingo.Hosting`; and an application's configuration, as a script's, is `weblingo.config`, in place of `weblingo.json` (§6.12), a site's renamed `wasp.config` the same day (below) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Configuration's names | A site's configuration, in its root, and a page's folder's under `wasp`, is `wasp.config`, in lower case, for Linux's sake, since it configures the WASP site, not the language; a script's folder's, under `weblingo`, stays `weblingo.config`; a site's `weblingo.config` is not read, nothing being kept for backward compatibility (§6.12) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| WL-G001 | Free and open-source project direction; release timing, scope, and MIT licensing proposal | Direction confirmed; release and licensing details proposed |
| Implementation | C#/.NET 10 initially, later Native C# compilation | Approved |
| WL-017 | Native C# portability contract and replaceable adapters | Approved |
| WL-002.3 | `var` mutable, `const` immutable; both block-scoped, in scope from their declaration to the end of the block; no `let` declaration keyword | Approved |
| Statements, operators and literals | The syntax M0.7 implements (§3.5): blocks, `true`, `false` and `null`, comparisons and logic, `if`, loops, `foreach`, strings, and functions, hoisted in their block; a hoisted function reading a variable before its declaration has run is a runtime error (WL2004) | Approved (12 November 2026; the hoisted function's read, 13 November 2026) |
| Hexadecimal and binary literals | `0x1F` and `0b1010`, int64s, their letters in either case, no octal; one beyond int64 WL1004, as a decimal one beyond it is (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Digit separators | `_` between two digits, one at a time, meaning nothing: `1_000_000`, `0.000_1`, `1e1_0`, `0xFF_FF`; never first, last, doubled, or beside a `.`, an `e`, a prefix or a suffix (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Rest parameters | JavaScript's, `function f(first, ...rest)`: the last parameter, an array of the arguments after the others, `[]` when there are none (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Spreading | `...values` into a call's arguments, `f(...args)`, and into an array's, a set's and a map's literals, `[...a, ...b]`, `#{...a}`, `{ ...defaults, ...given }`; anything `foreach` visits (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| String methods | `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf`, `contains`, `startsWith`, `endsWith`, `slice`, `split`, `trim`, `trimStart`, `trimEnd`, `toUpper`, `toLower`, `replace`, every one replaced, `repeat`, `padStart`, `padEnd` and `separator.join(array)`, with JavaScript's and C#'s other names for them as aliases, `includes`, `toUpperCase`, `toLowerCase`, `replaceAll`, `padLeft` and `padRight`; positions in Unicode scalar values; case by the invariant culture (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Trimming characters, removing a prefix or a suffix | `trim(chars)`, `trimStart(chars)` and `trimEnd(chars)`, the argument a set of characters, each a Unicode scalar value, any of them removed from the end or ends, as C#'s `Trim(chars)` and Python's `strip(chars)`, an empty one trimming nothing; and `removePrefix(text)` and `removeSuffix(text)`, a fixed text removed once, only where the string begins or ends with it, else the string unchanged, as Python 3.9's and Kotlin's, separate names keeping Python's trap out (`"report.txt".rstrip(".txt")` being `"repor"`), which the Standard Library Reference warns of (§3.5) | Approved (18 November 2026) |
| Array methods | `insert(i, v)`, `removeAt(i)`, `indexOf(v)`, `contains(v)`, `slice(start, end)`, `concat(other)`, `join(separator)`, `reverse()`, `sort(compare)`, `map(f)`, `filter(f)`, `reduce(f, initial)`, `find(f)`, `any(f)` and `all(f)`, with JavaScript's and C#'s other names as aliases where one exists, `includes`, `select`, `where`, `some` and `every`; `sort()` and `reverse()` giving a new array, the array unchanged, a compiler warning for a discarded result; `sort()` without a function ordering integers and numbers by value and strings by `<`, anything else, or a mix, WL3001 (§3.6) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Regular expressions | .NET's syntax, and its engine for now, Native C# then needing one that reads the same; a class only, `new Regex("a+b", "i")`, `r.test(s)`, `r.match(s)`, `s.replace(r, …)`, with no literal; and a limit on time against patterns that backtrack without end (§3.6) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| JSON | A built-in class, `Json.parse(text)` and `Json.stringify(value)`; a number read as an integer when it has no fraction or exponent and fits int64, a number otherwise; an instance written as its fields, as `write` writes them, a class's `toJson()` replacing them (§3.6) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Network access | `std:http`, an HTTP client, and `std:net`, TCP sockets, both first; under `weblingo` a script reaching any host, under `wasp` only those its site's `wasp.config` allows, `"network": { "allow": ["api.example.com"] }`, as COM's objects are allowed (§7.9) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Database access | One standard module for every database, `std:db`, `await Database.open("fish")`, `db.query(sql, values)` an array of maps, a map for each row, `db.execute(sql, values)` the rows changed, `db.transaction(async () => { … })`, and `using`; a statement's values named, `@name`, from a map; a database named by the configuration, `"databases"` in `wasp.config` and `weblingo.config`, or by a connection string in a script, both; a site's SQLite file outside its folder, its path in the configuration; ADO.NET's providers, Microsoft.Data.Sqlite and Microsoft.Data.SqlClient, in an adapter of their own, `WebLingo.Data.DotNet`; SqlClient's native network layer on Windows, under Microsoft's own terms, left out, its managed one used there too, so that what WebLingo ships stays MIT; a connection's operations one at a time, those a script starts without awaiting them in no defined order (§7.11) | Approved (16 November 2026; SqlClient's network layer, and the order of a connection's operations, 17 November 2026) |
| Remainder | `a % b`, beside `*` and `/`, its sign the dividend's, as C#'s and JavaScript's (`-7 % 3` is `-1`); an integer's by 0 an error of its own, WL3013, `DivisionByZeroError`; a number's IEEE 754's, as C# gives it (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Strings | Multi-line strings, `"""…"""` and `'''…'''`, raw, without the closing delimiter's indentation, their line breaks LF; interpolation, `$"…{x}…"` and `$$"""…{{x}}…"""`, any expression, `null` as nothing, braces doubled or escaped, formats after a colon, .NET's numeric format strings with the invariant culture, for integers and numbers, the subset in use written here, and alignments after a comma, as C# writes them (§3.5) | Approved (14 November 2026; formats and alignments, 14 November 2026) |
| Assignment's operators | `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `%=` and `??=`, and `++` and `--`, prefix and postfix, each an expression, as in C#, its target evaluated once (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Comparing strings | `< <= > >=` between two strings, by their Unicode scalar values, as a string's length and indexing count them; and `s.compare(other)`, -1, 0 or 1, for sorting (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| `switch` | A statement of cases with blocks and no `break`, `switch (x) { case 1, 2 { … } default { … } }`; an expression, C#'s, `x switch { 1 => "one", _ => "many" }`; and a case matching by a class too, `case Point p`, as a typed catch does (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Choosing a value | `c ? a : b`, as C#'s, and `if (c) a else b`, an expression, as Kotlin's, both; `a ?? b`, and `??=` (§3.5) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Collections | Arrays, maps and sets, with indexing, members, `length`, `foreach`, identity, text, set literals and optional access; a class visited by `foreach` through its `iterate()`, an iterator's `hasNext()` and `next()` giving each item, one at a time; arrays, maps, sets and strings visited as before, and having `iterate()` too (§3.6) | Approved (13 November 2026; `foreach`'s protocol, and its details, 15 November 2026) |
| Classes and disposal | Classes, instances, fields, `this`, methods, inheritance, `x?.name`, text, `dispose()` and `destructor()`, disposal once and chained, `using`, static members, `Object` and `equal()`, exceptions, `toString()`, a disposal that throws, typed catches, and the classes of values (§3.7) | Approved (13 November 2026) |
| No sealed classes | Every standard class may be extended, the standard modules' too, an instance of a class extending one having all its base's members; one extending a kind of value's class holding a value of that kind, `new Mine(…)` taking what its base's own making takes, `DateTime.utc(…)`'s arguments for a class extending `DateTime`; a class extending `Task` or `Module` making a task, completed by hand, or a module, defined in code, of its own; and a standard module's class created, then opened, its factories shorthand for both (§3.7, "No sealed classes"; built in parts, §9, "Standard classes extended, parts") | Approved (18 November 2026), in place of the classes of `Bytes`, `BytesBuilder`, `DateTime`, `Task`, `Module`, `Json` and `Html` sealed |
| Static methods' `this` | `this` in a static method the class it is called on, as JavaScript's, without a receiver: `static make() { return new this(); }`, `Green.make()` making a `Green`, a class's factories its own; `static make(this)` WL1001 (§3.7, "Static members") | Approved (20 November 2026), in place of `this` in a static method being WL2009 |
| Asynchrony | `async` functions, `await` of any value, tasks of the class `Task`, exceptions nothing awaits, the end of an execution, frames kept on the heap, cancellation, `async` disposal, templates' `await`, one worker per execution, scripts as `Module`s, and `t.forget()` (§3.8) | Approved (13 November 2026) |
| Tasks completed by hand | `new Task()` a pending task, which `t.complete(value)` and `t.fail(error)` complete, whoever holds it, C#'s split between a source and its task given up, and no factory; a task `new` did not make completed only by itself, `complete` or `fail` on one an error; completing a task a second time nothing, `complete` and `fail` giving whether they completed it; one still pending when the execution ends cancelled with its other tasks, nothing reported; and a program stuck awaiting one, nothing else able to run, given a runtime error at that `await`, of a class of its own extending `Error`, which a catch may catch, its message naming where the task was made and where it is awaited, again where it is stuck again (§3.8, "Tasks completed by hand") | Approved (18 November 2026); the error's class, `DeadlockError`, and its code, WL3020, Proposed |
| Modules | `import`, static and dynamic; a module run once per execution; `export`; paths; cycles; `param`; a script's parameters; the standard library and the built-ins as modules; native modules, an assembly whose `IWebLingoNativeModule` registers functions and classes, loaded into the host's context from a configured scheme's folder only, `std:system` among them, schemes configured by `weblingo.config` (a site's `wasp.config`) and the command line, `std/` beside the runtime; native classes' properties and indexers, their members called by the VMs directly, and a native function's arguments read where they are, the built-in classes the VMs implement keeping the VMs' own members, `std:core` dropped, once measured; a native class's instance holding one host object, which only its class reaches; `std:fs`, asynchronous by default, `…Sync` too, UTF-8 unless an encoding is named, any file the process may, `IOError` (WL3011) (§3.9) | Approved (13 November 2026; native modules, and native classes' host objects, 14 November 2026; `std:core` and its measurement, and `std:fs`, 15 November 2026; the built-in classes keeping the VMs' own members, `std:core` dropped, 15 November 2026) |
| `std:fs`'s bytes, folders and copies | Whole files as bytes, `readBytes`, `writeBytes` and `appendBytes`; a `File` opened for its bytes, `"rb"` or `"wb"`, `read(count)` up to that many, `null` at its end, and `write(bytes)`, or to append bytes, `"ab"`; `isFolder(path)`; and `copy(source, target)`, a file copied as it is, its bytes never reaching the script; each with its `Sync` form (§3.9, "Files: `std:fs`") | Approved (18 November 2026; `"ab"` the owner's request the same day) |
| Text written on Windows | `weblingo` and `wasp` write their output and their errors as UTF-8 on Windows, as on Linux: without a byte-order mark to a file or a pipe, and to a console through Windows's own Unicode console output, whatever its code page, as Python and Node.js write, in place of the console's code page, .NET's default, which lost every letter outside it ("Text Written on Windows: UTF-8"); a served page's response UTF-8 as before | Approved (18 November 2026) |
| A script run by its name | `weblingo script.wlg` the same as `weblingo run script.wlg`, its parameters after it; and a first line `#!/usr/bin/env weblingo`, which the lexer skips, so that a `.wlg` file made executable runs by its name, as a Python script does (the owner's answer, 18 November 2026, "Minimal WebLingo Installer: Linux", Q1's (a)). Built (19 November 2026): the command, a first argument ending in `.wlg` or `.wlbc` taken as `run`'s, for `weblingo` only (`WebLingoCommandLineProgram.RunsFileNamedFirst`), anything else a usage error as before; and, on Windows, `weblingo` started in a console window of its own, as a double-click starts it, waiting for a key before it ends, where its input and output are the console's (`WebLingoConsoleWindow`, Proposed), so that `Scripts/add-wlg-file-type.wlg` registers `weblingo.exe` itself for `.wlg` files, which Windows's "open with" choice then offers, where `cmd.exe`, which it hides, did not, its command running `weblingow.exe` since 20 November 2026 ("Console module"); the first line waits for that task | Approved (18 November 2026) |
| Versions | One version for the whole solution, every assembly's, set once in `Directory.Build.props`, `0.N.0-pre-alpha` for M0.N, the last milestone landed whole, raised by the task that lands a milestone, `0.20.0-pre-alpha` when decided; `weblingo --version` printing `WebLingo 0.20.0-pre-alpha` and `wasp --version` `WASP 0.20.0-pre-alpha`, in place of the milestones each named, `WebLingo M0.6 (pre-alpha)` and `WASP M0.12 (pre-alpha)`; and each printing it first when run without arguments, before how it is used | Approved (18 November 2026) |
| COM objects (Windows) | A native module, on Windows only, `windows:com`, `create(progId)` and `get(moniker)` giving `ComObject`s, their members through `IDispatch`, written as any member, the default member the indexer, `VARIANT`s mapped to values, `ComError` (WL3012), each worker a single-threaded apartment, its objects its own, COM allowed only by the application's configuration, `IDispatch` called through function pointers; COM's currency and decimals as decimals, its arrays of bytes as `Bytes`, and a date going out as `VT_DATE` (§3.10) | Its name, members, threads, permission and interop approved (15 November 2026), the rest, and decimals, bytes and dates, 17 November 2026; built (17 November 2026, M0.17) |
| Templates (M0.12) | The language is independent of templates; a template compiler extends the language's, with a program of its own; text, statements across blocks, escaping, `null`, line breaks, `include`, layouts and their parameters, components found at run time and their declared parameters, and codes WL5001 on (§13, "Contracts"); the technology's name, `import`, default values, and asynchrony, which comes first, still open; a block's code read as WebLingo's tokens, so that `%>` in a string or a comment does not end it; the application's root under `wasp`, `--root`, the page's folder when it is left out | Approved (13 November 2026; a block's end and the root under `wasp`, 14 November 2026) |
| M0.1 | Mandatory semicolons; no automatic semicolon insertion | Approved |
| M0.1 | Numeric categories int64, IEEE binary64, decimal128 | Approved; decimals .NET's `decimal` for now (16 November 2026, "Decimals" below) |
| Decimals | .NET's `decimal`, 28 or 29 digits, until Native C#, in place of decimal128 for now; an integer with a decimal converted exactly, `1.5m + 1` being `2.5m`; written with its fewest digits and its `m`, `1.50m` as `1.5m` (§3.2) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| M0.1 | String indexing and `length` count Unicode scalar values | Approved |
| M0.2 | WebLingo tracing GC, initially nonmoving mark-and-sweep | Approved |
| M0.1/M0.2 | `using` blocks for deterministic resource cleanup | Approved |
| WL-L011 | Canonical `dispose()`; optional `destructor()` declaration sugar | Approved |
| WL-T001–WL-T008 | Includes, layouts, and parameterized components all required in the first template-capable release | **Approved** |
| Template named sections | Optional page-specific layout sections | Proposed extension; not yet approved |
| WL-BC002 | Register VM preferred, stack VM benchmark required before final decision | **Provisional** |
| WL-BC002, until decided | Both VMs are kept, and every new feature is built on both, until property access, async suspension, template output and I/O-bound workloads can be measured too; those measured, both still, until something more is; and WLBC, a format for each VM's bytecode in one container (§5.6, §9) | Approved (13 November 2026; both kept still, every workload measured, and WLBC for both, 15 November 2026) |
| WL-BC002 comparison | The stack VM in a project of its own, `WebLingo.VM.Stack`, its code generator in `WebLingo.Compiler`; a console benchmark, `WebLingo.Benchmarks`, timing with `Stopwatch`, with no new package; the register VM's dispatch tuned for speed before the two are compared (§5.6) | Approved (13 November 2026) |
| M0.3 encoding | Variable-length register operands; jump targets as instruction indices, on disk as in memory (§5.3) | Operands Proposed; targets Approved (16 November 2026) |
| M0.13, WLBC | A `.wlbc` holds one VM's bytecode, its header saying which, each VM refusing the other's; each section's CRC-32 in its directory entry; `weblingo compile`, and `run` and `disasm` of a `.wlbc`, `run` of a `.wlg` writing none; the container built as §5.3 proposes, and kept Proposed, nothing frozen until the work is done (§5.3) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| VM-specific bytecode filenames | The compiler writes `.wlbr` for the register VM and `.wlbs` for the stack VM. The verified header identifies the VM (§5.3) | Approved (20 November 2026) |
| VM configuration | `weblingo.config` and `wasp.config` accept `"vm": "register"` or `"vm": "stack"`. Source defaults to register. Applies to `weblingo run`, `check`, and `compile`, and to `wasp run`, `check`, `serve`, and `fastcgi`. An explicit compile flag overrides the setting. With no setting, the verified header selects the VM for compiled files. With a setting, `run` and `check` refuse a bytecode file for the other VM. VS Code debugging keeps the register VM (§5.7, §8.4) | Approved (20 November 2026) |
| WASP and compiled bytecode | WASP never writes a bytecode file: wherever it runs pages, in `wasp` and in the web hosts, it compiles and caches a page, and what the page includes, imports, renders or is laid out by, in memory only (§5.1, WL-T007) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Bytes | A value of a kind of its own, `Bytes`, immutable, as a string is, built into both VMs: `b.length`, `b[i]`, an integer from 0 to 255, `b.slice(start, end)`, `b.text()`, decoding UTF-8, or `b.text("latin1")`, `Bytes.from("text")`, encoding it, `==` comparing their contents, `write(b)` writing `Bytes:[104, 105]`; a mutable builder and `Bytes.concat([parts])`, so that building bytes costs no O(n²); a comparison in constant time, for secrets; and bytes as hexadecimal and Base64 text and back, `b.toHex()`, `b.toBase64()`, `Bytes.fromHex(text)` and `Bytes.fromBase64(text)`, RFC 4648's Base64 with its padding, its URL-safe alphabet by an argument, `b.toBase64(true)` (§3.2) | Approved (16 November 2026; hexadecimal and Base64, 17 November 2026) |
| Dates and times | A kind of value of its own, immutable, built into both VMs, its class `DateTime`; an instant with its offset from UTC, as .NET's `DateTimeOffset` has them; to the millisecond; UTC and the server's own zone only, for now (other zones since, "Time zones" below); formatted and parsed by .NET's date and time format strings, standard and custom, with the invariant culture, in an interpolation too, `{d:yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm}`, the subset in use written into this specification; written `DateTime:2026-11-16T14:30:00Z`, as a classed value is; and moved and measured by methods and numbers, `d.addHours(2)`, `b - a` a number of milliseconds (§3.2) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Time zones | A date in any zone the platform knows, beside UTC and the server's: the platform's zones, Windows' and ICU's through .NET, which may differ from one server to another; named by their IANA names, `d.toZone("Europe/Warsaw")` and `DateTime.inZone("Europe/Warsaw", year, month, day, …)` (§3.2) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| M0.14.7, streaming | A page's output kept until it reaches about 64 KB, then sent as it is written, so that most pages are still a 500 on an error, and a larger one ends unfinished; a page whose output a slow client has not yet taken waits as at an `await`, its worker running other requests' tasks meanwhile, for at most 30 seconds, or the time `wasp.config`'s `"sendTimeout"` gives (§6.12) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| Deployment | WebLingo installed once on a server and shared by every site, as PHP is: `wasp`, which a site's `web.config` names, `weblingo` and the standard modules; a site holds only its own files, its pages beside its `web.config`, as a PHP site's are (§6.9) | Approved (16 November 2026) |
| M0.3 numbers | IEEE 754 decimal128 BID binary representation | Proposed |
| M0.3 exceptions | Table-driven exception and cleanup regions | Approved (13 November 2026, §3.7) |
| M0.3 dispatch | Initial C# `switch` interpreter | Proposed |
| WL-H009 | ASP.NET Core adapter first for IIS | Approved |
| WL-H012 | Worker-affine VM request execution contexts | Approved |
| WL-H013 | Logical application isolation in a shared runtime | Approved |
| WL-H004 | Atomic application generations for hot reload | Approved |
| WL-P007 | WebLingo-owned asynchronous operation model | Approved |
| WL-P004 | Both synchronous and asynchronous filesystem operations | Approved |
| WL-P013 | Worker-local mutable heaps, explicit cross-worker transfers | Approved |
| WL-P016 | Native portability inventory and CI checks | Approved |
| WL-S001–S016 | M0.6 solution scaffolding and initial implementation contracts | Approved |
| M0.6 implementation default | Register VM first; stack comparison later | Adopted M0.6 default; VM architecture still provisional |
| M0.6 testing default | xUnit on .NET 10 | Adopted M0.6 default |
| M0.6 implementation choices | Lexical, syntax, runtime and CLI choices made where this specification is silent, while implementing M0.6.2 onward (§15.2) | Each row of §15.2 has its own status; the lexical and syntax ones, and the binding ones but "Compilation stages", the bytecode ones but "Compiler output", and the runtime and CLI ones asked about, approved 12 November 2026; the rest are Proposed |
| Repository documents | One copy of this specification, `Documents/weblingo-architecture.md`; documents in the repository's root `Documents/` folder; a shallow folder structure, with no `docs/architecture/` folder; the whole repository may become public (§8.2) | Approved (12 November 2026) |

**Approval boundary:** M0.6 was approved as a specification. This does not imply the repository or implementation already exists. Detailed M0.3 encoding choices were recommended but not separately approved; the architectural decision between stack and register VMs remains open.

---

## 2. Product and deployment model (WL-001–WL-016 overview)

### 2.1 Goals

1. Simple deployment: copy `.wlg` and `.wasp` files into a configured site; runtime compilation and caching without Visual Studio, MSBuild or npm at deployment.
2. Strong server-side language with lexical scoping, closures, classes, modules, async execution and deterministic resource cleanup.
3. Secure template rendering by default; capabilities constrain filesystem, network, database and native extension access.
4. Portable compiler and execution engine; host-neutral interfaces.
5. Fast startup and request execution, bounded memory and streaming I/O.
6. Hot reload without exposing partially initialized application state.
7. Versioned, verified portable bytecode (WLBC).

### 2.2 Source types and template syntax

| Syntax | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `.wlg` | WebLingo module or script |
| `.wasp` | HTML plus server-side WebLingo template |
| `<% statements %>` | Execute statements |
| `<%= expression %>` | Output escaped expression |
| `<%- expression %>` | Output raw expression (security-sensitive) |
| `<%# comment %>` | Server-side template comment |

Template composition includes **mandatory first-release includes, layouts, and parameterized components** (WL-T001–WL-T008; see §13). Template escaping is **on by default**, and context-sensitive: a value is escaped for where it stands in the page's HTML, its text, an attribute, a URL, a script or a style, as Go's `html/template` escapes it (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, §13, "Context-aware escaping"); raw output must be explicit and subject to security review. Templates should stream output with backpressure and support asynchronous operations.

### 2.3 Modules and application structure

- Module graph and route table are associated with an application generation.
- Source compilation and bytecode caching are runtime services.
- `.wasp` pages and `.wlg` route handlers are equally supported.
- A generation compiles its modules once; a module runs once per execution, a request's, its instance the request's own (§3.9, the owner's decision of 14 November 2026, in place of running once per generation); a module that fails to compile prevents the generation from becoming active.
- Standard library and database access use provider interfaces.

---

## 3. Language semantics (WL-002.3, WL-L001–WL-L011)

### 3.1 Declarations and scope — approved

```javascript
var count = 1;      // mutable lexical binding
const limit = 10;   // immutable lexical binding
```

- `var` has **modern JavaScript `let`-style block scope**, not historical JavaScript function scope.
- `const` has the same lexical scope but disallows reassignment.
- `let` is **not** a WebLingo declaration keyword.
- A declaration takes effect where it stands, after its initializer: from there to the end of the block, the name means the declared variable; before it, and within the initializer, the name means what it means outside the block (an outer declaration, or nothing, which is an error). There is no temporal dead zone.
- Redeclaration in the same scope is an error; nested shadowing is allowed.
- Implicit global creation is prohibited.
- Per-iteration bindings are correctly captured by closures.
- Every declaration, `var` and `const` alike, requires an initializer: `var x;` is a syntax error. A variable therefore always has a value (there is no `undefined`).
- `const` does not deeply freeze referenced objects.
- Simple statements require semicolons; there is **no ASI**.

### 3.2 Value categories and operations

The language model proposed in M0.1/M0.2 includes:

| Category | Semantics |
|---|---|
| `null` | Single null value; no `undefined` |
| Boolean | Strict Boolean conditions; no implicit truthiness |
| Integer | Signed 64-bit, checked overflow |
| Number | IEEE 754 binary64 |
| Decimal | Exact decimal arithmetic, literal suffix `m`: .NET's `decimal` for now (decimal128 approved first) |
| String | Immutable Unicode string; scalar-value indexing and length |
| Bytes | Binary data, distinct from strings |
| Date and time | An instant, to the millisecond, with its offset from UTC |
| Array | Indexed collection |
| Map / Set | Keyed collections |
| Object / Class | Reference identity, class metadata |
| Function | Callable closure |
| Task | Async computation abstraction |
| Native resource | Handle with deterministic disposal |

Additional proposed semantics:

- **Bytes** (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026, M0.14.7.5's question): a value of a kind of its own, `Bytes`, immutable, as a string is, built into both VMs, as strings and arrays are: `b.length`, `b[i]`, an integer from 0 to 255, `b.slice(start, end)`, `b.text()`, decoding UTF-8, or `b.text("latin1")`, `Bytes.from("text")`, encoding it, `==` comparing their contents, and `write(b)` writing `Bytes:[104, 105]`; with, besides, a mutable builder and `Bytes.concat([parts])`, so that bytes are built without O(n²) cost, and a comparison in constant time, for secrets. Bytes are written as text, and read from it, as members of `Bytes`, `b.toHex()` and `b.toBase64()`, and its class's `Bytes.fromHex(text)` and `Bytes.fromBase64(text)`, Base64 RFC 4648's, with its padding, and its URL-safe alphabet by an argument, `b.toBase64(true)` (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, "Bytes as Text: Hexadecimal and Base64", rather than functions of `std:crypto`). As built: §15.2, "Bytes". By the owner's decision of 18 November 2026, its class and `BytesBuilder` are `std:bytes`'s, imported to name them, `import { Bytes } from "std:bytes";`, bytes keeping their members without the import (§3.9, "The standard library").
- **Dates and times** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): a kind of value of its own, immutable, built into both VMs, its class `DateTime`; an instant with its offset from UTC, as .NET's `DateTimeOffset` has them; to the millisecond; UTC and the server's own zone only, for now (other zones since, "Time zones" below); formatted and parsed by .NET's date and time format strings, standard and custom, with the invariant culture, in an interpolation too, `{d:yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm}`, the subset in use written into this specification; written `DateTime:2026-11-16T14:30:00Z`, as a classed value is; and moved and measured by methods and numbers, `d.addHours(2)`, `b - a` a number of milliseconds. As built: §15.2, "Dates and times". By the owner's decision of 18 November 2026, its class is `std:datetime`'s, imported to name it, `import { DateTime } from "std:datetime";`, a date keeping its members without the import (§3.9, "The standard library").
- **Time zones** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): a date in any zone the platform knows, beside UTC and the server's; the zones are the platform's, Windows' and ICU's through .NET, which may differ from one server to another, not a database shipped with WebLingo; a script names one by its IANA name, `d.toZone("Europe/Warsaw")` being the same instant on that zone's clocks, and `DateTime.inZone("Europe/Warsaw", year, month, day, …)` a date made from its parts there, as `DateTime.local(…)` makes one in the server's. As built: §15.2, "Time zones".
- **Decimals** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): a kind of value of its own, its class `Decimal`, built as .NET's `decimal`, 28 or 29 digits, until Native C#, in place of decimal128 for now; `1.5m` its literal; an integer with a decimal converted exactly (`1.5m + 1` is `2.5m`), a binary64 with one only by an explicit conversion; and written with its fewest digits and its `m`, `1.50m` as `1.5m`. As built: §15.2, "Decimals".
- `7 / 2` yields `3.5`, not integer truncation.
- Integer overflow throws rather than silently wrapping.
- Decimal/binary floating-point mixing requires explicit conversion.
- Object equality uses identity unless a specific operation says otherwise.
- Missing property access throws; optional access may return `null`.
- Single class inheritance, no exposed JavaScript prototype chain.
- Closures capture binding cells, not merely initial values.
- Functions have defined arity/default/rest behavior; `switch` has no implicit fallthrough.

These details were included in the M0.1/M0.2 design; the **five explicitly approved M0.1/M0.2 choices** are semicolons, numeric categories, tracing GC, `using`, and Unicode scalar-value string indexing. Other language semantics remain the proposed specification unless separately approved.

### 3.3 String representation

- **Approved observable semantics:** `length` and indexing count Unicode scalar values (not UTF-8 bytes, UTF-16 code units, or grapheme clusters).
- **Proposed internal representation:** immutable UTF-8 strings, with optional indexing acceleration where needed.
- Malformed Unicode handling and normalization policies need explicit test cases.

### 3.4 Resource disposal — WL-L011 approved

The **canonical WebLingo disposal protocol** is `dispose()`. A class may optionally declare `destructor()` as alternative syntax for implementing the **same** disposal slot. The language does **not** use `~ClassName()` syntax.

```javascript
class FileWriter {
    constructor(path) {
        this.file = File.openWrite(path);
    }

    destructor() {
        this.file.close();
    }
}

using (var writer = new FileWriter("output.txt")) {
    writer.write("Hello, WebLingo!");
}
```

- Callers use `writer.dispose()` even if the class declares `destructor()`.
- `using` performs deterministic, exception-safe disposal on scope exit.
- Disposal releases resources; it **does not free the GC-managed object's memory**.
- Aliases remain references to the disposed object; resource-dependent operations reject use after disposal (proposed `ResourceClosedError`).
- Disposal must be idempotent; the resource cannot be released twice.
- A class should not declare both `dispose()` and `destructor()` directly; compiler error is recommended.
- Compiler/class metadata should map both declaration forms to one disposal slot.
- **Proposed inheritance rule:** compiler-generated derived-to-base cleanup, each implementation once, including when derived cleanup throws.
- **Proposed state model:** Active → Disposing → Disposed; partial failure must not silently reactivate the resource.
- GC finalization may provide a fallback for native handles, but **must never be relied on for timely cleanup**.
- No automatic deterministic destruction merely because a variable leaves scope.
- Native C# object destructors are a **different** mechanism from WebLingo `dispose()`.

**Open implementation contract:** define async disposal policy and interaction between cancellation, outstanding I/O and cleanup. Synchronous `using` is the initial approved mechanism.

### 3.5 Statements, operators and literals — approved 12 November 2026

The syntax M0.7 implements (§9, "M0.7 parts"). Everything here was approved by the owner on 12 November 2026, except where a point says it is still open.

- **Blocks.** `{ statements }` is a statement, with no `;` after its `}`. A block opens a scope (§3.1): a declaration inside it is in scope from where it stands to the block's `}`, and may shadow an outer name. An empty block `{}` is allowed; a lone `;` is not a statement.
- **Literals.** `true`, `false` and `null` are keywords, as are `Infinity` and `NaN` (§15.2, "Binary64 literals"). An integer may be written in hexadecimal, `0x1F`, or in binary, `0b1010`, the prefix's letter and the digits in either case, without octal; one beyond int64 is WL1004, as a decimal one beyond it is (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026; as built, §15.2, "Hexadecimal and binary literals"). A numeric literal may have `_` between two of its digits, one at a time, which means nothing, `1_000_000`, `0.000_1`, `1e1_0`, `0xFF_FF`, `1_000.50m`, but never first, last, doubled, or beside a `.`, an `e`, a prefix or a suffix (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026; as built, §15.2, "Digit separators").
- **Equality.** `==` and `!=` give a Boolean and never convert, except that an int64 and a binary64 compare as numbers (`1 == 1.0` is true; `NaN == NaN` is false, as IEEE 754 says). `null == null` is true. Values of different kinds are unequal, which is not an error. There is no `===`.
- **Ordering.** `< <= > >=` take numbers (int64 and binary64 compare as numbers), and give a Boolean; anything else is an error (WL3001). Two strings compare by their Unicode scalar values, never by culture, and `s.compare(other)` gives -1, 0 or 1 by the same order (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026); two dates by their instants (§3.2); a string and a number stay WL3001.
- **Logic.** `!`, `&&` and `||` take Booleans only, with no truthiness (WL3001 otherwise); `&&` and `||` short-circuit.
- **Precedence**, loosest first: assignment, `=` and its compound forms (right-associative), `c ? a : b` (right-associative), `??` (right-associative), `||`, `&&`, `== !=`, `< <= > >=`, `+ -`, `* / % \`, prefix `++` and `--`, unary `- + !`; binary operators are left-associative.
- **Remainder** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `a % b`, of integers and of numbers, as tight as `*` and `/`; an integer's remainder has the dividend's sign, as C#'s and JavaScript's has (`-7 % 3` is `-1`, `7 % -3` is `1`), and by -1 is 0, even for the smallest int64; an integer's remainder by 0 is an error of its own, WL3013, of the class `DivisionByZeroError`, which `/` does not need, its result a number; a number's remainder, or an integer's with a number, is IEEE 754's as C# gives it (`5.5 % 2` is `1.5`, `7.0 % 0` is `NaN`); and a class may declare `static operator %`.
- **Integer division** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Mathematics", Visual Basic's operator, in place of none, a method or Python's `//`): `a \ b`, two integers' quotient, rounded towards zero, as C#'s and Java's integer division and Visual Basic's `\` round it, pairing with `%`, whose sign is the dividend's, so that `a == (a \ b) * b + a % b`, `-7 \ 2` being `-3`; integers only, a number or a decimal WL3001, made an integer first by `math.floor` and the others; by 0 a `DivisionByZeroError`, WL3013, as `%`'s; the smallest integer by -1 an `OverflowError`, WL3002; as tight as `*`, `/` and `%`; with `\=`, as `%=`; and a class's `static operator \`, as its `%`. As built (18 November 2026): an operation of its own on each VM, `IntegerDivide`, the last of each VM's operations, the runtime's arithmetic giving its errors; in an interpolation's hole too, `$"{a \ b}"`, as `\` outside a string meant nothing before; measured against `main`, alternately, every workload the same, the stack VM's `loops` and `closures` measured eight rounds, within the machine's noise.
- **Assignment's operators** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `x += v`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=` and `%=` assign `x op v`, and `x ??= v` assigns `v` only when `x` is `null`; `++x`, `x++`, `--x` and `x--` add or subtract 1, an int64's overflow WL3002 as `+`'s is. Each is an expression, as in C#: an assignment gives the value assigned, and postfix `x++` the value before it. Its target is a variable, an element or a member, evaluated once (`a[f()] += 1` calls `f` once); a constant's is WL2003, and anything else's WL1007.
- **Choosing a value** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `c ? a : b`, as C#'s, and `if (c) a else b`, an expression, as Kotlin's, its `else` required, both choosing by a Boolean (WL3001 otherwise) and evaluating only the value chosen; `a ?? b`, `a` unless it is `null`, `b` evaluated only then. An interpolation writes a conditional in parentheses, `{(c ? a : b)}`, its `:` otherwise beginning a format, as in C#.
- **`switch`** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): a statement, `switch (value) { case 1, 2 { … } case Point p { … } default { … } }`, whose first case that matches runs its block, else `default`'s, no case running into the next and none needing a `break`; and an expression, as C#'s, `value switch { 1, 2 => "low", Point p => p.x, _ => "other" }`. A case matches by `==`, against any of its values, or by a class, as a typed catch does, its value then named in its block or its arm, `Point p`.
- **`if`.** `if (condition) statement`, with an optional `else statement`; a branch is any statement, and an `else` belongs to the nearest `if` without one. The condition must be a Boolean at run time (WL3001 otherwise).
- **Loops.** `while (condition) statement`, `for` and `do … while`, each with a strict Boolean condition; `break;` and `continue;` act on the innermost loop, and are a compile-time error outside one. A loop is bounded only by the execution's instruction budget (WL3003).
- **`for`.** `for (init; condition; step) statement`, as in C: `init` is a declaration or an expression, or empty; `condition` a Boolean expression, or empty, meaning `true`; `step` an expression, or empty. A `var` declared in `init` is scoped to the loop and gets a new binding on each iteration, so that closures capture each one (§3.1).
- **`do … while`.** `do statement while (condition);`, ending with a `;`.
- **`foreach`.** `foreach (var item in items) statement` loops over a collection (not JavaScript's `for … of`). The loop variable is declared with `var` only, and gets a new binding on each iteration. Over a map, each entry is a key and a value, `foreach (var (key, value) in map)`, with the destructuring syntax decided when maps are. It comes with the collections.
- **Strings.** Literals in double or single quotes (`"a"`, `'a'`), alike in meaning, each on one line. Escapes: `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\0`, `\\`, `\'`, `\"`, and `\u{…}`, which names a Unicode scalar value in at most six hex digits (no surrogates). Any other escape is WL1009, an unterminated string WL1010. `+` concatenates two strings; a string and a number are not joined, and nothing converts implicitly (WL3001). `length` and indexing (§3.3) come with member access, later. Multi-line strings (approved 14 November 2026): three double quotes, `"""…"""`, or three single quotes, `'''…'''`, more of them for a string that holds three; raw, `\` a character like any other; the whitespace before the closing delimiter's column removed from every line, as C# removes it, and neither the line break after the opening delimiter nor the one before the closing one part of the string; and every line break in one LF, whatever the source file's. Interpolation, designed with them, for single-line strings too (approved 14 November 2026): a string marked with `$` before it interpolates, `$"Hello, {name}"` and `$"""…{name}…"""`, a string without it keeping its braces as they are; an interpolation holds any expression; a value is written as `write` writes it, an instance's `toString()` included, `null` as nothing, and so a `toString()` that an `extend` block gives a kind of value built into the VMs, which `write` does not call, is not called there either, `$"{5}"` being `5` under `extend Integer { toString() { … } }` (the owner's decision, 17 November 2026, in place of an interpolation's calling it, as `x.toString()` does); in a single-line interpolated string, a brace is written doubled, `{{` and `}}`, or escaped, `\{` and `\}`; in a multi-line one, raw, as C# has it, as many `$` before it as the braces that open an interpolation, `$$"""{ "name": {{name}} }"""`; and a format may follow a colon, `{price:0.00}`. A format is one of .NET's numeric format strings, standard or custom (`N2`, `0.00`, `X4`, `E3`), applied with the invariant culture to an integer or a number, a format given any other value being WL3001; and the subset of them WebLingo keeps is written into this specification as it is used, so that another toolchain (Native C#) knows what to give (approved 14 November 2026). An alignment may follow a comma, before any format, as C# writes it, `{name,10}` and `{name,-10}`: the value's text padded with spaces to that width, on the left when it is positive, and on the right when it is negative (approved 14 November 2026). The formats in use, and so kept: the standard `D`, `E`, `F`, `N` and `X`, each with a precision or without, and custom formats of `0`, `#`, `.`, `,` and `;`, which separates a negative number's format from a positive one's; as .NET 10 rounds them, a standard format rounds a number exactly halfway to the even digit (`{0.125:F2}` is `0.12`), and a custom one away from zero (`{0.125:0.00}` is `0.13`).
- **String methods** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `indexOf`, `lastIndexOf`, `contains`, `startsWith`, `endsWith`, `slice`, `split`, `trim`, `trimStart`, `trimEnd`, `toUpper`, `toLower`, `replace`, every one replaced, `repeat`, `padStart`, `padEnd` and `separator.join(array)`, their names C#'s in lower camel case, with JavaScript's and C#'s other names as aliases (`includes`, `toUpperCase`, `toLowerCase`, `replaceAll`, `padLeft`, `padRight`); positions counting Unicode scalar values, as indexing does; and case by the invariant culture, as .NET's `ToUpperInvariant` gives it, the same on every server. As built: §15.2, "String methods". **Trimming characters** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Enhance String.trimEnd"): `trim`, `trimStart` and `trimEnd` take a set of characters too, `"a//".trimEnd("/")` being `"a"` and `"x;,;".trimEnd(";,")` `"x"`, each a Unicode scalar value, any of them removed from the end or ends, as C#'s `Trim(chars)` and Python's `strip(chars)`, an empty argument trimming nothing; and a fixed text is removed by `removePrefix(text)` and `removeSuffix(text)`, once, only where the string begins or ends with it, else the string unchanged, `"report.txt".removeSuffix(".txt")` being `"report"`, as Python 3.9 and Kotlin have them, separate names keeping Python's trap out of the language, `"report.txt".rstrip(".txt")` being `"repor"`, which the Standard Library Reference warns of where it describes `trim`.
- **Functions.** `function name(a, b) { … }` declares a function, in scope throughout its block, so that functions can call each other in any order (unlike `var` and `const`, §3.1). `return value;` ends it, and `return;` gives `null`. Parameters are like `var`s. A function is a value (`var f = name;`), and `function (a) { … }` is a function expression; so is `(a) => { … }`, the same as `function (a) { … }`, its parameters written as a function's, a receiver included, `(this) => { … }` (the owner's request, recorded 14 November 2026). An arrow function's body may be an expression, which it gives, `(a, b) => a + b` being `(a, b) => { return a + b; }`; one parameter may be written without parentheses, `x => x * 2`; and `async (x) => { … }`, or `async x => …`, is the same as `async function (x) { … }` (approved 14 November 2026). Closures capture variables (binding cells), not copies. A hoisted function can therefore be called before a variable it uses has been declared; reading that variable then is a runtime error (WL2004, read before initialization), and only such a read can raise it (approved 13 November 2026). A parameter may have a default value, `function f(a, b = 1) { … }`, which makes it optional, as a template's parameters may (approved 13 November 2026, §13). A function's last parameter may be a rest parameter, as JavaScript writes it, `function f(first, ...rest) { … }`: an array of the arguments after the others, `[]` when there are none, so that the function takes any number of them, a call giving at least those before it but the ones with defaults (WL2006 otherwise; the owner's answer, 16 November 2026; as built, §15.2, "Rest parameters").
- **Spreading** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `...values` spreads what `foreach` visits in `values`, each item an argument of a call, `f(...args)`, or an element of an array's or a set's literal, `[...a, ...b]`, and a map's entries into a map's literal, `{ ...defaults, ...given }`. As built: §15.2, "Spreading".

### 3.6 Collections — approved 13 November 2026

The syntax and meaning of arrays, maps and sets, which M0.9 implements (§9), approved by the owner on 13 November 2026.

- **Arrays.** `[a, b, c]` makes a new array of those values, which may be of any kinds, mixed; `[]` makes an empty one; there is no trailing comma, as in calls.
- **Indexing.** `x[i]` reads element `i` of an array and `x[i] = v` replaces it. The index must be an int64. A binary64, even `1.0`, is WL3001. Nonnegative indexes start at 0. Negative indexes count from the end (`x[-1]` is the last element, the owner's request, 19 November 2026). A negative index is resolved by adding the array length. If the position still falls outside the array, ordinary indexing raises WL3005 and optional indexing gives `null`. The rule applies to array subscripts, including compound assignment, and leaves method arguments and map keys to their own rules.
- **Members.** `x.name` reads a member of a value, and `x.name(…)` calls one, a method. Collections and strings have the members below; a member a value does not have is a runtime error, WL3006 (no such member), as a missing property is (§3.2).
- **Length.** `x.length` is an array's number of elements, a map's or a set's number of entries, and a string's number of Unicode scalar values (§3.3). It is an int64, and cannot be assigned.
- **Strings by index.** `s[i]` is the string's `i`-th Unicode scalar value, as a string of one (`"abc"[1]` is `"b"`), not its number. Strings are immutable, so `s[i] = v` is WL3001.
- **JSON** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): a built-in class, `Json`, `Json.parse(text)` and `Json.stringify(value)`, rather than a standard module; its objects maps, its arrays arrays, a number an integer when it has no fraction or exponent and fits int64, else a number; and an instance written as its fields, as `write` writes them, a class's `toJson()` replacing them. As built: §15.2, "JSON". By the owner's decision of 18 November 2026, imported, `import { Json } from "std:json";`, in place of every program's having it (§3.9, "The standard library").
- **Regular expressions** (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): .NET's syntax, its engine used now, Native C# then needing one that reads the same; written by a class only, `new Regex("a+b", "i")`, with `r.test(s)`, `r.match(s)` and `s.replace(r, …)`, no literal `/a+b/i`; each match's time limited, against a pattern that backtracks without end. As built: §15.2, "Regular expressions". By the owner's decision of 18 November 2026, its class is `std:regex`'s, imported, `import { Regex } from "std:regex";` (§3.9, "The standard library").
- **Array methods.** `a.push(v)` adds `v` at the end, giving `null`; `a.pop()` removes the last element and gives it, and on an empty array is WL3005. Besides (the owner's answers, 16 November 2026): `insert(i, v)`, `removeAt(i)`, `indexOf(v)`, `contains(v)`, `slice(start, end)`, `concat(other)`, `join(separator)`, `reverse()`, `sort(compare)`, `map(f)`, `filter(f)`, `reduce(f, initial)`, `find(f)`, `any(f)` and `all(f)`, their names as listed, with JavaScript's and C#'s other names as aliases wherever they can be, so that both feel at home; `sort()` and `reverse()` give a new array and leave the array unchanged, the compiler warning where a result is discarded; and `sort()` without a function orders integers and numbers by value and strings by `<` (§3.5), anything else, or a mix, being WL3001. As built: §15.2, "Array methods".
- **Array callback arguments.** By the owner's decision on 19 November 2026, methods that take callbacks check that the argument is callable before they examine the array, including an empty one. An invalid callback raises WL3001 and names the method, including aliases such as `select` and `where`. The optional `sort` comparator accepts `null` for the default ordering. The compiler-generated methods use a runtime intrinsic so both VMs apply the same callability and diagnostic rules.
- **Maps.** `{ key: value, … }` makes a map, where a key written as a name or as a string literal is a string (`{ active: true }` is `{ "active": true }`), and `{}` is an empty map; other keys are added by indexing. At the start of a statement, `{` still opens a block.
- **A map's entries.** `m[k]` reads the value of key `k`, and a missing key is a runtime error, WL3007 (no such key); `m[k] = v` adds or replaces it; `m.has(k)` tells whether it is there; `m.remove(k)` removes it, giving whether it was there. Keys are read only by indexing: `m.active` is a member of the map, not its key `"active"`.
- **Keys.** Strings, int64s, Booleans and `null` may be keys, and a set's values, compared as `==` compares them (so an int64 is never equal to a string key). A binary64 key is WL3001, so that `1` and `1.0`, and NaN, raise no question; so are arrays, maps, sets and functions, until a use for keying by identity comes.
- **Order.** The order of a map's entries, and of a set's values, is not defined: it is a hash table's.
- **`foreach`.** `foreach (var item in a)` visits an array's elements in order, a set's values, and a string's Unicode scalar values, each as a string of one; `foreach (var (key, value) in m)` visits a map's entries, and `(key, value)` is allowed only there for now (§3.5). `foreach (var item in m)` over a map is WL3001, and so is anything else to loop over. A collection changed while `foreach` visits it is not an error: the loop sees the change, as a `for` loop with an index would. A class's instances are visited by a protocol of the language's own, which classes written in WebLingo, native classes and COM's collections all implement: the class's `iterate()` gives an iterator, whose `next()` gives each item in turn (approved 15 November 2026). The iterator also has `hasNext()`, which `foreach` asks before each `next()`, so that `null` may be an item; a class's instances are visited one item at a time, `foreach (var item in x)`, `(key, value)` staying a map's alone, for now; and arrays, maps, sets and strings are visited the VMs' own way, as before, each also having `iterate()`, which a script may call itself (all three approved 15 November 2026). How the protocol works where this leaves it open is Proposed (§15.2, "The `foreach` protocol").
- **Sets.** `s.add(v)` adds `v`, giving whether it was new; `s.has(v)` tells whether `v` is in it; `s.remove(v)` removes it, giving whether it was there. `#{a, b, c}` makes a new set of those values, and `#{}` an empty one; its values are what a map's keys may be (see "Keys"), and an equal value given twice is there once.
- **Optional access.** `x?[k]` reads as `x[k]` does, but gives `null` where `x[k]` would raise a missing key (WL3007) or an index out of range (WL3005), whether `x` is a map, an array or a string, and gives `null` too when `x` is `null`, so that `rows?[0]?["name"]` stops at the first `null`. A key or an index of the wrong kind (`m?[1.5]`, `a?["x"]`), and a value that cannot be indexed (`5?[0]`), stay WL3001: `?[` allows for an absent entry, not for a wrong program. It is written as C# writes it; the conditional operator, `c ? a : b`, is told apart from it by a space: `?[` written together is always an optional index, and `c ? [1] : [2]` a conditional. `x?.name`, its form for members, comes later.
- **Equality.** Arrays, maps and sets are equal only to themselves, as functions are (§3.2), so `[1] == [1]` is false.
- **Text.** `write` writes an array as `[1, "a", null]` and a map as `{"active": true}`: their strings quoted, with their escapes, their collections written the same way, and one it is already writing as `[…]` or `{…}`. A set is written as its literal, `#{1, "a"}`.

### 3.7 Classes and disposal — approved 13 November 2026

The syntax and meaning of classes, the disposal protocol and `using` (§3.2, §3.4, WL-L011), which M0.10 implements (§9), approved by the owner on 13 November 2026, but for the points that say they are still open.

- **Declaring a class.** `class Name { constructor(a) { … } method(b) { … } }`: a method is written as a name, parameters and a body, with or without `function` before the name (`function method(b) { … }`). A class is in scope throughout its block, as a function is, and its name is a constant.
- **Instances.** `new Name(a, b)` makes one and runs its `constructor` with those arguments; a class without a `constructor` passes its arguments on to the nearest base that declares one, so that `class B extends A {}` makes `new B(1)` run `A`'s with `1`, a special case that saves a constructor that would only call `super(1)`, and with no such base takes none (approved 14 November 2026). Calling a class without `new`, or `new` on anything but a class, is WL2005.
- **Fields.** A field exists once a method assigns it (`this.file = …`), as §3.4's example does; reading one that was never assigned is WL3006.
- **`this`.** A keyword, the instance a method or the constructor runs on; a function expression inside a method sees the method's `this`, as a closure sees a variable. `this` anywhere else is a compile-time error, WL2009.
- **Methods as values.** `obj.method` without a call gives a function bound to `obj`: `var f = obj.method; f();` calls it on `obj`.
- **Inheritance.** `class B extends A { … }`; a method of B with the name of one of A's replaces it; `super(…)` in B's constructor runs A's, and `super.name(…)` calls A's method. A class extends at most one other (§3.2). A constructor that does not call `super(…)` runs the base's constructor first, with no arguments, as C# does.
- **Visibility.** Every field and method is public.
- **Static members.** `static name(…) { … }` declares a method of the class itself, called as `Name.name(…)`, and `static var count = 0;` a field of the class, `Name.count`, set when the class is declared. In a static method, `this` is the class it is called on, as JavaScript has it, without a receiver, so that a script writes factories of its own: `static make() { return new this(); }`, called on a class extending it, `Green.make()`, makes a `Green` (the owner's decision, 20 November 2026, "Static Methods Given Their Class", in place of `this` there being WL2009, and of a receiver, `static make(this)`, which stays WL1001); a static method taken without a call, `var make = Green.make;`, keeps that class. `B.name` finds A's static member when B has none of its own, as C# does. A static member is reached through an instance too, `p.count` and `p.make()`, after the instance's fields and its class's methods, which shadow it (the owner's request, recorded 14 November 2026); and `p.count = 5` assigns the static field, when the class or a base has one, as Java does, else a field of the instance (approved 14 November 2026). Assigning a class a member it does not have, `Point.origin = new Point(0, 0);` or `Point.parse = function (s) { … };`, declares a static member, as `static` would.
- **Properties.** `property area { get { return this.w * this.h; } set(value) { … } }` declares a property, as C# groups one: `p.area` runs its getter, and `p.area = 5` its setter, with `5`; a property with only a getter cannot be assigned (WL3001), and one with only a setter cannot be read (WL3006). A property has a field of its own, `field` inside its getter and setter, as in C# 14, which nothing else can reach. Static properties (`static property count { get { … } }`), properties in `extend` blocks, a subclass's replacing its base's, and `super.area` reading the base's, are as for methods. `write` writes an instance's fields, and not its properties, which are computed, but for a property's own field, which it writes under the property's name (approved 14 November 2026).
- **`x?.name`** gives `null` when `x` is `null`, and when it has no member `name`, as `x?[k]` does for a missing key (§3.6).
- **Equality.** An instance is equal only to itself (§3.2), so two instances with equal fields are not equal: `==` stays identity for instances, and `a.equal(b)` runs only where a program calls it. Every value has `equal`, `Object`'s giving `==` (`"a".equal("a")` is true), which a class may override (the owner's decision of 13 November 2026, once every value had a class, in place of instances only).
- **`Object`.** A class without `extends` extends `Object`, a built-in class with no fields and two methods, which a class may override: `equal(other)`, which gives `this == other`, and `toString()` (see below). `new Object()` makes a plain instance.
- **`toString()`.** Every value has `toString()`, which gives the text `write` writes (`Point {x: 1, y: 2}`, and `n.toString()` is `"5"` when `n` is 5), so that `"n = " + n.toString()` joins a number to a string. A class may override it: `write`, `writeLn`, an interpolation and a template's `<%= %>` then write what it returns, wherever the instance is, inside an array or a map too; a `toString()` that returns anything but a string is WL3001 where it is written.
- **Text.** `write` writes an instance as its class's name and its fields, `Point {x: 1, y: 2}`, in no defined order, one it is already writing as `Point {…}`.
- **`destructor()`.** A class may declare `dispose()` or `destructor()`, which are the same method (§3.4); both in one class is a compile-time error, WL2011. Callers always call `dispose()`.
- **Disposing once.** The runtime runs an object's disposal at most once; a second `dispose()` does nothing and gives `null` (§3.4).
- **Derived classes.** Disposing an instance of B runs B's disposal, then A's, each once, without `super.dispose()` (§3.4).
- **Disposal that throws.** When a dispose method throws while an instance is disposed, the disposal goes on: its next methods still run, each once, and the exception is thrown again after the last; an exception a later one throws replaces it, as for `using`.
- **`using`.** `using (var x = expr) statement` declares `x`, in scope in the statement only, and calls `x.dispose()` when the statement ends, by its end, `break`, `continue` or `return`; a `null` value is not disposed, and a value without `dispose` is WL3006 at the end. One resource per `using`; nest them for more.
- **Throwing.** `throw value;` throws any value. `throw;` alone, in a `catch`, throws the caught exception again, keeping where it was first thrown.
- **Catching.** `try { … } catch (e) { … }`, `try { … } finally { … }`, and `try { … } catch (e) { … } finally { … }`, with one `catch`, which catches every exception; `e` is a `var` in scope in its block. A `catch` may name what it catches (see "Typed catches").
- **`finally`** runs on every way out of its `try`: its end, `break`, `continue`, `return`, and an exception. A `break`, `continue` or `return` that would leave a `finally` block is a compile-time error, WL2012, so that a `finally` cannot drop an exception or a result.
- **`Error`.** A built-in class, `new Error(message)`, with the fields `message` and `code` (a diagnostic code, or `null` for one a program throws), which programs may extend.
- **Runtime errors as exceptions.** The runtime errors (WL2004 to WL2006 at run time, WL3001, WL3002, WL3005 to WL3007) become instances of `Error`, with their code and message, which a `catch` may catch. The execution's limits (WL3003) and a cancellation (WL3004) cannot be caught, and still stop the program. Each kind of runtime error has a class of its own, extending `Error` (`TypeError` for WL3001, `IndexError` for WL3005, `KeyError` for WL3007, and so on), so that a catch can name one kind; the others are `InitializationError` (WL2004), `CallError` (WL2005), `ArgumentError` (WL2006), `OverflowError` (WL3002) and `MemberError` (WL3006).
- **Argument errors** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Argument Errors That Name the Argument"): a built-in member or a standard module's function given an argument of the wrong kind, or a value it does not take, is WL3001, its message naming the member, as the program called it, `includes` where it called `contains` so, and, where the member has several parameters, the argument by its place and its name, as the references name it, then saying what the argument must be and what it was given, as messages describe a value: `"tea".trim(1)` is `The argument of 'trim' must be a string of characters to trim, not the integer 1.`, and `"7".padStart("3")` `The first argument of 'padStart', the width, must be an integer, not a string.` (Q1's (b) and Q2's (b), read together: Q2's place and name in Q1's form). An element of an argument, a map's entry or an array's, is named through its argument, `The second argument of 'query', the values, must give @x null, a Boolean, …, not an array.`; an array's own elements, which no argument holds, keep the member's words, `'join' writes strings, … not an instance of P`. Built with `WebLingoArgument` (`WebLingo.Runtime`), which every such message is made by; a property's assignment, and a host's own classes, `Request`, `Response` and `Headers`, keep their messages for now.
- **Typed catches.** `catch (NotFound e) { … }` catches only an instance of `NotFound`, or of a class extending it. A `try` may have several catches, `catch (A e) { … } catch (B e) { … } catch (e) { … }`: an exception goes to the first that catches it; a catch without a class catches any exception, and only the last may be one; and an exception that none catches goes on outward, after the `finally`. A catch names its class by a name or a dotted name (`errors.NotFound`), with no calls or other expressions, so that nothing there can fail or have side effects. A catch may name a kind of value by its class (`catch (String e)`). A catch whose name is not a class when an exception reaches it catches an exception equal to it (`var A = 5;` … `catch (A e)` catches `throw 5;`); that equality is `A.equal(e)`, the program's own `equal` if `A`'s class overrides it, an exception it throws replacing the one being caught.
- **`is`.** `x is Point` is `true` when `x` is an instance of `Point`, or of a class extending it: the test a typed catch makes, a name that is not a class included.
- **Classes of values.** Every kind of value but `null` has a class, extending `Object`: `Boolean`, `Integer`, `Number` (binary64), `String`, `Array`, `Map`, `Set`, `Function` and `Class`, so that `5 is Integer` and `catch (String e)`. `null` has no class: `null is Object` is false, and only a catch without a class catches it. A class may extend `Array`, `Map` or `Set`: `class Stack extends Array { top() { return this[this.length - 1]; } }`; `new Stack()` is an empty array of class `Stack`, indexed, with `length`, `push` and `pop`, visited by `foreach`, `is Array` and `is Stack`, with its own methods, and fields as any instance has; it is written `Stack:[1, 2]`, as an instance of a class extending another kind of value is (below). `new Array()`, `new Map()` and `new Set()` make empty ones, as `[]`, `{}` and `#{}` do. `Boolean`, `Integer`, `Number`, `String`, `Function` and `Class` are like any other class. A class's values may get new methods by an `extend` block, `extend Integer { isEven() { … } }`, after which `4.isEven()`; and a class may extend any of them: an instance of a class extending `Number`, `new Celsius(21.5)`, holds a number, which arithmetic, comparisons and `==` use, `is Number` and `is Celsius`, with its own methods and fields, and likewise for `Integer`, `String` and `Boolean`; a subclass of `Function` is a function with methods of its own. `new Integer()` is `0`, `new String()` `""`, `new Boolean()` `false`, and `new Integer(42)` the same as `42`. `Integer.parse("10")`, `Number.parse("1.5")` and `Boolean.parse("true")` read a string as a literal of their kind, and `new Integer("10")`, `new Number("1.5")` and `new Boolean("true")` read one the same way (approved 14 November 2026). Such an instance is written as its class's name, a colon, and the value it holds, `Celsius:21.5`, without its fields; calling a class stays WL2005, so that text is for reading, not a program's. An `extend` block extends the class itself, everywhere: from when it runs, every instance of the class and of its subclasses, made before or after, has its methods; it is hoisted with its block's classes, and runs when the block begins, after they are made; a method it gives that the class has already replaces the class's own, as a subclass's would; and it may hold methods and static members, `static` as in a class. Arithmetic on such an instance keeps its class: a result has its left operand's class, if it has one, else its right's, so that `new Celsius(21.5) + 1` and `1 + new Celsius(21.5)` are both a `Celsius`, and `celsius + fahrenheit` a `Celsius`; it is a new instance of that class, holding the result, its constructor not run, with the fields of the operand whose class it is, copied. A class may declare what an operator does with its values, as C# does, by a static method, `static operator +(a, b) { … }`, which `+` calls when either operand's class declares it, the left's first, and likewise for `-`, `*`, `/` and the comparisons, the default applying where none does. A class extending `Number` gives the number it holds by its constructor's `super(value)`, as `new Number(value)` makes one, and a class without a constructor passes its arguments on, so `new Celsius(21.5)` holds `21.5`. `new Function(f)` is `f` itself; `class Handler extends Function { constructor(f) { super(f); } }` makes a function that calls `f`, with `Handler`'s methods and fields; and `new Function()` is a function that does nothing and gives `null`. `new Class()` makes a class, which a program may then name and give methods and fields while it runs, after which it acts as a class declared in the source does, so that a program can build classes, their methods and their fields entirely (approved 13 November 2026). `new Class("Point")` makes one extending `Object`, and `new Class("Point", Shape)` one extending `Shape`; `new Class()` makes an unnamed one, written `class`, its instances `class {x: 1}`, which `c.__className = "Point"` names. `Point.__className` gives a class's name, and assigning it renames any class, a built-in one included (`Integer.__className = "Double"`, at the program's own risk). A function with a receiver, `function (this, prefix) { … }`, whose first parameter is named `this`, is bound to a call's target (`p.describe("x = ")`), or given its receiver as its first argument when it is called directly (`describe(p, "x = ")`). `Point.setMethod("describe", f)` (named `addMethod` until the owner had it renamed, 14 November 2026, as A2 of "Building Classes at Run Time, Continued" names the others) gives a class a method, which its instances, and its subclasses', made before or after, then have, replacing one of that name as an extension's does; `Point.setMethod("constructor", f)` gives it its constructor, which `new Point(…)` runs. `Point.removeMethod("describe")` removes a class's own method, declared or given while the program runs, a base's of that name then reaching its instances (approved 14 November 2026). A static member is given by assignment, `Point.origin = f;`, as any class's is (see "Static members"), which replaces a static method of that name, a declared class's included (approved 14 November 2026). `Point.setProperty("sum", getter, setter)` gives a class a property, each a function with a receiver, or `null` for none, and `Point.setStaticProperty("unit", getter, setter)` a static one, each without a receiver, acting as declared properties do, but without a `field` of their own; `Point.setConstant("dimensions", 2)` gives it a constant, a static member that cannot be assigned (WL3001), as `static const dimensions = 2;` declares one in a class, and `const PI = 3.14;` in a class too, read through the class or an instance, `MathStuff.PI` and `m.PI` (the owner's request, recorded 14 November 2026). Each of them adds its member the first time, and replaces it after (approved 14 November 2026). A class's declaration, and an `extend` block, make a class and give it its members by the same means, one implementation serving both, so that a declared class and one a program builds while it runs are made alike (the owner's request, recorded 14 November 2026). A class may extend `Class`: an instance of such a class is a class, which its constructor's `super(name)` makes, and whose members are, after its own static members, its class's methods, as a metaclass's are (`class Entity extends Class { … }`, `var User = new Entity("User");`, `User is Entity`). `p.__instanceOf` gives a value's class, and `Point.__parent` a class's base, `Object` for `Point`, names that a program's own members are unlikely to take (approved 14 November 2026). The classes' names are in scope everywhere, as `Object`'s and `Error`'s, and a program may declare a name of its own over one. The text of a value agrees with its literal representation, where it has one.
- **No sealed classes** (the owner's answer, 18 November 2026, "BytesBuilder Extendability", in place of the classes of `Bytes`, `BytesBuilder`, `DateTime`, `Task`, `Module`, `Json` and `Html` being sealed): every standard class may be extended, the standard modules' too, and an instance of a class extending one has all its base's members, as a class written in WebLingo has its base's. Where the base is a kind of value's class, the instance holds a value of that kind, as one extending `Integer` holds an integer, and has that value's methods as well as its properties, `new Name("anna").toUpper()` giving `"ANNA"`; and `new Mine(…)` takes what its base's own making takes: `DateTime.utc(…)`'s arguments for a class extending `DateTime`, which has no `new` of its own, `new Mine(2026, 11, 18)`, and, read so, `Bytes.from(…)`'s for one extending `Bytes`, `new Mine("tea")`, and nothing for one extending `BytesBuilder`, as `new BytesBuilder()` takes nothing; `new` on such a class itself then makes a plain value, `new DateTime(2026, 11, 18)` being `DateTime.utc(2026, 11, 18)`, as `new Integer(42)` is `42`. Built in parts (§9, "Standard classes extended, parts"). **`Task` and `Module`** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, part 3's): a class extending either makes values of that kind, not holders of one. A `Job` is a task: `new Job(…)` a pending task, its constructor's `super()` taking nothing, completed by hand, `job.complete(value)` or `job.fail(error)`, as "Tasks Completed by Hand" has it, awaited, forgotten and combined as any task, its class's own members first. A `Plugin` is a module: `new Plugin(name)` a module defined in code rather than in a file, its constructor's `super(name)` taking what `Module`'s own making takes, a name, and `new Module(name)` a plain, empty one; its members its exports, its own fields and its class's methods, and its body its `exec()`, which it may override; `p is Module`. A module made so has no path, so that `import` cannot find it; registering one under a name an import can find, and a file whose module is of a class it names, are questions for later.
