A class that only wants to hold a task or a module holds it in a field. **The standard modules' classes** (the owner's answer, 18 November 2026, part 4's): a `LogFile` is a `File`, made in two steps, as .NET makes its connections, created, then opened: each standard module's class whose instances its factories make gets instance methods that do what its factories do, `f.open(path, mode)`, a task, and `f.openSync(path, mode)` for `File`, `db.open()` for `Database`, its name given to `new Database("fish")`, `s.connect(host, port)` for `Socket`, and so for the others, `new File()` a file not yet open, its other methods saying so until it is; its factories shorthand for both steps, `File.open(path, mode)` making `new this()` and awaiting its `open(path, mode)`, so that, called on a class extending it, they make that class's instance, where its constructor takes no arguments; a class extending one keeping an ordinary, synchronous constructor, which never blocks on I/O; and opening an instance already open, or disposed, an error, its message saying which.
- **Uncaught exceptions.** An exception nothing catches stops the program as a runtime error does: exit code 3, reported where it was thrown, with its own code for a runtime error, and WL3008 (uncaught exception) for a value a program threw.
- **`using` and exceptions.** `using` disposes on every way out, an exception's included, as a `finally` would. An exception from `dispose()` while another is on its way out replaces it, as in C#.
- **How the VMs do it.** Table-driven protected regions (§5.4): each function lists its `try` ranges and their handlers, and nothing runs on the way into a `try`.
- **Use after disposal.** For a class written in WebLingo, nothing is checked: the class checks its own state if it needs to.

### 3.8 Asynchrony — approved 13 November 2026

How a program writes asynchrony, on WebLingo's own operation model (WL-P007), approved by the owner on 13 November 2026, before templates, which use it (§13), but for the points that say they are still open.

- **`async` and `await`.** `async function f() { … }`, `async` methods and `async function (…) { … }`: a call of one gives a task at once. `await t` waits for the task `t`, giving its result, or throwing its exception.
- **Where `await` stands.** In an `async` function, and at the top level of a script or a template, which is its executable block. A script, or a module, is an instance of a built-in class `Module`: its top level is the body of an `async` method, `exec()`, which its host calls and awaits; a module's exports are its instance's members, as the module system (`import`) will define them.
- **Starting.** An `async` function starts at once, when it is called, and runs until its first `await`, as in C# and JavaScript.
- **What `await` takes.** Any value: a task gives its result once it completes, any other value itself; `await` always lets other tasks run first, even on a value that is not a task.
- **Tasks.** A task is a value of the built-in class `Task`. `Task.all(values)`, given an array of values, tasks or not, gives a task that completes with an array of their results, in their order, once all have, or faults at once with the first exception one of them throws, the others going on, observed, as JavaScript's `Promise.all` does; `Task.any(values)` completes, or faults, as the first of them to complete does (JavaScript's `Promise.race`); and `Task.timeout(task, milliseconds)` gives the task's result, or faults with a `TimeoutError`, a class extending `Error`, with a code of its own (WL3010), if the task takes longer, the task itself going on. They are static methods of `Task`, and `std:tasks`'s exports too, `import { all } from "std:tasks";` (approved 14 November 2026; `std:tasks` implemented the same day).
- **Tasks completed by hand** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Tasks Completed by Hand"): a task a program completes itself, for queues, events and test doubles, where the code that gives the result is not the code that started the wait, as C#'s `TaskCompletionSource` and JavaScript's `new Promise(executor)` make one. `new Task()` makes a pending task, and `t.complete(value)` and `t.fail(error)` complete it, whoever holds it, C#'s split between a source and its task given up for it, and no factory needed; a task `new` did not make, an `async` function's call's, `Task.all`'s, `Task.any`'s, `Task.timeout`'s or the host's, completes itself, and `complete` or `fail` on one is WL3001, its message saying so. Completing a task a second time, or failing one completed, does nothing, `complete` and `fail` giving whether they completed it, as C#'s `TrySetResult` does. One still pending when the execution ends is cancelled with its other running tasks, and nothing is reported, a pending task not being a failure. A program stuck waiting for one is given an error, not left to hang: where nothing is ready to run, no timer is due, no work is out on the host, and the execution waits for a task only a `complete` or a `fail` could finish, a runtime error is thrown at the `await` that waits for it, as Go stops a program whose goroutines all sleep, of a class of its own extending `Error`, `DeadlockError`, its code WL3020 (both Proposed), its message naming where the task was made and where it is awaited, `The program waits for a task nothing can complete: made at line 12, awaited at line 30.`, the lines the host's, which knows its files (`WebLingoExecutionContext.Lines`); it travels outward as any exception does, failing the tasks of the `async` functions it leaves, so that a program may catch it, and one that does not stops as for any uncaught exception; a program stuck again gets it again, where it is stuck then. A deadlock of tasks no `new` made, an `async` function's waiting for itself, stays WL3009, which ends the program. As built (18 November 2026, both VMs): the first such task the heap holds, and the first function waiting for it (`WebLingoHeap.TryFindStuckTask`, `WebLingoScheduler.Raise`); a task awaited only through `Task.all` or `Task.any` is not found so, its program ending with WL3009.
- **Exceptions nothing awaits.** When an execution ends, the first exception of a task that nothing awaited stops it, as an uncaught one does, so that none goes unnoticed; `t.forget()` opts a task out of this, for one started and forgotten: nothing reports its exception.
- **Where tasks come from.** Besides `async` functions, the host's operations give tasks (files, network, database: WL-P004, WL-P009, WL-P011), and the standard library has `delay(milliseconds)`, for tests.
- **The end of an execution.** It ends when its top level does; its tasks still running are cancelled.
- **How the VMs do it.** A waiting `async` function's frames are kept on the heap, held by its task, and resumed by the worker's scheduler when what it waits for completes; no thread blocks (WL-P007, WL-P008).
- **Cancellation.** An execution's cancellation (WL3004) ends its waiting tasks too, uncatchable, as now.
- **`using`.** A `dispose()` may be `async`, and `using` awaits it; in a function that is not `async`, where it cannot wait, an `async` `dispose()` is WL3001, at the end of the `using`. `x.dispose()` on an instance whose disposal has an `async` method gives a task, which completes, with `null`, once the last of its methods has run, so that `await x.dispose();` waits for it (approved 13 November 2026).
- **Templates.** A template's body may `await` anywhere, as an `async` function's may, and `renderComponent(…)` gives a task, which the page awaits (§13).
- **Concurrency.** An execution's tasks run on its one worker, one at a time, switching only at `await`, so that no two run at once and nothing needs a lock (WL-H012, WL-P013); many executions, a server's requests, run at once on a pool of workers. Parallel work within one program will come later, as workers with heaps of their own that exchange copied messages (WL-P013's explicit transfer).

### 3.9 Modules — approved 13 November 2026

The module system, `import`, `export` and `param`, approved by the owner on 13 November 2026, but for the points that say they are still open. Templates build on it (§13, "Contracts").

- **Modules.** A script, or a module, is a `.wlg` file, and an instance of the built-in class `Module`, whose top level is the body of its `async` method `exec()` (§3.8).
- **Importing.** `import db from "lib/db.wlg";` imports a module statically, at a module's top level: it is resolved, and compiled, with the importing program. `Module.import("lib/db.wlg")` imports one dynamically, anywhere, its path known only when it runs: the module is compiled then. Either gives the module's instance; `Module.import(…)` gives it as a task, which completes once the module has run, `var db = await Module.import("lib/db.wlg");`, a module's top level being `async`. A module also imports names, `import { f, g } from "lib/util.wlg";`, as JavaScript does, beside its instance.
- **Running once.** A module runs once per execution, the first time it is imported; every later import gets the same instance, already run. Under a host, an execution is a request's, and the instance the request's own. A module's static imports run before its top level, in their order, each awaited, so that it begins with them run, as JavaScript's do.
- **Exports.** Only the declarations a module marks `export` (`export function f() { … }`) are members of its instance: a `function`, `class`, `var` or `const` declaration at its top level. An importer reads an exported `var` as it is when read (`db.count`), and cannot assign it (WL3001): an export is its module's to change.
- **Paths.** A path is from the importing file's folder, `./` being that folder and `/` the drive's root, as for the language proper in §13 ("Includes"); `.wlg` may be left out.
- **Cycles.** A module may import itself, directly or through others: the second import gets the instance as it is so far, as JavaScript does.
- **Parameters.** `param name;` and `param size = 10;` declare a module's parameters, at its top level, before its other statements, each a `const` of it: `param` is a keyword of the language. An importer gives them, `import db from "lib/db.wlg" { size: 10 };`, and a template's caller gives a component's or a layout's (§13). A parameter without a default that is not given is an error, where the module is imported, of a code of its own. A module runs once for each set of values its imports give it, an import with other values getting an instance of its own, so that one module serves, in effect, as several; a static import's values are a map literal, whose names the compiler checks against the module's parameters, a missing or an extra one being an error at compile time (WL2016), and a dynamic import's any expression giving a map, checked when it runs (approved 14 November 2026).
- **A script's parameters.** `weblingo run script.wlg size=10 name=Anna` gives a script its parameters, each value a string, `"10"` and `"Anna"`, which the script converts itself where it needs another kind, since a shell removes the quotes that would tell a string from another literal (approved 14 November 2026, in place of reading a value as a WebLingo literal when it is one).
- **Native modules.** A module may be native code, installed to extend what scripts can use, as Python's C extensions are. On .NET, it is an assembly with one public class implementing `IWebLingoNativeModule`, whose `Register(IWebLingoNativeRegistry registry)` gives the module's exports: functions, each with a name, an arity, and a delegate that takes the call's arguments, as `WebLingoValue`s, with the execution's heap, and gives a value, or a task for asynchronous work, as the host's built-ins (`IWebLingoBuiltinFunction`) do; and classes, from the start, each declared by the module with its methods as native functions (`registry.Class("Connection", …)`), which is how the built-in classes are to be implemented too (approved 14 November 2026). A native class's instance may hold one host object, a .NET object such as a database's connection, which no script sees, and which only its class's native functions reach, given the instance's class, not only the object's .NET type, checked, so that a native method that reaches another class's instance fails cleanly; its `dispose()`, or the collector, releases it, a release being idempotent and leaving the instance disposed, so that its methods report it as such instead of reaching a freed object (approved 14 November 2026). A script imports one by its assembly's path, relative or absolute, or by a scheme the host is configured with, each naming a folder of native modules (`import db from "native:sqlite";` finds the module `sqlite` in the folder configured as `native`), several such folders, and schemes, being allowed; native modules and scripts are not, in general, kept in one folder. A scheme's folder holds each module's assembly, `native/sqlite.dll`, its dependencies beside it; and a native module loads only from a configured scheme's folder, one an explicit path names too, which scripts cannot write to (§7.13's capabilities), so that no script can write an assembly and import it (both approved 14 November 2026). On .NET, a native module is loaded into the host's own context and never unloaded, for now (adapter-only, §7.14; approved 14 November 2026). A host is configured with its schemes by a file in the program's folder, `weblingo.config` for a script and `wasp.config` for a page or a site (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026), `{ "schemes": { "native": "C:/WebLingo/native" } }`, and by its command line, `weblingo run --scheme native=C:/WebLingo/native script.wlg`, which comes before the file's; the `std` scheme's folder is `std/`, beside the runtime's program, which a configuration may point elsewhere; and the built-in classes the VMs implement (`Array`, `Map`, `Set`, `Task`, `Module`, `Class` and `Function`) become a standard module's native classes, their methods native functions, their effect on performance measured, while those written in WebLingo (`Object`, `Error` and the runtime errors' classes) stay so (all three approved 14 November 2026). That standard module is `std:core`, a module of their own, which every module imports implicitly, as it imports `std:system`, so that their names stay in scope everywhere; their members that are not methods become native too, a native class having properties and an indexer, each getter and setter a native function, so that `length` is a property to a script; and both ways of calling their native methods are built and measured on the benchmark, the VMs calling the native function directly, given the instance first, and the VMs calling a method written in WebLingo that calls it, the owner choosing between them, and between them and the VMs' own members, on the numbers, nothing of it landing before the owner has seen them (approved 15 November 2026). Measured (§5.6, "The built-in classes as native classes"), the built-in classes as `std:core`'s native classes made a program that uses their members most about twice as slow, a native member's call costing what the VMs' own check does not; so the built-in classes keep the VMs' own members, `std:core` dropped, and what landed is what serves every native module (the owner's decision, 15 November 2026, in place of making them native): a native class has properties (`DeclareProperty`) and an indexer (`DeclareIndexer`), each getter and setter a native function, `c.value` and `c[k]` to a script; the VMs call a native member's function directly, given the instance first, without the frame of the member written in WebLingo that calls it (`WebLingoFunction.NativeBuiltin`); a native function, as every built-in, takes its arguments as a read-only view of them (`ReadOnlySpan<WebLingoValue>`), never copied; and a class's members that the runtime names by number (`WebLingoMember`) are found by that number. `x?[k]` calls a native class's indexer as `x[k]` does, and gives `null` where its getter raises a missing key (WL3007) or an index out of range (WL3005), as §3.6 has it for a collection. An installed native module is trusted, as a built-in is, and any script may import it; what must not happen is a script fetching a native module and running it. A native function takes and gives WebLingo's values through the runtime's API, may make classes and instances, and may give a task for asynchronous work, on WebLingo's own operation model (WL-P007), never on .NET's `Task` in the core; under Native C#, the same module's source is compiled by that toolchain, its contract unchanged (WL-017). The standard modules are native modules too, installed with the runtime, which another implementation may replace: every native module is packaged and treated alike, none privileged; `std:system`'s built-ins are moved into a native module installed with the runtime, in the `std` scheme's folder, now, not with the first standard module that is not the host's built-ins (approved 14 November 2026). Scripts will reach C libraries directly too, by a foreign-function interface still to be designed (approved 14 November 2026, but for the points still being discussed).
- **The standard library.** Its parts are modules named by a scheme, `std:fs` and `std:system`, which the compiler and the runtime resolve, never as paths (`import fs from "std:fs";`). The built-ins belong to modules too: `write` and `writeLn` are `std:system`'s, which every module imports implicitly. **Imported** (the owner's decision, 18 November 2026, "JSON import", in place of that implicit import, and of `Json` and `Html` in every program): every standard module imported, nothing of one in a program that does not import it: `Json` by `import { Json } from "std:json";`, `Html` by `import { Html } from "std:html";`, and `write` and `writeLn` by an import, so that a script that imported neither could not write, `std:io`'s, a module of their own, until the owner moved them back to `std:system` the same day, which every program imports implicitly ("The rest", below); every program's, without an import, only what the language itself makes, the classes its literals and statements make or need, `Object`, `Array`, `Map`, `Set`, `Error` and the runtime errors' classes, `Task`, `Function`, `Class` and `Module`, and its constants, `Infinity` and `NaN`; so that a script's imports list everything it depends on, and everything a host must grant it, no standard name is taken in a script that does not import it, and a standard module a script does not use costs it nothing; a standard module's name used without its import WL2001, its message suggesting the import, `'Json' is not defined; did you mean Json, from std:json?`, and a name differing only by case the name, `did you mean Json?`, for every standard export; a template's text and its `<%= %>` writing without an import, the template compiler's own output, a block that calls `write` importing it; and nothing kept for backward compatibility, every script, sample and test gaining its imports. Read so, as the rule has it, the classes literals make, `Boolean`, `Integer`, `Number`, `String` and `Decimal`, and `Iterator`, which `foreach` needs, are every program's too, and the runtime errors' classes the standard modules' failures' among them. **The rest** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Standard Modules, Imported: The Rest"): a kind of value is every program's exactly when the language's own syntax makes it, as literals make integers and strings; the classes of the others are their modules', `Bytes` and `BytesBuilder` `std:bytes`'s, `DateTime` `std:datetime`'s and `Regex` `std:regex`'s, a program importing one to name it, `Bytes.from(text)`, `DateTime.now()`, `x is Regex`, and a value of its kind, which a module or a method gives too (`crypto.sha256(x)` gives bytes), keeping its members without the import, `b.toHex()`; and `delay` is `std:system`'s, `std:system` staying for now, with `write` and `writeLn` moved back to it, `std:io` gone, nothing kept for it; and `std:system` is imported implicitly by every program, as before M0.20.3, `write`, `writeLn` and `delay` in its scope without an import, an import of them allowed and meaning the same, the imports M0.20.3 and M0.20.4 gave every script, sample, test, example and document left where they are (the owner's answer, 18 November 2026, "The Rest", A3). `std:system` exports the host's built-ins, those its implementation gives, which another implementation may replace, `write`, `writeLn` and `delay` the standard names among them. Built in parts (§9, "M0.20 parts"), the last landing on 18 November 2026. **The console** (the owner's answers, 20 November 2026, "Console Module"): `write`, `writeLn` and the console's reading are `std:console`'s, which a program imports, `std:system` keeping `delay`, its one standard name ("The console: `std:console`", below).
- **Files: `std:fs`** (approved 15 November 2026). The standard module of files and folders, a native module installed with the runtime, which reaches the platform's file system (§7.4): whole files as text, `readText(path)`, `writeText(path, text)`, `appendText(path, text)`, `exists(path)` and `remove(path)`; folders, `list(path)`, `makeFolder(path)` and `removeFolder(path)`; and files opened as handles, a native class `File`, `File.open(path, mode)`, its `read`, `write` and `dispose()`. Each is asynchronous by default, `await fs.readText(path)`, a task, its synchronous form named with `Sync`, `fs.readTextSync(path)`, as Node.js names them (WL-P004). Text is UTF-8 unless an optional argument names another encoding. Under `weblingo`, a script reaches any file the process may, capabilities coming with hosting (§7.13); under `wasp`, a site's scripts reach only the folders its `wasp.config` allows, a relative path from the site's root, a path refused a `FilePermissionError` (the owner's answers, 17 November 2026, §7.13, "File capabilities"). An I/O failure, a file that is not there or cannot be read or written, is a runtime error of a code of its own, WL3011, of a class `IOError` extending `Error`, which a catch may catch, its message naming the path as the script gave it, never the host's own paths. **Bytes, folders and copies** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Enhance std:fs", so that an installation script copies files without COM): whole files as bytes, `readBytes(path)`, giving `Bytes`, `writeBytes(path, bytes)` and `appendBytes(path, bytes)`; a `File` opened for its bytes, `File.open(path, "rb")` or `"wb"`, its `read(count)` giving up to that many bytes, `null` at its end, and `write(bytes)`, for a file too large to hold whole, or `"ab"`, its bytes written after the file's own, a file not there made, as `"a"` writes text (the owner's request, 18 November 2026, "Append Mode"); `isFolder(path)`; and `copy(source, target)`, a file copied as it is, its bytes never reaching the script; each with its `Sync` form.
- **Console input** (the owner's answers, 19 November 2026, "Console Input"): `std:system`, beside `write` and `writeLn`, every program having them without an import (A5), reads a script's console, its standard input, typed or piped: `readLine()`, a task of the next line, without its line break, and `readLineSync()`, blocking its worker, as `std:fs`'s functions have both forms (A1); `null` at the input's end, so that `while ((line = await readLine()) != null)` reads it all (A3); `prompt(text)`, writing the text, then reading a line, and `readPassword()`, a line not shown as it is typed (A4); and, under `wasp`, where there is no user at a console, and Apache's `mod_fcgid` makes the standard input the FastCGI socket, each an error where it is called, its message saying that a site has no console (A2). As built (19 November 2026, Proposed where it says more): through the platform's `IWebLingoConsoleInput`, which `weblingo` gives an execution (`WebLingoExecutionContext.ConsoleInput`, `DotNetConsoleInput`) and `wasp` does not; the error an `IOError`, WL3011; `prompt` and `readPassword` tasks, without `Sync` forms; a piped input read as UTF-8 and a console's through .NET's Unicode input, `ReadConsoleW`, as output is written ("Text written on Windows"); a password read key by key, Backspace removing the last, Ctrl+Z or Ctrl+D ending the input where nothing is typed, and read as a line where the input is not a console's. On 20 November 2026 the owner moved all four to `std:console`, which a program imports (A1 of "Console Module", below), the error under `wasp` becoming that module's WL2015.
- **The console: `std:console`** (the owner's answers, 20 November 2026, "Console Module", in place of `write`, `writeLn` and the console's reading being `std:system`'s, every program's): no program is assumed to be a console program, one that writes to a console: a script is one only where it imports `std:console`, and is otherwise, on Windows, an application of Windows's own, in preparation for scripts that show windows of their own. `std:console`, a standard module, exports the console's writing, `write(value)` and `writeLn(value)`, and `writeError(value)` and `writeErrorLn(value)`, as they are, to the standard error, as .NET's `Console.Error` (A9), and `print(value)` and `printLn(value)`, `write` and `writeLn` by other names (the owner's request, 20 November 2026, "Write Aliases"); and its reading, `readLine`, `readLineSync`, `prompt` and `readPassword`, as "Console input" has them, moved from `std:system`, which keeps `delay` (A1). Its class `Console`, its members static, as `std:env`'s `Environment`, gives at least all of .NET's `Console`, its names in lower camel case, `Console.foregroundColor = "red";`, `Console.setCursorPosition(0, 5);`, `Console.clear();`, the writing and the reading among its members too, `Console.writeLn(x)`, as .NET's has them, beside the functions exported by name, `import { Console, writeLn } from "std:console";` (A4); what .NET has on Windows only an error elsewhere. A colour is a name, .NET's `ConsoleColor`'s in lower camel case, `"darkBlue"`, a wrong one WL3001, or a constant of a class of its own, `ConsoleColor.darkBlue`, as .NET's enumeration, both (A5). A key read, `await Console.readKey()`, a task, and `Console.readKeySync()`, is a map, `{ key: "enter", char: "\r", shift: false, alt: false, control: false }`, the key's name .NET's `ConsoleKey`'s in lower camel case (A6). Under `wasp`, where a site has no console, `std:console` is not installed, its import WL2015, as for a module that is not (A3); a page's text and its `<%= %>` write the response as before, its blocks with `write` and `writeLn` of their own, without an import, which write where its text goes, and a `.wlg` route handler with the response's `response.write(value)` and `response.writeLn(value)`, as classic ASP's `Response.Write` (A2). On Windows, a script that does not import `std:console`, started without a console, by a double-click, opens no window: `weblingo.exe`'s manifest says `detached` (Microsoft's "Console allocation policy", Windows 11 24H2 and later, earlier Windows opening a console as before), a console made only for a script that imports `std:console`; and a second program, `weblingow.exe`, a Windows application, as Python's `pythonw.exe`, which a double-click runs, on every Windows, both (A7); such a script, without a console, failing, a compile error or an uncaught exception, shows what `weblingo` writes for it in a message box (A8). Built in parts (§9, "Console module, parts"). **As built** (part 1, 20 November 2026; Proposed where it says more, §15.2, "The console"): `std:console` is a native module, `WebLingo.StdLib.Console`, installed as `std/console.dll`, its functions the standard library's (`WebLingo.StdLib`), writing to the execution's output and its errors (`WebLingoExecutionContext.Output` and `ErrorOutput`), `weblingo`'s standard output and standard error; `wasp` refuses it (`WebLingoNativeModuleLoader.Refused`); a page's own `write` and `writeLn` only a template's code sees, a page's, its includes', a layout's and a component's, never a module it imports; and `response.write` and `response.writeLn` write a value's text as `write` does, a statement that calls either waiting for a slow client as a page's write does (§6.12, "Streaming"). From part 3 (20 November 2026): `weblingo.exe`'s manifest says `detached`; `weblingow.exe`, `WebLingo.Windowed.Cli`, is `weblingo` as a Windows application; and either, started with no console, and no standard output or error a file or a pipe takes, makes a console window of its own when it first loads `std:console` to run a script, keeps what it writes until then, and shows a failure's text in a message box where it never had one (`WebLingoConsoleSession`). From part 2 (20 November 2026, "The console's control"): `Console`'s members are .NET's `Console`'s in lower camel case, static: `title`, `foregroundColor`, `backgroundColor`, `cursorLeft`, `cursorTop`, `cursorSize`, `cursorVisible`, `windowWidth`, `windowHeight`, `windowLeft`, `windowTop`, `bufferWidth`, `bufferHeight`, `largestWindowWidth`, `largestWindowHeight`, `keyAvailable`, `capsLock`, `numberLock`, `treatControlCAsInput`, `isInputRedirected`, `isOutputRedirected`, `isErrorRedirected`, `inputEncoding` and `outputEncoding`; `beep()`, `clear()`, `resetColor()`, `getCursorPosition()`, `setCursorPosition(left, top)`, `setWindowSize`, `setWindowPosition`, `setBufferSize` and `moveBufferArea`; `readKey(intercept)`, `readKeySync(intercept)`, `read()` and `readSync()`; the writing and the reading, `Console.writeLn(x)`; and `onCancelKeyPress(handler)`. Through the platform's console, `IWebLingoConsole` (`DotNetConsole`), which `weblingo` gives a script.
- **Mathematics: `std:math`** (the owner's answers, 18 November 2026, "Mathematics"). A standard module, imported, `import math from "std:math";`, rather than methods of the values, as the standard modules' rule has every standard name imported: `math.abs(x)`, `math.sign(x)`, `math.min(a, …)` and `math.max(a, …)`, each keeping its argument's kind, an integer's an integer, a decimal's a decimal; `math.floor(x)`, `math.ceiling(x)`, `math.round(x)` and `math.truncate(x)`, each giving an integer, an integer itself, an error where the number is beyond int64, NaN or infinite, `math.round` halfway away from zero, `2.5` to `3` and `-2.5` to `-3`, as most people round by hand; `math.sqrt(x)`, `math.pow(x, y)`, `math.exp(x)`, `math.log(x)`, `math.log10(x)`, `math.log2(x)`, `math.sin(x)`, `math.cos(x)`, `math.tan(x)`, `math.asin(x)`, `math.acos(x)`, `math.atan(x)` and `math.atan2(y, x)`, each taking an integer or a number and giving a number, as IEEE 754 has them, `math.sqrt(-1.0)` being `NaN`; and the constants `math.PI` and `math.E`. As built (18 November 2026, Proposed where it says more): a number or a decimal beyond int64, infinity among them, an `OverflowError`, WL3002, and NaN WL3001, an argument's error; `min` and `max` taking one value or more, its rest parameter's, and giving the value as it was given, compared as `<` compares, a number and a decimal not compared, and NaN where one is NaN; a held value given as its kind's, as to every native function; a decimal WL3001 to the functions giving a number, as nothing converts by itself. For it, a native module's function may have a rest parameter, its function given the array as its last argument, and a native module may export constants, `null`, Booleans, integers and numbers, read as its members, which no importer may assign (`IWebLingoNativeRegistry.RestFunction` and `Constant`, Proposed).

**Standard-library additions approved 19 November 2026.** Path manipulation, URL handling, and CSV are the first
additions. The owner approved `std:path` with `join`, `dirname`, `basename`, and `extension`, accepting both slash
styles and Windows roots on both platforms and emitting the host separator. `std:url` parses named components,
resolves references, and reads and writes queries as ordered pairs. `std:csv` parses and writes arrays of string
rows or maps keyed by headers. The .NET path and URL modules are adapter-side native modules. CSV is portable.

**Testing framework approved 20 November 2026.** `weblingo test` discovers directly declared, zero-parameter
instance methods with names beginning with `test` and an uppercase letter in top-level exported classes whose
names end in `Tests`. It runs files in command order and cases in declaration order, with a fresh instance for
each case. The runner awaits cases and optional `beforeEach` and `afterEach` hooks, keeps cleanup errors alongside
case errors, and supports listing and filtering by case label. The explicit `std:test` import provides Boolean,
equality, structural, membership, regular expression, approximate number and exception assertions, plus `checkAll`
for reporting multiple failures. The [standard library reference](weblingo-standard-library-reference.md#30-stdtest)
specifies the user-facing contract.

The CLI compiles an in-memory driver with the test file as an import, leaving execution of methods and tasks
to the selected VM. The compiler supplies `std:test` as a WebLingo module with a compiled initializer. It uses
bundled native comparison and reporting routines. Both VMs attach assertion locations to caught `WL3021` errors.

### 3.10 COM objects on Windows — built 17 November 2026

The owner asked on 15 November 2026 for a native extension for Windows through which scripts use COM objects, as classic ASP's pages did (`Server.CreateObject("ADODB.Connection")`), designed, not built. The owner approved its name, how its objects' members are written, the threads it runs on, the permission it needs and how its C# calls COM on 15 November 2026, each marked so below; the rest of this section was Proposed until the owner approved it, as written, on 17 November 2026 (A1 of "COM Objects on Windows: `windows:com`"), with three changes for the values WebLingo has had since it was written (A2 to A4, below, "Values"). Built on 17 November 2026 (M0.17; as built, §15.2, "COM objects").

- **A native module, on Windows only.** COM is Windows's, and the portable core may depend on no platform (§11), so COM comes as a native module (§3.9), an adapter (§7.14), which only a Windows host installs: `windows:com`, under a scheme of its own for Windows's native modules, `windows`, which leaves `std:` the same on every platform (approved 15 November 2026), its folder `windows/` beside the runtime's program, as `std/` is, which a configuration may point elsewhere (Proposed); elsewhere, importing it is WL2015, as for a module that is not installed. COM is no reason to call anything "Active" (§1): it is one native module among others.
- **Making objects.** `com.create(progId)` makes an object by its ProgID or its CLSID (`"ADODB.Connection"`, `"{00000514-0000-0010-8000-00AA006D2EA4}"`), as `Server.CreateObject` did, and `com.get(moniker)` binds to one that exists, as `GetObject` did. Each gives an instance of the module's native class `ComObject`, whose host object (§3.9) is the COM object's interface, released once: by `dispose()` and `using`, by the collector, or at the execution's end.
- **Members.** A COM object's members are known only while the program runs, through its `IDispatch` (`GetIDsOfNames`, then `Invoke`), their names compared as COM compares them, ignoring case, each written as any member is (approved 15 November 2026), a native class getting a fallback for the members it does not declare, a getter, a setter and a call, each a native function, which `ComObject` declares: `conn.Open(text)` calls a method, `rs.EOF` reads a property and `rs.Filter = "Price > 10"` assigns one, each giving a value or another `ComObject`; a property that takes arguments is read as a call, `rs.Fields.Item("Name")`; and `x[k]` reads, and `x[k] = v` assigns, the object's default member (`DISPID_VALUE`) with `k`, by the indexers native classes get (§3.9), so that `rs["Name"]` is the field, as `rs("Name")` was. Nothing converts implicitly: where VBScript's assignment read a field's default value, a script reads `rs["Name"].Value`. A COM member named as a member every value has (`toString`, `equal`, `dispose`) is hidden by it, and reached by `ComObject`'s own `call(name, arguments)`, `get(name)` and `set(name, value)` (Proposed).
- **Values.** Going out, as `VARIANT`s: `null` as `VT_NULL`; a Boolean as `VT_BOOL`; an integer as `VT_I4` when it fits, else `VT_I8`; a number as `VT_R8`; a string as `VT_BSTR`; an array as a one-dimensional `SAFEARRAY` of `VARIANT`s; a `ComObject` as `VT_DISPATCH`; anything else is WL3001. Coming back: `VT_EMPTY` and `VT_NULL` as `null`; `VT_BOOL` as a Boolean; every integer type as an integer, an unsigned 64-bit one beyond int64 being WL3002; `VT_R4` and `VT_R8` as numbers; `VT_BSTR` as a string; `VT_CY` and `VT_DECIMAL` as decimals (§3.2, "Decimals"; the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, in place of numbers until WebLingo had decimal128 values), so that ADO's `MONEY` and `DECIMAL` fields keep their digits, and a decimal going out as `VT_DECIMAL`; `VT_ARRAY | VT_UI1`, as ADO gives a binary field, as `Bytes`, and `Bytes` going out as one (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026); a date going out as `VT_DATE`, its time on the server's clocks, as `VT_DATE` comes back (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026); `VT_DATE`, a time on the server's clocks, as a `DateTime` in the server's zone (§3.2, "Dates and times"); `VT_DISPATCH` and `VT_UNKNOWN` as `ComObject`s; a `SAFEARRAY` as an array, one of several dimensions as arrays of arrays.
- **Errors.** An `Invoke` that fails, with its `HRESULT` and its `EXCEPINFO`, is a runtime error of a code of its own, WL3012, of a class `ComError` extending `Error`, with the fields `number` (the `HRESULT`, an integer), `source` and `description`, as ASP's `Err` had them, which a catch may catch.
- **Collections.** `foreach` over a COM collection goes through the language's protocol (§3.6): `ComObject`'s `iterate()` gives an iterator over its `_NewEnum` (`IEnumVARIANT`), which reads one item ahead to answer `hasNext()`; `coll.Count` and `coll.Item(i)` are members as any other.
- **Threads.** A COM object lives in an apartment, and ASP ran each page on a single-threaded apartment's (STA's) thread. An execution runs on one worker (WL-H012), and its heap is the worker's (WL-P013), so that a COM object never leaves the worker that made it, as COM's rules ask; each worker that runs scripts is a single-threaded apartment's thread, with a message pump, as ASP's were, so that every object's calls are direct (approved 15 November 2026): a Windows host starts its workers so, and a worker that waits pumps messages, which the portable core does not know of; and a call blocks its worker until it returns, as it blocked ASP's thread, no asynchronous form being offered.
- **Which objects.** COM objects such as `WScript.Shell` and `Scripting.FileSystemObject` reach the whole machine, beyond any capability (§7.13): so COM is not allowed by default, and needs a permission in the application's configuration (approved 15 November 2026), which lists the ProgIDs and CLSIDs a script may make, the configuration's (`weblingo.config`'s, a site's `wasp.config`'s) `"com": { "allow": ["ADODB.Connection"] }`, `"*"` allowing any (Proposed, the owner's answer read so); making one it does not list is a `ComPermissionError`, WL3018, a class and a code of its own (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, so that each refusal of the configuration is told apart, in place of WL3001; its name and code Proposed).
- **How it is built** (approved 15 November 2026). In C#, `IDispatch` called through unmanaged function pointers on its table of methods (`delegate* unmanaged[Stdcall]`), with `CoInitializeEx`, `CLSIDFromProgID`, `CoCreateInstance`, `VARIANT`s and `BSTR`s by platform invoke, rather than through .NET's built-in COM interop (`dynamic`, `Type.InvokeMember`, `[ComImport]`), which Native C# will not have (WL-017), so that one source compiles with either toolchain; adapter-only code, listed in the portability inventory when it is built. Left out at first, as ASP's pages mostly did without them: events (connection points), arguments by reference and out arguments, and named arguments.

---

## 4. Runtime values, objects and garbage collection (WL-R001–WL-R010)

### 4.1 Runtime value representation

The proposed implementation uses a compact tagged `WebLingoValue` (target size **16 bytes**) with fields conceptually including kind, flags, auxiliary metadata and a 64-bit payload. Reference-like values use VM-managed handles rather than exposing arbitrary managed object pointers.

- Numeric primitives may fit inline; decimal128 may require boxed storage.
- Objects carry type metadata and reference identity.
- Arrays, functions and closures are VM-managed object types.
- Physical layout and handle generation must be validated against GC, interop and Native C# portability constraints; 16 bytes is an implementation target, not a proven ABI guarantee.

### 4.2 Garbage collection — approved

- WebLingo uses its **own tracing GC**, initially **nonmoving mark-and-sweep**.
- WebLingo GC is distinct from the .NET GC used to run the initial implementation.
- WebLingo GC is also distinct from the future Native C# runtime's deterministic lifetimes and reference-counted structures.
- Resource cleanup through `dispose()` is independent of reachability-based memory reclamation.

### 4.3 Worker-local heap ownership — approved M0.5

- Mutable WebLingo heaps are worker-local.
- Request VM frames and mutable request state belong to their assigned worker.
- Immutable application data may be shared under explicit rules.
- Cross-worker transfer of mutable data requires a defined copy, ownership-transfer, or message-passing contract; unsynchronized shared mutable references are forbidden.
- Async operations must root or retain buffers and handles until completion.
- Native handles cannot be released while an outstanding operation uses them.

---

## 5. Bytecode and VM architecture (WL-BC001–WL-BC016)

### 5.1 WLBC goals

WebLingo ByteCode (WLBC) is intended to be portable, versioned, verified, architecture-independent and suitable for caching. It must not embed .NET object references, machine pointers or hosting-framework types.

WASP never writes a bytecode file: wherever it runs pages, in `wasp` and in the web hosts, it compiles and caches a page, and what the page includes, imports, renders or is laid out by, in memory only (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026; WL-T007).

### 5.2 VM architecture — **provisional**

The register VM is the **preferred first implementation**, but the final register-versus-stack decision is explicitly reserved until benchmarks compare equivalent workloads compiled from the **same bound/lowered IR**.

| Consideration | Register VM | Stack VM |
|---|---|---|
| Dispatch count | Often lower | Often higher |
| Instruction size | Often larger | Often smaller |
| Compiler complexity | Higher | Lower |
| Dataflow/liveness | More explicit | More implicit |
| Native code / optimization potential | Good | Good |

M0.6 implementation default: implement the minimal register VM first and add a stack VM benchmark prototype later. **This does not approve WL-BC002 permanently.** The stack VM and the comparison exist since 13 November 2026 (§5.6, "The comparison"). The owner kept both VMs that day: every new feature is built on both, and the choice is made once the workloads not yet measurable can be measured. Those measured, the owner kept both still, on 15 November 2026 (§5.6, "Both VMs, for now").

### 5.3 Proposed WLBC binary container

- Header target: **32 bytes**, magic `WLBC`, major/minor version, ABI/feature flags, section count, directory offset and total length.
- Section-directory entry target: **32 bytes**.
- Sections: constants, type/module metadata, function table, code, exception tables, debug/source mapping, integrity metadata.
- Integer encoding: signed LEB128 where appropriate.
- Floating point: IEEE binary64.
- Decimal128 encoding: IEEE 754 BID **proposed**, not yet approved.
- Strings: UTF-8.
- Register count: proposed maximum **65,535 per function**.
- Operand encoding: variable-length **proposed**.
- Serialized control-flow targets: the instruction's index, as in memory (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026, in place of byte offsets).

The binary layout is **not yet frozen**. M0.6 may begin with an in-memory instruction model.

**The owner's answers** (16 November 2026, M0.13):

- A WLBC file holds one VM's bytecode and identifies that VM in its header. The compiler writes `.wlbr` for register bytecode and `.wlbs` for stack bytecode (approved 20 November 2026). A VM-specific suffix must agree with the header. Legacy `.wlbc` files still use their header to select the VM.
- A jump's target is its instruction's index, on disk as in memory: both VMs decode a function before they run it.
- Each section's CRC-32 is in its directory entry, checked when the file is read, in place of an integrity section.
- The container is built as proposed above, but stays Proposed: nothing is frozen until the work is done, and until then it may change without a new version.
- `weblingo compile` writes bytecode, `weblingo run` runs it and `weblingo disasm` shows it as text (§8.4, "CLI"). Running a `.wlg` compiles it in memory. WASP likewise writes no bytecode (§5.1).

**As built** (M0.13.1, Proposed; §15.2, "WLBC's layout"): version 0.1, which `WebLingoBytecodeFile` (`WebLingo.Bytecode`) writes and reads, for both VMs' modules. The header names the VM, 1 for the register VM and 2 for the stack VM. Seven kinds of section follow, each once and in this order: the module (its name, flags, special classes, parameters and imports), constants, functions, code, classes and units, handlers, and source positions, the last optional. A file's source positions are the compiler's (UTF-16 indices), with a table for each file of the line and column each of them stands at, so that a runtime error is located without the source. The reader checks the header, the directory, each section's bounds and CRC-32, every count against the bytes left and every value's range, then verifies the module with its VM's verifier (§5.5); a file that fails is WL4001. On a damaged file it never throws, and allocates no more than the file's size and the verifier's own bounds allow.

### 5.4 Proposed instruction families

- Constant/local/global loads and stores.
- Integer, floating and decimal arithmetic; comparisons.
- Branches and conditional jumps.
- Function calls, returns, closures and captures.
- Object, array and property operations.
- Exceptions, cleanup and disposal regions.
- Async await, suspend/resume and cancellation checks.
- Template literal, escaped expression and raw expression writes.

Function calls may use contiguous argument registers and an explicit receiver. Exceptions use table-driven protected/cleanup regions, approved by the owner on 13 November 2026 (§3.7).

### 5.5 Verification

Verification is mandatory before executing bytecode. It must validate:

1. Container structure, versions and section bounds.
2. Opcode and operand validity.
3. Branch and exception-region targets.
4. Constant, module and function references.
5. Register initialization and control-flow consistency.
6. Async/cleanup state and resource-safety constraints as implemented.

Malformed bytecode must fail with a defined verification error, not crash the VM.

### 5.6 Dispatch and benchmarks

An initial C# `switch` dispatch loop is **proposed** for clarity and portability. Alternative dispatch techniques may be benchmarked later. Benchmarks should include arithmetic, loops, calls, closures, property access, async suspension, template output, and representative I/O-bound server workloads. Measure throughput, latency, allocation, bytecode size, startup and implementation complexity.

**The comparison's parts** (WL-BC002, open question 1). The owner decided on 13 November 2026 that the stack VM is a project of its own, `WebLingo.VM.Stack`, beside `WebLingo.VM`, with its code generator in `WebLingo.Compiler`; that the benchmark is a console project, `WebLingo.Benchmarks`, timing the VMs with `Stopwatch` over fixed workloads (loops, calls, closures, strings), with no new package; and that the register VM is compared with a dispatch tuned for speed. The work lands in four parts:

1. **The benchmark:** `WebLingo.Benchmarks`, its workloads, and what it measures (time, instructions run, bytecode size, allocation), run on the register VM as it is, for a baseline.
2. **The register VM's dispatch, tuned:** the same bytecode, with the same results, run faster; measured against the baseline.
3. **The stack VM:** stack bytecode and its verifier (`WebLingo.Bytecode`), its code generator (`WebLingo.Compiler`), from the same lowered IR as the register one, and its VM (`WebLingo.VM.Stack`), sharing `WebLingo.Runtime`; every conformance program gives the same output and the same errors on both VMs.
4. **The comparison:** both VMs measured on the same workloads, the results recorded here, and the choice between them put to the owner.

**Workloads** (part 1, 13 November 2026): `loops`, nested `for` loops with int64 arithmetic, comparisons and `if`, a million times; `calls`, a recursive `fib(25)`, about 243,000 calls; `closures`, 20,000 closures made and 200,000 calls of them, sharing captured cells; `strings`, 200,000 concatenations and 20,000 comparisons, with the heap's collector. Each writes one result, which every VM must give.

**Baseline and tuned dispatch** (parts 1 and 2, 13 November 2026), on one Windows development machine (x64, .NET 10.0.15, Release), each the median of 7 timed runs after 3 warm-ups, with every method fully optimized from its first call (the benchmark turns .NET's tiered compilation off, so that no VM is timed while it is still being compiled; with the tiers on, part 1 measured the baseline at 130.3, 33.1, 51.6 and 29.4 ms). The baseline is the register VM as M0.7.6 left it; the tuned one runs the same bytecode, with the same instruction counts and results: one loop with the running frame in locals, the budget and the cancellation signal looked at only when they come due, int64 and binary64 arithmetic and comparisons done in the loop (the rest, and every error, by `WebLingoOperators`), and frames and their registers reused by the next call at the same depth. Allocation is .NET's, a run's. Instructions run, and code size (instructions, and bytes with a byte per operation and each operand it uses in LEB128), are the same for both: 10,010,008 instructions run and 24 instructions of code (76 bytes) for `loops`, 2,063,676 and 23 (74 bytes) for `calls`, 3,120,009 and 35 (106 bytes) for `closures`, 1,920,008 and 26 (83 bytes) for `strings`.

| Workload | Baseline | Tuned | Speed-up | Instructions/s, tuned | Allocated, baseline | Allocated, tuned |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| loops | 120.1 ms | 19.5 ms | 6.2× | 513.6 M | 3,288 B | 3,992 B |
| calls | 31.9 ms | 9.2 ms | 3.5× | 223.3 M | 48,561,928 B | 10,552 B |
| closures | 44.9 ms | 12.3 ms | 3.7× | 253.5 M | 36,733,560 B | 3,294,656 B |
| strings | 26.2 ms | 9.7 ms | 2.7× | 198.1 M | 10,731,560 B | 10,732,304 B |

**The stack VM** (part 3, 13 November 2026). Its bytecode (`WebLingoStackBytecodeContracts.cs`) is in `WebLingo.Bytecode`, beside the register bytecode, whose constants, imports and captures it shares, since the compiler, which generates it, may not depend on a VM (§8.3). A function has locals, its parameters first, and an operand stack, whose deepest point the generator counts; its frame has a slot for each, at most 65,535 in all, as a register function has registers, and the execution's register budget bounds its slots. An instruction takes its operands from the top of the stack and pushes its result, with at most two operands of its own (a local, a constant, a jump target, an argument count). Its verifier checks what the register verifier checks, and that the stack has the same depth wherever paths meet, never pops what it does not have, and never grows past its counted depth. `WebLingo.VM.Stack` holds the VM, which shares `WebLingo.Runtime`, and the result of an execution (`WebLingoExecutionResult`), with the register VM; since 16 November 2026 that result is the runtime's, and the stack VM no longer depends on `WebLingo.VM`, so that a host running one VM loads that VM's assembly alone (the owner's request). It is tuned as the register VM is (part 2). Every conformance program gives the same diagnostics, output and errors on both VMs, but for one difference: the instruction budget (WL3003) counts each VM's own instructions, and the stack VM runs more of them for the same program, so a program that runs out of its budget stops at a different point on each.

**The comparison** (part 4, 13 November 2026), as part 2 measured, with 11 timed runs: the tuned register VM, and the stack VM, tuned the same way, on the same workloads, compiled from the same lowered IR. The register VM is faster on every workload, by 1.10 to 1.65 times, and executes 1.29 to 1.75 times fewer instructions; the stack bytecode has more instructions, but is 3 to 35 percent smaller in bytes, since most of its instructions have one operand or none; both allocate the same in .NET's heap, and in WebLingo's.

| Workload | Register | Stack | Stack ÷ register | Instructions run, register | Instructions run, stack | Code, register | Code, stack |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| loops | 17.3 ms | 28.6 ms | 1.65 | 10,010,008 | 17,514,512 | 24 (76 B) | 41 (74 B) |
| calls | 9.1 ms | 10.0 ms | 1.10 | 2,063,676 | 2,670,639 | 23 (74 B) | 28 (48 B) |
| closures | 12.4 ms | 14.5 ms | 1.17 | 3,120,009 | 4,660,014 | 35 (106 B) | 52 (93 B) |
| strings | 11.0 ms | 12.8 ms | 1.16 | 1,920,008 | 3,100,012 | 26 (83 B) | 42 (76 B) |

Implementation complexity, in lines of source, the stack VM's against the register VM's: the code generator 375 against 468 (it allocates no registers, and needs no copy of a local that a later operand assigns), the verifier 346 against 402, the VM 633 against 604, and the instruction model 121 against 148. Not measured yet, because the language does not have them: property access, async suspension, template output, and I/O-bound server workloads (this section's list); and startup and latency, which matter once there is a host. The choice between the VMs is the owner's (WL-BC002).

**Both VMs, for now** (owner's decision, 13 November 2026). WL-BC002 stays provisional until property access, async suspension, template output and I/O-bound workloads can be measured too. Until then, every new feature is built on both VMs, from the same lowered IR, each with its code generator, its verifier and its VM, and gives the same results on both; and each of those workloads joins the benchmark when the language can express it, so that the choice is made on all of them. Every one of them measured (15 November 2026, "I/O-bound work"), the owner kept both VMs still, each feature built on both, until something more is measured, startup and latency, which matter once there is a host, among what is not yet; and WLBC goes ahead, a format for each VM's bytecode in one container (§9, M0.13; the owner's decision, 15 November 2026).

**Collections workload** (M0.9.4, 13 November 2026), measured as part 4 was: `collections` fills an array and a map, and reads, writes and visits them with `foreach` and indexing. The register VM took 3.7 to 4.5 ms over two runs, the stack VM 4.7 to 5.2 ms, running 605,064 and 1,030,106 instructions, from 67 instructions of code (223 bytes) and 115 (218 bytes); both allocated 1.06 MB.

**Property access** (M0.10.1, 13 November 2026), the first of the workloads "Both VMs, for now" waits for, measured as part 4 was: `objects` makes 100,000 instances, adds them, and reads their fields through their methods. The register VM took 80.7 to 81.7 ms over two runs, the stack VM 85.8 to 92.9 ms, running 3,200,025 and 5,000,039 instructions, from 50 instructions of code (168 bytes) and 73 (136 bytes); both allocated 43 MB of .NET's heap, since each instance keeps its fields in a table of its own. Async suspension, template output and I/O-bound work are still to come.

**Async suspension** (M0.11.1, 13 November 2026), the second of them, measured as part 4 was: `async` starts 100 tasks, each awaiting 1,000 times, so that 100,000 suspensions and resumptions take turns through the scheduler. The register VM took 5.4 to 6.4 ms over two runs, the stack VM 7.0 to 10.5 ms, running 701,913 and 1,403,221 instructions, from 34 instructions of code (108 bytes) and 59 (108 bytes); both allocated 77 KB of .NET's heap, a waiting frame being kept as it is, not copied. Template output and I/O-bound work are still to come.

**Template output** (M0.12.5, 14 November 2026), the third of them, measured as part 4 was: `templates`, a page (`templates.wasp`, compiled by the template compiler), writes a table of 10,000 rows, each its text, an integer and a string written escaped, and an integer written as it stands. The register VM took 6.1 to 6.7 ms over two runs, the stack VM 6.4 to 6.5 ms, running 280,010 and 400,015 instructions, from 40 instructions of code (135 bytes) and 58 (104 bytes); both allocated 14.8 MB of .NET's heap, the page's text and the strings the writes make. Writing and escaping text, the same for both, takes most of the time, so the two VMs are close here, the register VM's fewer instructions counting for less. I/O-bound work is still to come, with the platform layer.


**The built-in classes as native classes** (§9, "Native modules", part 4; §3.9, 15 November 2026), measured as part 4 was, three rounds of 11 timed runs, `main` and two builds that did not land run alternately, each the middle of its three medians: `std:core`'s `Array`, `Map`, `Set` and `Task`, their methods, `length` and indexers native functions, called through methods written in WebLingo (two calls for one), or directly by the VMs, given the instance first, without the method's frame. Only `collections`, which calls built-in members 140,000 times, changed beyond the machine's noise:

| Workload | VM | VMs' own | Through methods | Direct |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| collections | register | 4.5 ms | 12.6 ms (2.80×) | 10.2 ms (2.27×) |
| collections | stack | 5.1 ms | 13.7 ms (2.69×) | 11.7 ms (2.29×) |

Both ways, each call copies its arguments into an array of its own (8.1 MB allocated, against 1.06 MB), finds its member by its name in its class, and passes through two delegates to the runtime's own member; through methods, it runs a frame besides (1,105,096 instructions run on the register VM, against 605,064 either other way). The choice is the owner's. Measured again with their costs cut (15 November 2026): a built-in's arguments read where they are, never copied (a read-only view, `ReadOnlySpan`), and a member the runtime names by number found by it, `collections` took 9.0 ms on the register VM and 10.2 ms on the stack VM, against 4.4 and 5.0 on `main` (2.05 and 2.04 times), allocating 1.10 MB, as `main` does; the cost that is left is a native member's call itself, about 38 and 43 nanoseconds a use. With arguments read where they are, every built-in's call is cheaper: `templates` took 4.6 ms on the register VM, against 5.9, and 5.2 on the stack VM, against 5.8; the other workloads were as on `main`. The owner had the built-in classes keep the VMs' own members (15 November 2026, §3.9); what landed, native classes' properties, indexers and direct calls and arguments read where they are, measured against `main` as before, three rounds of every workload but `io`, alternately: every workload within the machine's noise (`collections` 4.5 ms against 4.4 on the register VM, 5.1 against 4.9 on the stack VM), but `templates`, 4.9 ms against 6.0 on the register VM and 5.0 against 6.2 on the stack VM, allocating 12.0 MB, not 14.8.

**I/O-bound work** (15 November 2026), the last of the workloads "Both VMs, for now" waits for, measured as part 4 was: `io` writes 1,000 small files through `std:fs` and reads them back, one at a time, each awaited, then all at once, with `Task.all`, in a folder of its own, which the benchmark makes and removes. The register VM took 1,100.5 ms, the stack VM 1,136.0 ms, running 114,080 and 152,109 instructions, from 245 instructions of code (758 bytes) and 307 (560 bytes); the file system's work, on the host's threads, takes nearly all the time, so that the two VMs are as close as the machine's noise. Every workload of this section's list can be measured now, and the choice between the VMs (WL-BC002) is the owner's. All of them, measured together in one run (15 November 2026), as part 4 was:

| Workload | Register | Stack | Stack ÷ register | Instructions run, register | Instructions run, stack | Code bytes, register | Code bytes, stack |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| loops | 26.9 ms | 33.5 ms | 1.25 | 10,010,008 | 17,514,512 | 76 | 74 |
| calls | 10.4 ms | 11.4 ms | 1.10 | 2,063,676 | 2,670,639 | 74 | 48 |
| closures | 13.6 ms | 15.9 ms | 1.17 | 3,120,009 | 4,660,014 | 106 | 93 |
| strings | 10.9 ms | 12.9 ms | 1.18 | 1,920,008 | 3,100,012 | 83 | 76 |
| collections | 4.4 ms | 4.9 ms | 1.11 | 605,064 | 1,030,106 | 223 | 218 |
| objects | 98.5 ms | 102.4 ms | 1.04 | 3,200,026 | 5,000,042 | 187 | 156 |
| async | 4.0 ms | 5.0 ms | 1.25 | 701,913 | 1,403,221 | 108 | 108 |
| templates | 6.2 ms | 6.1 ms | 0.98 | 280,010 | 400,015 | 135 | 104 |
| io | 1,100.5 ms | 1,136.0 ms | 1.03 | 114,080 | 152,109 | 758 | 560 |

**Bytes built from pieces** (18 November 2026, "Native BytesBuilder"), measured as part 4 was, two rounds of 11 timed runs, the class written in WebLingo and the native one run alternately, both with the heap's fix of the same day (`e07f95c`): `bytes` adds 300,000 small pieces, integers, two-character strings and three bytes, to 100 builders, each made into bytes once and read. The native builder, a buffer that doubles as it must (§15.2, "Bytes"), is about 18 times as fast, runs a quarter of the instructions, and allocates a twelfth of .NET's heap; so it was kept, as the task asks. The other workloads were within the machine's noise on it, `objects`, the noisiest, run three rounds alternately, the middle of each build's medians 119.1 ms against 119.2 on the register VM.

| Builder | VM | Median | Instructions run | Allocated |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| written in WebLingo | register | 216.9–222.4 ms | 6,403,531 | 140.9 MB |
| written in WebLingo | stack | 228.0–238.5 ms | 9,805,547 | 140.9 MB |
| native | register | 11.2–12.7 ms | 1,602,126 | 11.9 MB |
| native | stack | 12.4–14.4 ms | 2,303,442 | 11.9 MB |

---

### 5.7 Local source debugging

On 19 November 2026, the owner approved local `.wlg` launches on the default register VM in VS Code.
The first release covers line breakpoints, stepping, the active call stack, and local variables. Debugging WASP,
the stack VM, and attaching to running processes require separate work.

The VS Code extension starts `weblingo --debug-adapter` and communicates over the Debug Adapter Protocol on
standard streams. Debug compilation records statement points and lexical local ranges in memory. The adapter
verifies breakpoints against those points after compilation, and the VM snapshots the active frames and their
values on its execution worker. Inspection reads values without calling script methods.

On 20 November 2026, the owner approved a double-click Windows setup. Each WebLingo ZIP includes
`Install WebLingo.bat`, which starts a setup script with the bundled WebLingo runtime. Only the machine installer
requests elevation through Windows PowerShell. Setup offers Explorer's `.wlg` registration and the VS Code debugger
to the account that opened the launcher. The installed `VSCode\WebLingo.vsix` also supports offline installation later.

On 20 November 2026, the owner requested a matching uninstall script. The installed runtime and release ZIP provide
`Uninstall WebLingo.bat` to undo setup's machine installation and the initiating account's optional integrations.

## 6. Hosting architecture (WL-H001–WL-H014)

### 6.1 Approved hosting decisions

1. **IIS Stage 1:** ASP.NET Core adapter first (WL-H009).
2. **Concurrency:** worker-affine request execution contexts (WL-H012).
3. **Isolation:** logical isolation among applications in a shared runtime (WL-H013).
4. **Reload:** atomic application generations; old requests drain on old generation (WL-H004).

The initial IIS adapter is an implementation choice, not a permanent dependency of the language or runtime. FastCGI, standalone HTTP and eventually a native IIS integration remain targets; FastCGI's host is built (§6.13, M0.19).

### 6.2 Host-independent layers

```text
.wlg scripts / .wasp templates / modules / routes
                    |
         WebLingo Compiler + VM
                    |
          WebLingo Runtime
                    |
    WLHI: host-neutral request/response/lifecycle API
                    |
      +-------------+--------------+
      |             |              |
  IIS adapter    FastCGI       Standalone HTTP
  (ASP.NET Core) adapter       (Kestrel initially)
```

Hosting adapters must not directly manipulate VM registers, GC internals or compiler structures.

**WLHI, the WebLingo Host Interface**, is what a host and the WebLingo runtime know of each other, and all they know: the contracts through which a host, IIS's adapter, FastCGI's, or a standalone HTTP server, starts and stops the runtime, and has it run requests. It is made of the runtime's lifecycle (§6.4); the lifecycle of a request, from the application generation it is bound to through its execution context to its release (§6.6); the HTTP request and response a script sees, with their headers and streamed bodies (§6.7); and, for a host that is not .NET, the C-compatible native ABI that carries these across a process's boundary (§6.8). A host adapts to WLHI, never the reverse (§11). It was WSHI, the WebScript Host Interface, until the names were renamed (§1; the owner's decision, 14 November 2026).

### 6.3 Runtime hierarchy

```text
WebLingoRuntime
  ApplicationManager
    ApplicationGeneration A
    ApplicationGeneration B
  Scheduler
  ModuleCache
  CompilerService
  PlatformServices

ApplicationGeneration
  Configuration
  ModuleGraph
  RouteTable
  CapabilitySet
  Immutable/shared generation state
  Worker-local execution state

RequestContext
  Request / Response
  OwningWorker
  ExecutionContext
  Cancellation
  ResourceBudget
  Request-local heap and resources
```

The runtime can host multiple applications. Application boundaries isolate module namespaces, capabilities and mutable state logically; **OS process isolation is still required for hostile tenants or stronger security guarantees**.

### 6.4 Runtime lifecycle

`Created → Initialized → Running → Stopping → Stopped`

- Initialization configures compiler, VM, scheduler, GC and platform services.
- Shutdown stops accepting requests, drains in-flight work up to a configured deadline and releases resources.
- Host termination after the deadline is possible; cleanup code must not assume process exit always permits graceful completion.

### 6.5 Application generations and hot reload — approved

`Discovered → Loading → Compiled/Validated → Initialized → Ready → Serving → Draining → Unloaded`

- Source/configuration changes create a **new generation**.
- Compile and initialize the replacement before publication.
- Atomically publish only a healthy new generation.
- New requests use the new generation; existing requests finish on the old one.
- The old generation unloads after its active requests and retained operations drain.
- Failed new generations do not displace the last healthy generation.

**As built** (M0.14.6, 16 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "Reloading"): `WebLingoApplication.TryReload` makes a new generation from the root's files and publishes it, at once and whole, if its routes can be made, and otherwise keeps the generation served; a request already running finishes on the generation it began on, which goes, with its compiled pages, once the last such request has. `wasp serve` watches the root (`WebLingoReloader`, in the ASP.NET Core adapter) and reloads once the files have stopped changing for a quarter of a second, one generation for a burst of changes.

### 6.6 Request lifecycle

`Received → Select application generation → Resolve route → Create worker-affine execution context → Invoke script/template → Stream response → Cleanup → Release`

- Request-local mutable state is not shared between requests.
- Async suspension does not block an OS thread.
- Completion callbacks enqueue resumptions onto the owning VM worker.
- A request remains bound to its original application generation.

**As built** (M0.14.7.2, 16 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "Executions that wait without a thread"): an execution whose context `Suspends` returns, `Waiting`, when it has nothing to run but waits for work off its worker or for a timer on the host's clock, and its VM's `Resume()` goes on with it, on both VMs; `WebLingoApplication.Handle`, given the request's own scheduler host, gives such a request's outcome as `Waiting`, with its `Resume`; and the ASP.NET Core adapter's worker pool resumes it on its worker when its scheduler host is posted work, its timer is due, or its client goes, running other requests meanwhile.

### 6.7 HTTP request/response interfaces

Conceptual interfaces:

```csharp
public interface IWebLingoHttpRequest
{
    string Method { get; }
    string Path { get; }
    string QueryString { get; }
    string Scheme { get; }
    string Host { get; }
    IWebLingoHeaderCollection Headers { get; }
    IWebLingoBodyReader Body { get; }
}

public interface IWebLingoHttpResponse
{
    int StatusCode { get; set; }
    IWebLingoHeaderCollection Headers { get; }
    IWebLingoBodyWriter Body { get; }
}
```

Response states: **Uncommitted → Committed → Completed**. Headers cannot be mutated after commitment; body output must support streaming and backpressure. Request bodies must be streamable without mandatory full buffering.

**As built** (M0.14.1, 16 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "WLHI's HTTP contracts"): the contracts are in `WebLingo.Hosting.Abstractions`, free of .NET's HTTP types, their errors results, not exceptions: `IWebLingoHttpRequest`, `IWebLingoHttpResponse` (its status set by `TrySetStatusCode`, `Commit()` and `Complete()`), `IWebLingoHeaderCollection`, `IWebLingoBodyReader` and `IWebLingoBodyWriter`; `WebLingoHeaderList` holds headers for every adapter, and `WebLingoBufferedRequest` and `WebLingoBufferedResponse` hold a request and a response in memory. Bodies are read and written in pieces, synchronously; their streaming with backpressure is M0.14.7's.

### 6.8 WLHI native ABI — proposed

A future C-compatible ABI will use opaque runtime, application and request handles, with create/load/begin/cancel/release operations. Final signatures must define ABI versioning, struct sizes, calling conventions, ownership, callback lifetime, threading and error reporting. No managed .NET object crosses the ABI.

### 6.9 IIS and deployment

Example site:

```text
MySite/
  index.wasp
  products.wasp
  api/products.wlg
  lib/database.wlg
  wasp.config
```

The desired deployment model is automatic source detection, compilation and caching, with no build tooling required on the server. IIS uses the ASP.NET Core adapter first. FastCGI and standalone hosts translate protocol details into WLHI.

**One installation for every site** (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026, WebLingo replacing PHP): WebLingo is installed once on a server, in a folder of its own, and every site shares it, as PHP is installed once for every PHP site: `wasp`, which a site's `web.config` has IIS's ASP.NET Core Module run, `weblingo`, their libraries and the standard modules (`std/`). A site holds only its own files, its pages, modules and other files, its `wasp.config` and its `web.config`, never a copy of the runtime, and updating the installation updates every site. How it is published and where it goes are Proposed (§15.2, "Installing WebLingo on a server").

**A site's files beside its `web.config`** (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026): a site's folder is its application's root, as a PHP site's is, its pages, modules and other files side by side with its `web.config`, and the module runs `wasp serve` in it, without `--root`. `wasp` never serves `web.config`, nor `wasp.config` (§6.12, "Routes"). Where IIS writes `wasp`'s output is Proposed (§15.2, "Installing WebLingo on a server").

### 6.10 Scheduling — approved affinity, proposed fairness

- Bounded VM worker pool.
- Execution contexts are **worker-affine** and resume on their owning worker.
- Platform I/O completion may happen on another host thread but must post work to the owner.
- Instruction-quantum yielding and bounded blocking-operation pools are recommended.
- Work stealing or migration is deferred until supported by explicit safe-transfer semantics.

### 6.11 Security and diagnostics

Applications have immutable capability policies for filesystem, network, database, environment (§7.15), process and native extensions. Enforcement occurs at the service boundary; hiding functions is not sufficient. Diagnostics should include request IDs, latency, VM time, await time, GC pauses, allocations, module cache, generation count, worker utilization, failures and leaks.

### 6.12 Applications, routes and handlers — approved

The owner's answers to M0.14's questions (16 November 2026), which its parts build (§9, "M0.14 parts"):

- **The request and the response in a page:** values its parameters take, `param request;` and `param response;`, rather than values every page has, or a standard module's. What they are (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026, M0.14.3's question): instances of two built-in classes, `Request` and `Response`, read and changed through their members, `request.method`, `request.query["id"]`, `request.headers["Accept"]`, `response.status = 404;`, `response.headers["Content-Type"] = "text/plain";`, a wrong status or header an error where it is set; the compiler gives `param request;` its value when the statement runs, once the program's classes are made, a change to the compiler and the runtime, not to the VMs. In a layout and a component (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026): `param request;` and `param response;` are the host's, the request's own, unless the page gives others, as it gives any other parameter, `{ request: request }`; and a page's own are made before its layout's values are, so that it can give them on. The response is written by `response.write(value)` and `response.writeLn(value)` (the owner's answer, 20 November 2026, "Console Module", A2), as classic ASP's `Response.Write`, a route handler's way to write it, a page's blocks having `write` and `writeLn` of their own besides, without an import, which write where its text goes (§3.9, "The console: `std:console`").
- **A `.wlg` route handler:** a module exporting a function for each HTTP method it answers, `export async function get(request, response) { … }`, `post`, and so on; any other method is a 405.
- **Routes:** a request's path finds its file under the application's root by the file's path without its extension: `/products` is `products.wasp` or `products.wlg`, both being an error; `/` and a folder's path its `index.wasp` or `index.wlg`. Any other file of the root is served as it is (CSS, images), but never a `.wlg` that is not a handler, a bytecode file, or the configuration.
- **Serving:** `wasp serve [--root site] [--port 8080]`, locally, and under IIS, which the site's `web.config` has run it (§8.4: `weblingo` runs scripts only).
- **Errors:** a runtime error in a request is a 500, its body a page showing the error, its file, line, column, code and message, when the application runs in development, and a page without them otherwise; the configuration's `"mode"` says which, development for `wasp serve` and production under IIS by default (its `"environment"` until the owner had it renamed, 18 November 2026, so that the machine's environment's permission, `"env"`, is not taken for it, §7.15, nothing kept for the old name).
- **The host's core:** the runtime, its applications, their generations, routes and requests (§6.3 to §6.6) are a portable project of their own, `WebLingo.Hosting`, composing the compiler, the templates and the VMs; the ASP.NET Core adapter only translates HTTP to and from WLHI.
- **Configuration:** `weblingo.config`, in place of `weblingo.json`, for a script's folder; and for an application's root, and a page's folder under `wasp`, `wasp.config`, since what it configures is the WASP site, not the language, its name in lower case, for Linux's sake (the owner's decision, 16 November 2026, in place of `weblingo.config`, nothing kept for backward compatibility, and a script's folder keeping `weblingo.config`, the owner's answer the same day).
- **Streaming** (the owner's answers to M0.14.7's questions, 16 November 2026): a page's output is kept until it reaches about 64 KB, then sent as it is written, so that most pages, smaller, are still a 500 on an error, and a larger one, failing after its output was first sent, ends there, the connection closed so that the client sees it unfinished, the error in the host's log and, in development, at the page's end too; and a page whose output a slow client has not yet taken waits as at an `await`, its worker running other requests' tasks meanwhile, template output becoming an asynchronous operation (WL-T008), a change to both VMs, for at most a time, its request then cancelled. That time (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026, M0.14.7.3's question): 30 seconds, unless the application's `wasp.config` gives another, `{ "sendTimeout": 30 }`, in seconds. A request's body (the owner's answer, 16 November 2026, M0.14.7.4's question): `await request.text()`, the whole body as text, UTF-8 unless its `Content-Type` names another charset, and `await request.form()`, an `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` form's fields as a map, as PHP's `$_POST`, each read as the body arrives but given whole; and `await request.read()`, the body's next piece as it arrives, as bytes rather than text, `null` at its end, for a body too large to hold whole.

**As built** (M0.14.2, 16 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "The host's core"): `WebLingo.Hosting`'s `WebLingoApplication` loads an application from its root through the platform's files, its routes made then (`WebLingoRouteTable`), and serves a request on the worker its host gives: a page compiled when first requested, once on each worker, and kept for the generation's life with the components it renders, its output kept until it has run, then written to the response as UTF-8 (from M0.14.7.1, kept until it reaches 64 KB, then sent as it is written, §15.2, "Output sent as it is written"); a file that is not a page's, nor a handler's, left to the host to serve (`WebLingoRequestOutcomeKind.File`); and a 404 for a path without a route. The host gives its built-ins, its native modules, its workers' count and the environment.

**As built** (M0.14.3, 16 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "The request and the response in scripts"): a page's `param request;` and `param response;` are instances of the host's native classes `Request` and `Response`, their headers of `Headers`: a request's `method`, `path`, `scheme`, `host`, `query`, a map, and `headers`; a response's `status` and `headers`; a header read and set by indexing, and `has`, `add` and `remove`.
