- Detect static include and layout cycles at compilation/linking time and report source-located diagnostics.
- Bound dynamic component recursion or cycles at runtime where static detection is impossible.
- Do not allow template composition to bypass module visibility, application isolation, or capability checks.
- Maintain deterministic path normalization across Windows and Linux.

### WL-T007 — Compilation, caching, and atomic reload

Maintain a dependency graph for pages, includes, layouts, and components. When a shared template changes, invalidate affected compiled artifacts and build a **new application generation**. Publish that generation atomically only after successful compilation/initialization; existing requests finish on their previous generation. Compilation and cache identity must account for transitive dependencies, configuration, compiler version, and relevant security policy. A component invocation must not cause reparsing on every request. The cache is held in memory: WASP writes no `.wlbc` file (§5.1, the owner's decision of 16 November 2026).

### WL-T008 — Streaming, scheduling, and delivery gate

Rendering must support asynchronous output and backpressure without mandatory full-document buffering. Component and layout rendering must honor the WebLingo-owned operation model and worker-affine execution contexts. The first `.wasp`-capable release is **not complete** until all three mechanisms (includes, layouts, parameterized components) are implemented and covered by end-to-end tests, including nested composition, escaping, invalid paths, cycles, dependency invalidation, cancellation, and streaming.

**Project structure amendment:** Introduce `WebLingo.Templates` as the dedicated `.wasp` parser, composition resolver, dependency-graph builder, and lowering layer feeding the common compiler IR. Add `WebLingo.Templates.Tests` and template composition conformance tests. This is an approved architectural responsibility; the precise project boundaries and public C# interfaces can be finalized during implementation.

**Named sections:** Optional layout sections for page-specific scripts, styles, and metadata are a **proposed extension**. They are not part of the three mandatory mechanisms unless separately approved.

### Contracts — approved 13 November 2026

The owner's answers to what sections 2.2 and 13 left open, approved on 13 November 2026:

- **The language does not depend on templates.** WebLingo is a general-purpose, embeddable scripting language; nothing in the language itself depends on templates. Templates are a technology built on it, for web servers, as classic ASP was: WASP, Web Application Server Pages (§1; WebLingo Pages until 15 November 2026).
- **Their compiler.** A `.wasp` compiles through a template compiler (`WebLingo.Templates`), which incorporates the WebLingo compiler and extends it; it has a command-line program of its own, `wasp`, in a project of its own, `WebLingo.Wasp.Cli`. `weblingo` compiles and runs the language only.
- **What a template is.** Its text is written as it stands, `<% … %>` are statements, `<%= … %>` and `<%- … %>` write their expression, and `<%# … %>` is nothing. A statement may span several blocks, the text between them written as if it were printed there, as classic ASP did: `<% foreach (var p in products) { %><li><%= p.name %></li><% } %>`.
- **Where it writes.** Where `write` writes: standard output under a command-line program, a response's body under a host.
- **Escaping.** `<%= value %>` writes the text `write` would write, an instance's `toString()` included, with `&`, `<`, `>`, `"` and `'` as `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;`, `&quot;` and `&#39;`, the same in text and in attribute values; escaping for URLs, scripts and styles comes later, as its own decision (decided 17 November 2026, "Context-aware escaping", below, these five staying HTML's text's and a quoted attribute's).
- **`null`.** `<%= null %>` and `<%- null %>` write nothing, so that an optional read that finds nothing is blank; `write(null)` goes on writing `null`.
- **Line breaks.** A line holding only a `<% … %>` block, and spaces, writes nothing, not even its line break, so that statements leave no blank lines.
- **A block's end** (approved 14 November 2026). The template compiler reads a block's code as WebLingo's tokens, so that `%>` in a string or a comment does not end it: `<% var end = "%>"; %>`. A `//` comment therefore runs to the end of its line, `%>` included.
- **Includes.** `<% include "/includes/header.wasp"; %>` is a statement whose path is a string literal, resolved and compiled with the page. It is textual: the included template sees the variables where it is included, as if its text stood there. A path starting with `/` is from the application's root, any other from the including file's folder. In the language proper, `/` is the drive's root, `./` the current folder, and `xyz/abc` the current folder's `xyz` subfolder, and so on. Under `wasp`, the application's root is the folder `--root` names (`wasp run --root site page.wasp`), the page's folder when it is left out; under a web server, the site's root (approved 14 November 2026). `import`, for what has a scope of its own, is the language's module system, for `.wlg` files and templates alike, designed as a task of its own.
- **Layouts.** `<% layout "/layouts/main.wasp", { title: "Products" }; %>`: at most one for a page, given the values the layout reads as its parameters, which it declares as a component does, a missing or an extra one being an error, so that the page's body runs, and streams, where the layout calls `renderBody()` (WL-T003). A layout may itself have a layout, and so on; a cycle of them is a compile-time error.
- **Components.** `renderComponent(path, { product: p })` is a built-in: its path is any string, and the component is found and compiled when it runs. A component declares its parameters each with a statement of its own, as `var` declares a variable, `<% param product; param size = 10; %>`, each then a variable of it, a missing one an error, an extra one an error, a default making one optional, any expression, evaluated when the parameter is missing. `param` is a keyword of the language itself, a module's parameter (§3.9). A component is compiled on first use and kept, recompiled when its file changes, in the application's generation (WL-T004, WL-T007); a missing one is a runtime error, WL5xxx, which a catch may catch.
- **Asynchrony.** `async` and `await` are designed first, before templates (section 13's examples use `await`): §3.8.
- **Errors.** Template errors (a cycle of includes or layouts, a missing file, a path outside the application, a component's parameters) have a range of codes of their own, WL5001 on.

### Context-aware escaping — approved 17 November 2026

**The owner's answer** (17 November 2026, "Context-Aware Escaping in Pages", M0.15): the template compiler reads the HTML around each `<%= %>` and escapes its value for the context it stands in, HTML's text, a quoted or an unquoted attribute, an event's attribute, a URL's, `<script>`, `<style>`, as Go's `html/template` does; with functions the author calls for what it cannot tell, `<%= Html.url(x) %>` and the like; in place of the author naming each context (Razor's helpers) or the compiler alone.

**As built** (17 November 2026; Proposed, §15.2, "Context-aware escaping"):

- **Where a value stands.** The template parser reads a page's HTML as it reads its text, as Go's `html/template` follows its contexts, through tags, attributes, their quotes, URLs and their parts, scripts and their strings, template literals, regular expressions and comments, styles and their strings and URLs, comments, titles and text areas, an attribute's value read with its character references decoded; it changes none of the page's text. Includes are textual, so the HTML goes on through them; a component and a layout begin in HTML's text.
- **How it is escaped.** HTML's text, and a quoted attribute's plain value, as before: `&`, `<`, `>`, `"` and `'` as entities. An unquoted attribute's value, its spaces, `=` and `` ` `` too. Where an attribute's name stands, only a plain attribute's name. A URL whose scheme could run a script or carry a page (only `http`, `https` and `mailto` are allowed) is refused at its beginning, and a URL is percent-encoded as it needs, its query's and fragment's values whole. A script's value is written as JSON safe in HTML; a script's string, template literal and regular expression get JavaScript's escapes. A style's value that could change what the style means is refused; a style's string gets CSS's escapes. A value refused is written `zWebLingoz`, as Go writes `ZgotmplZ`; a value in a script's or a style's comment, nothing.
- **What the compiler cannot follow** is WL5008, at compile time: HTML that browsers read in more than one way (a quote in an unquoted attribute's value or in an attribute's name), a block of code that ends somewhere other than where it began, the branches of an `if` that end in different places, text that moves the HTML in a statement's body without braces, a component rendered or a layout's body written outside HTML's text, a page that ends outside it, and a value where it is not known which part of a URL it is in, or a `/` where it is not known whether it divides.
- **What the author calls.** `Html`, a built-in class, `std:html`'s, imported (the owner's decision, 18 November 2026, §3.9, "The standard library"): `Html.escape(x)`, `Html.url(x)`, `Html.urlPart(x)`, `Html.js(x)`, `Html.jsString(x)` and `Html.css(x)`, each giving a value's text escaped so, for where the compiler cannot tell what an attribute holds (`hx-get`, `x-data`), or for text built in code; a helper's text is escaped again for where it stands, as any string is.
- **Raw output** stays explicit, `<%- %>`, which the compiler takes to leave the HTML where it was.

---

*This document consolidates the decisions and technical designs discussed through approval of M0.6 and WL-T001–WL-T008. It distinguishes approved requirements from proposed implementation details and deliberately unresolved contracts; it is an architectural reference, not a claim that implementation is complete.*


## 14. M0.6.1 solution scaffold — implementation status (22 October 2026)

**Status: generated; built and tested on Windows and Linux.** This is an implementation milestone, not a change to previously approved language semantics.

- Created `WebLingo.slnx`, .NET 10 project files, centralized package versions, solution-wide build settings, and GitHub Actions build/test workflow for Windows and Linux (moved on 12 November 2026 to `.github/workflows/` at the repository root, where GitHub Actions reads it).
- Created portable Core, Language, Bytecode, Compiler, Runtime, VM, Platform.Abstractions, Templates, Hosting.Abstractions, StdLib project boundaries; .NET platform, ASP.NET Core host adapter, and CLI projects.
- Added a minimal lexer seed and unit tests, public scaffolding interfaces, template-composition contract placeholders, examples, and a Native C# portability inventory.
- The CLI supports `--version` only; `run` and `check` explicitly report that execution is not implemented. No compiler, VM execution, or template rendering is claimed.
- **Verification:** On Windows (12 November 2026, .NET SDK 10.0.404, a fresh clone), `dotnet restore WebLingo.slnx`, `dotnet build WebLingo.slnx -c Release` (no warnings) and `dotnet test WebLingo.slnx -c Release` (5 tests) succeed, and `weblingo --version` answers. (An earlier static validation, with .NET SDK 10.0.401, could not restore.) On Linux, the CI workflow's first run (CI run 1, 12 November 2026, commit `5229806`) built and tested the solution, by then M0.6.7's.
- The initial `WebLingoSourceSpan` and lexer positions use .NET string indices in this scaffold; production source-map byte offsets remain specified as UTF-8 and require explicit conversion.
- Register-based execution remains provisional (WL-BC002); both stack and register execution must eventually be benchmarked.
- All three approved template composition features (includes, layouts, parameterized components) remain mandatory together in the first `.wasp`-capable implementation.

## 15. M0.6 implementation — status and proposed choices

This section records the state of the M0.6 increments after M0.6.1 (§8.7), and the choices made while implementing them where this specification is silent. Each choice in §15.2 was made to unblock the work, and is kept in one place in the code so that it stays easy to change; its row gives its status, **Approved** once the owner decided it, **Proposed** while it awaits that decision. None of them changes an approved decision.

### 15.1 Status

| Increment | Status |
|---|---|
| M0.6.2 lexer and tokenization tests | Implemented (12 November 2026); built and tested on Windows and Linux |
| M0.6.3 parser, AST and diagnostics | Implemented (12 November 2026); built and tested on Windows and Linux |
| M0.6.4 binder and lowered IR | Implemented (12 November 2026); built and tested on Windows and Linux |
| M0.6.5 minimal bytecode generator and verifier | Implemented (12 November 2026); built and tested on Windows and Linux |
| M0.6.6 minimal register VM, arithmetic, locals and `write` | Implemented (12 November 2026): `weblingo run` runs §8.4's example and prints `30`; built and tested on Windows and Linux |
| M0.6.7 end-to-end conformance on Windows and Linux | Implemented (12 November 2026): every program of §8.6 runs through the CLI with its expected output, diagnostic and exit code; passes on Windows and Linux (CI run 1, 12 November 2026, commit `5229806`) |

M0.6's acceptance criteria (§8.8), as of 12 November 2026:

| Criterion | Status |
|---|---|
| Builds cleanly on Windows and Linux | Yes, with no warnings (warnings are errors): locally on Windows, and in CI on `ubuntu-latest` and `windows-latest` (CI run 1, 12 November 2026, commit `5229806`) |
| Dependency rules enforced | By the project references, and by a test of the built assemblies' references (`WebLingo.Architecture.Tests`, `DependencyTests`) |
| CLI compiles and runs simple `.wlg` programs | Yes: `weblingo run` and `weblingo check` (`WebLingo.Conformance.Tests`) |
| Lexer, parser, binder, generator, verifier and VM are separate components | Yes: `WebLingoLexer`, `WebLingoParser`, `WebLingoBinder` and `WebLingoLowerer`, `WebLingoCodeGenerator`, `WebLingoBytecodeVerifier`, `WebLingoVirtualMachine` |
| Tests pass; malformed inputs fail safely | Yes, on Windows and Linux (294 tests at M0.6.7, 305 with M0.8's checks); fuzz tests give random source to the compiler, and mutated bytecode to the VM, and find diagnostics, never a crash |
| Portability inventory exists | Yes, `Documents/native-compatibility.md`, with what M0.6 uses |
| Portable core has no ASP.NET Core or ADO.NET dependency | Yes, checked by `DependencyTests` |
| Compiler IR is not tied to a permanent VM choice | Yes: the lowered IR is a tree; only the code generator is register-specific |
| Build and test instructions are documented | Yes, the solution's `README.md` |

### 15.2 Proposed choices

| Area | Choice | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Line terminators | CRLF, LF, and CR each end a line (`WebLingoSourceText`) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Columns | One-based, counting Unicode scalar values from the start of the line; a tab is one column (`WebLingoSourceText`) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Whitespace | Space, tab, CR, and LF only; any other character, Unicode spaces included, is invalid (WL1003) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Comments | `//` to the end of the line; `/* … */`, not nested; an unterminated `/*` is an error (WL1005) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Identifiers | ASCII letters, digits and `_`, not starting with a digit; case-sensitive | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Keywords | `var` and `const`; `Infinity` and `NaN`, binary64 literals, which therefore cannot be declared or shadowed; `let` reserved and reported (WL-002.3); no other reserved words yet | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Integer literals | Decimal digits only; leading zeros allowed and still decimal (`007` is 7); at most 9223372036854775807, larger is an error (WL1004); hexadecimal and binary ones too, from 16 November 2026 (§3.5) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Hexadecimal and binary literals | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): `0x` or `0X` and hexadecimal digits, `0b` or `0B` and binary digits, at least one; a prefix without digits, or digits running into a letter, a digit or `_` (`0x1G`, `0b102`, `0x10m`), one invalid literal (WL1004), as "Literal suffixes" has it for decimal digits, so that no hexadecimal or binary literal is a decimal or a binary64; at most `0x7FFFFFFFFFFFFFFF`, larger WL1004, its message naming the largest in decimal; a `-` before one is negation, as before a decimal one | Proposed |
| Binary64 literals | Digits, then a fraction (`.` and digits, at least one on each side of the point: `1.` and `.5` are not literals), an exponent (`e` or `E`, an optional sign, and digits), or both: `1.5`, `0.1`, `1e21`, `2.5E-3`. The value is the nearest binary64; one too large for a binary64 is an error (WL1004), and one too small becomes 0. `Infinity` and `NaN` are keywords for those values, and `-Infinity` is unary minus applied to `Infinity` | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Literal suffixes | Digits followed directly by a letter, digit or `_` are one invalid literal (WL1004), which keeps room for the decimal suffix `m` (§3.2); `_` between two digits a separator, from 16 November 2026 (§3.5) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Digit separators | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): a `_` is read as part of a literal only with a digit of the literal's base on each side of it, a hexadecimal or binary one's included (`0xFF_FF`, `0b1010_0101`); any other `_` beside its digits, `1__000`, `1_`, `1_.5`, `1_e5`, `1e_5`, `0x_FF` or `1_m`, makes the literal run into it, one invalid literal (WL1004), as "Literal suffixes" has it. The value ignores them, and so does a decimal's constant, its digits written without them; a token's text keeps them | Proposed |
| Not yet lexed | Decimal literals: their characters are invalid (WL1003) until their syntax is specified, which it was on 16 November 2026, `1.5m` (§3.2, "Decimals") | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Statements | Variable declarations and expression statements, each ended by `;`; a lone `;` (an empty statement) is an error (WL1001); blocks, `{ … }`, from M0.7.1 (§3.5) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Operators | Unary `-` and `+` (`+` gives the number itself; on anything but a number either is WL3001); binary `* /` above `+ -`, both left-associative | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Assignment | An expression, right-associative (`a = b = 3`), whose value is the value assigned; only a variable's name may be assigned (WL1007), not even a parenthesized one | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Calls | `callee(argument, …)`, no trailing comma; the callee is any expression, checked when bound | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| `let` | Reported as not a WebLingo keyword (WL1001), with a hint to use `var` or `const`, and parsed as `var` to recover | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Declaration without a value | `var x;` and `const x;` are syntax errors (WL1006, §3.1) | Approved |
| Error recovery | After an error, parsing resumes at the next `;` or declaration keyword; a token the lexer reported is not reported again, and a statement gets at most one diagnostic for what follows its first error | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Nesting limits | Parsing recurses at most 256 levels (parentheses, unary operators, assignments, arguments), and an expression is at most 1000 operations deep, so that a recursive compiler stage cannot overflow its stack; deeper source is an error (WL1008). Both limits are arbitrary, and are constants that are easy to change (`WebLingoParser.MaxRecursionDepth`, `WebLingoParser.MaxExpressionDepth`). A thread's stack of 1 MB, Windows's, held about 200 levels before .NET had optimized the compiler, its frames then larger; so the command lines and the web hosts compile on threads of their own with a stack of 16 MB, the limits kept (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, "Parser: Deep Nesting Overflows the Stack", rather than a check of the stack left, or lower limits): `weblingo` and `wasp` run each command on such a thread (`WebLingoCommandThread`), and the web hosts' workers, where a page is compiled when first requested, are such threads (`WebLingoWorkerPool`), the size `WebLingoParser.CompilerStackSize` | Approved (12 November 2026; the compiler's stack, 17 November 2026) |
| Compilation stages | Binding runs only on a source without syntax errors, and lowering only on one that binds without errors: a source reports the errors of its first failing stage | Proposed |
| Host built-ins | The host declares its built-ins (name and arity) to the compiler; they live in a scope around the program's and are not privileged in any way, so a declaration may shadow one (`var write = 1;`) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Functions as values | Functions are values (M0.7.6, §3.5), and so are the host's built-ins (`var w = writeLn;`). Calling a literal is WL2005 at compile time, and calling a built-in or a declared function by its name with the wrong number of arguments is WL2006 at compile time; any other call is checked when it runs, with the same codes. Naming a built-in to assign it is WL3001 | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| The `foreach` protocol | `foreach (var item in x)`, for a value the VMs do not visit themselves (anything but an array, a set, a map or a string, or an instance holding one), calls `x.iterate()` once, before the loop, then the iterator's `hasNext()` before each `next()`, which gives the item; a value without a member `iterate` is WL3001, as before, an iterator without `hasNext()` or `next()` WL3006, and a `hasNext()` that gives anything but a Boolean WL3001, as a condition's is. A collection of a class extending `Array`, `Map` or `Set` is visited the VMs' own way, an `iterate()` of its class's aside; a class, or a module, with a static `iterate()` is visited by it, and so is any value whose class an `extend` block gives one. An array's, a map's, a set's and a string's `iterate()` gives an instance of a built-in class, `Iterator`, whose fields are `items` and `position`: an array's iterator visits the array as it is when each item is read, a set's, a map's and a string's what it held when `iterate()` was called; a map's items are its entries, each an array of two, `[key, value]`, and a string's its Unicode scalar values, each a string of one; `next()` with no item left is WL3005 (`WebLingoMembers.TryIterate`, `TryVisit`; `WebLingoPrelude`) | Proposed |
| Functions and calls | A function declaration is in scope throughout its block, and its name is a constant (WL2003). A variable a closure captures lives in a cell, which its block makes when it begins and its declaration initializes, so that every closure of it shares it; a `for`'s captured variable gets a new cell, holding the last value, before each step, so that each iteration's closures see their own. A hoisted function that reads such a variable before its declaration has run is WL2004 at run time (approved 13 November 2026, §3.5). `return` outside a function is WL2008. A call pushes a frame onto the VM's own stack, at most 10,000 deep (`WebLingoResourceBudget.MaxCallDepth`, WL3003). A function is written as `function`, and functions are equal only to themselves. From arrow notation (§3.5, 14 November 2026): `=>` is one token, after `==` (`a==>b` is `a == > b`); a `(` whose matching `)` is followed by `=>` begins an arrow function's parameters, and any other a parenthesized expression; after an error in its parameters, parsing resumes at their `)`; its body is a block when a `{` follows `=>`, so that a map is given in parentheses, `() => ({a: 1})`, and else an expression, parsed as an assignment's value is, read into a block holding `return` of it; an identifier followed by `=>` is a parameter without parentheses, a receiver never (`this => …` is not one); `async` before either form makes an `async` function; and it is parsed into the syntax `function (…) { … }` is, so that nothing after the parser tells them apart | Proposed |
| Members and indexing | From M0.9.1 (§3.6): a point without digits on both sides is a member access's, so `1.` and `.5` are a number and a point, and `5.length` is the integer's member (WL3006). A member is never assigned (`x.length = 1` is WL1007 at compile time) until classes have fields. Calling a member that is not a method (`s.length()`) is WL2005. A method call's target is evaluated before its arguments, and an element assignment's target, then its index, then its value. From M0.9.2: a method read without a call (`a.push`) is WL3001; an array's elements are evaluated in order; one being written already is written `[…]`, with the character U+2026. From M0.9.3: in a map literal, a later key replaces an earlier equal one (`{a: 1, a: 2}` is `{"a": 2}`), and a map's text writes a key as it writes a value (`{1: "one"}`), a map being written already as `{…}`. From M0.9.5: `#{` is one token, and `#` alone is still an invalid character; a set is a map's hash table without values. From M0.9.6: `?[` is one token, and `?` alone is still an invalid character; the index is evaluated even when the target is `null`; each `?[` stands alone, so a member or an index after one that gave `null` is read as usual (`m?["a"].length` is WL3006 when there is no `"a"`); a map is a hash table whose entries a removal fills from the last, so their order is not defined (§3.6) | Proposed |
| Classes | From M0.10.1 (§3.7): `class`, `new` and `this` are keywords, and `?.` one token. A class's value is made when its block begins, as a function's closure is, its methods being closures of the scope around it. A method is a function whose first parameter is `this`. `new` needs its parentheses (`new A;` is WL1001); calling a class by its name is WL2005 at compile time, and any other call of a class at run time. A constructor's own result is dropped: `new` gives the instance. `x.name(…)` on an instance calls a field holding a function as any function is called, and a method on the instance, a field being looked up first; a field cannot take a method's name (WL3001), and only an instance has fields to assign (WL3001 otherwise). A method read as a value is bound anew each time, so `p.m == p.m` is false. A class written as a value is `class Point`, and has no members but its static ones (from M0.10.4; `Point.x` is WL3006 when it has no static `x`). An instance keeps its fields in the order they were first assigned, which its text follows, the order being not defined. From M0.10.2: `extends` and `super` are keywords. A class's base is evaluated when its block begins, as the class is made then: it may name the block's classes, whatever their order (the compiler makes a base before the classes that extend it), and what is declared around the block, but not the block's variables (WL2001); a base that is not a class is WL3001, and a class that extends itself is WL2013. A class without a constructor gets one that only runs its base's; a constructor of a derived class that does not call `super(…)` anywhere, a function inside it included, runs `super()` first. `super(…)` outside a constructor, `super` outside a method of a derived class, and `super` neither called nor naming a member, are WL2009. A class's methods are its base's with its own in their place, gathered when it is made; `super.name` is the base's method bound to `this`, and a method the base does not have is WL3006 | Proposed |
| Disposal | From M0.10.3 (§3.7): `using` is a keyword. A `using`'s parentheses take `var` only: `const` or `let` there, or no keyword, is reported (WL1001) and read as `var`, for one diagnostic. Its value is evaluated before its name exists, so `using (var r = r)` names an outer `r` (WL2001 if there is none). `destructor()` is a name a class may give its `dispose()`: callers call `dispose()`, and `x.destructor()` is WL3006. A class's disposal is its own `dispose`, if it declares one, then its base's disposal, gathered when the class is made. Disposing an instance whose class and bases declare none is WL3006, at `x.dispose()` or at the `using`'s end, and so is a `using` of a value that is not an instance; a field holding a function, even one named `dispose`, does not make a value disposable. Disposal gives its methods no arguments: `x.dispose(1)` is WL2006, and so is a dispose method that takes some, when the disposal begins, before any of its methods runs. An instance's disposal begins once: from then on, `x.dispose()`, inside a dispose method too, does nothing and gives `null`; and a disposal gives `null`, whatever its methods return. `x.dispose` read as a value is bound as any method is, and calling it disposes `x` as `x.dispose()` does; `super.dispose()` is the base's method called as any other is, so it runs, besides the disposal's own run of it. A `using`'s disposal runs on each way out of its statement, innermost first: a `return`'s value is evaluated before it, and a `break` or a `continue` disposes the resources of the `using` statements inside its loop only | Proposed |
| Static members | From M0.10.4 (§3.7): `static` is a keyword. A static field's value is evaluated when its class is made, where its block begins, as a base is: it may name the block's classes and functions, and what is declared around the block, but not the block's variables (WL2001). Once a block's classes are made, their static fields are set, class by class in the order they are made (a base before the classes that extend it), each class's in its own order; a static field exists from when its class is made, as `null`, so that one read before it is set gives `null`. `this` in a static field's value is WL2009, and so is `super` in a static method; and, from 20 November 2026 (§3.7), `this` in a static method is the class it is called on, but in an operator, WL2009, `'this' is not allowed in an operator or a static field's value.`, an operator's class being one of its operands'. A static method whose body names `this`, an interpolation's and a closure's included, is a function with a receiver, as a method is (`HasThis`), the parser saying which do (`WebLingoMethodSyntax.UsesThis`), so that one that does not costs nothing more; reading it through a class binds it to that class (`WebLingoHeap.TryGetStatic`'s `takesClass`, as a native class's factory is bound), and a static property's accessor that names it is given the class it is read on; reached through an instance, or a collection of a class extending `Array`, `Map` or `Set`, its `this` is the instance's class, as Python's class methods are given theirs. `static let` is reported (WL1001) and read as `static var` (`static const` declares a constant, from 14 November 2026), `static var x;` is WL1006, and a static `constructor` is WL1001. A class's static members have names of their own, apart from its methods' (a static member and a method may share one), but not from each other (WL2002). Static members are reached through a class, and from 14 November 2026 through an instance or a collection of the class, after its fields and its class's methods, a static method so reached called without `this`, or, one that names `this`, with the instance's class; assigning one through an instance, `p.count = 5`, assigns the static field, when the class or a base has one (a static constant being WL3001), or the instance's own field of that name, if it has one already, which shadows it, and else makes a field of the instance (from 14 November 2026); a static method so reached is not replaced, the instance getting a field. Assigning a static field that neither the class nor a base declares declares it, on the class itself, as the owner's note of 13 November 2026 has it (§3.7), a built-in class included (`Integer.twice = …`); assigning a static method replaces it (from 14 November 2026; WL3001 before). `Name.name(…)` calls a static member as any function is called, a static field holding a function included | Proposed |
| `Object` | From M0.10.5 (§3.7): `Object` is a class written in WebLingo, `class Object { equal(other) { return this == other; } }`, which the compiler adds to a program that uses it, made where the program begins, in a scope around the program's. A program may declare a name `Object` of its own; a class without `extends` still extends the built-in one, and `Object = 1;` is WL2003, as for any class. Every class but `Object` is therefore derived: `super.name` is allowed in any class's methods (`super.m()` where `Object` has no `m` is WL3006 when it runs, no longer WL2009), and `super()` in any constructor; a class without `extends` does not run `Object`'s constructor, which does nothing. An instance of `Object` is written `Object {}`. A limit of the execution reached inside `Object`'s method (WL3003, WL3004) is reported at a position of its source, not of the program's | Proposed |
| Exceptions | From M0.10.6 (§3.7): `try`, `catch`, `finally` and `throw` are keywords. `throw;` outside a `catch` block, in a function inside one included, is WL2010. `Error` is a class written in WebLingo, `class Error { constructor(message) { this.message = message; this.code = null; } }`, extending `Object`, which the compiler adds to a program that names it or has a `try` or a `using`. A runtime error becomes an instance of it only when a handler catches it, its fields set without running its constructor (`Error {message: "…", code: "WL3005"}`), so that a program that catches nothing reports its runtime errors as before. A `catch`'s variable is a new one for each exception, which a closure may capture. An exception that nothing catches is reported where it was first thrown: an instance of `Error`, or of a class extending it, whose `code` is a string, as that code and its `message`, so that a runtime error caught and thrown again (`throw;`, or a `finally`'s end) is reported as it would have been; an `Error` without a code as WL3008 with its message; any other value as WL3008 with its text. `throw e;` throws `e` from where it stands, `throw;` from where it was first thrown. When a dispose method throws, the disposal's next methods still run, The execution's limits (WL3003), a cancellation (WL3004) and invalid bytecode (WL4001) are not exceptions, and run no `finally` | Proposed |
| `toString()` | From M0.10.7 (§3.7): `Object`'s `toString()` gives `this`'s text, an instance's as its class's name and its fields, by an operation, `ToText`, that nothing else has. A value that is not an instance has `Object`'s `toString()` as its own, `null` and a class included (`null.toString()` is `"null"`, `Point.toString()` `"class Point"`), a class's static `toString` coming first. What `write` writes of an instance whose class overrides `toString()` is the string it gives, as it is, unquoted, inside a collection too; an instance being written as its fields, by `Object`'s `toString()`, is written `Name {…}` where it holds itself. A `toString()` that takes arguments is WL2006, and one that throws throws from the `write`, both where the text is written. The VMs run an overriding `toString()` as a call of the instruction that writes, on their own stacks, the text built with a stack of its own (`WebLingoTextBuilder`, `WebLingo.Runtime`), kept as a root while it waits; a built-in that writes text says so (`IWebLingoTextBuiltin`), and is given the text as a string. A program that has given no class a `toString()`, declared or while it runs, writes as before, without any of this, until it gives one (`WebLingoHeap.ToStringGiven`, which `setMethod` sets as a declaration does; until 18 November 2026 a VM decided it when it began, from the classes declared in the source alone, so that `write` ignored a `toString()` that `setMethod` gave, or one given to a class `new Class` made) | Proposed |
| Typed catches and classes of values | From typed catches (§3.7): `is` is a keyword, at the comparisons' precedence, its class a name or a dotted name. A catch's class is evaluated when an exception reaches it, where the `try` is; catches are tried in order; a catch that catches every exception before the last is WL1001; and an exception none catches is thrown again, from where it was first thrown, after the `finally`. The built-in classes (`Object`, `Error`, the kinds of values' and of runtime errors') are written in WebLingo, and added to a program that names them; a program with a `try` or a `using` gets every runtime error's class, and a runtime error that a handler catches becomes an instance of its kind's class (`IndexError {message: …, code: "WL3005"}`). `Class` was sealed until what extending it means was built, extending it, or `new` on it, being WL3001 (until 14 November 2026, see "Classes made while the program runs"); `Task` is, from M0.11.1 (`Boolean`, `Integer`, `Number`, `String` and `Function` were too, until classes could extend them, below). A value whose kind's class the program has not made is still an `Object`. A catch, or `is`, whose name is not a class calls its `equal(value)`, `IsInstance` making that call as a method call is made, and tests its result as a condition, so that one that is not a Boolean is WL3001 there; before the program has `Object`, `==` decides (`WebLingoMembers.IsInstance`). `x.equal(y)` on a value that is not an instance calls `Object`'s `equal`, as `toString()` is called | Proposed |
| Classes extending `Array`, `Map` and `Set` | From classes extending collections (§3.7): a collection that a class extending `Array`, `Map` or `Set` made is a collection of that kind, with its class and fields beside its elements, and its value marked as such, so that a plain collection's members cost nothing more. On it, a member is its field, then its class's method, then the collection's own member, so that a class may override `push`; `super.push(…)` is WL3006, `Array` declaring no methods in WebLingo. Its text, `Stack:[1, 2]`, does not show its fields; its class's `toString()` is used, as an instance's is. `dispose()` and `using` do not apply to it yet. `new Array()`, `new Map()` and `new Set()` are of no class, as `[]`, `{}` and `#{}` are | Proposed |
| Default values | From default values (§3.5): a default is evaluated each time its parameter is left out, when the function begins, left to right, in the function's scope but before its own parameter is declared, as a variable's initializer is (§3.1), so that it may use the parameters before it (`b = a * 10`), and `this` in a method, while `b = b` names an outer `b`. A parameter without a default after one with a default is WL1001. A call giving too few or too many arguments is WL2006 (`'f' takes 1 to 2 arguments`), at compile time for a function the call names, as before. A VM puts a missing value in the place of a parameter left out, which the function's code replaces with its default before anything else reads it, so that a `null` given is not taken for a missing one | Proposed |
| Asynchrony | From M0.11.1 (§3.8): `async` and `await` are keywords. `async` stands before `function`, in a declaration or an expression, and before a method's name; an `async` constructor is WL1001. `await` is a prefix operator, as unary `-` is, so `await a + await b` adds two results. `await` in a function that is not `async`, a function inside an `async` one included, is WL2014; at the top level, it makes the top level an `async` function itself, whose task is the program's, and the execution ends when that task completes; a top level that does not await ends the execution where it ends, the work its tasks have left being dropped. An `async` function's call makes its task, and runs it on the caller's stack until its first `await`, its `return` or its exception, when the call gives the task; from then on its frame is kept by its task, off the VM's stack, the values it holds being roots of the heap, and the scheduler resumes it alone on the stack. The scheduler resumes tasks in the order they became ready; `await` of a value that is not a task, or of a task already complete, makes its task ready after those ready already. With nothing ready, the earliest `delay` completes, on the host's clock (`WebLingoExecutionContext.Clock`, an `IWebLingoExecutionClock`, which the CLI gives as the platform's monotonic clock), or, without one, in virtual time, at once, so that tests take no time; timers due together complete in the order they were made. With nothing ready and no timer left while the top level waits, nothing can ever complete what it waits for: WL3009. An exception leaving an `async` function faults its task, and is thrown at each `await` of it, from where it was first thrown, a runtime error becoming an instance of its kind's class at each, anew. A task is observed once something awaits it, even before it faults; when the execution ends, the top level's exception, if it faulted, is reported, else the first fault that nothing observed or forgot, in the order they faulted, where it was first thrown. A task is written `task`, is equal only to itself, and cannot be a key (WL3001); `t.forget()` takes no arguments and gives `null`. `delay(milliseconds)` takes an integer of 0 or more (WL3001 otherwise). An `async` `toString()` gives a task, which is WL3001 where the text is written. From M0.11.2, a disposal one of whose methods is `async` is `async` as a whole: its methods run in turn in the frame of one call, which an `await` in one of them suspends, the next running once it returns, and the disposal's task completes, with `null`, after the last, or faults with the exception they threw (§3.7); `x.dispose()` then gives that task (§3.8); a `using` awaits it, dropping what it gives, in an `async` function and at the top level, which a `using` therefore makes `async`, and is WL3001 at its end in a function that is not `async`, before the disposal begins, so that the resource stays undisposed; and a `using` whose disposal is not `async` does not await, even in an `async` function. In the bytecode, `Await` (register: A, the destination, B, the value; stack: pops a value, pushes what the `await` gives) is allowed only in a function marked `IsAsync`, as both verifiers check; a resumed frame goes on after its `Await`, or throws there. From task combinators: `all`, `any` and `timeout` are members of `Task` alone (`P.all` is WL3006 on any other class), which a static member of `Task`'s own of their names would hide, and which, read without a call, are WL3001; `all` and `any` take one argument, an array (WL3001 otherwise), and `timeout` two (WL2006). A value that is not a task counts as complete, with itself: `Task.all` puts it among the results, `Task.any` completes with the first such value, or the first task already complete, in their order, and `Task.timeout` completes with it at once. `Task.all([])` completes with `[]`; `Task.any([])` is WL3001, since nothing could complete it. Each task a combinator is given is observed, so that a fault the combinator does not pass on is not reported at the execution's end; a combinator's own fault is, if nothing awaits it. `Task.all` faults with the first exception in time, or, among tasks already faulted when it is called, the first in their order. `Task.timeout`'s time is an integer of 0 or more (WL3001 otherwise), on the execution's clock, as `delay`'s is; its `TimeoutError`, a runtime error that no instruction raised, is thrown from the `await` it reaches, and reported, if nothing awaits it, at no place in the source. Combinators are tasks without a function, which the scheduler completes when the tasks they wait for do (`WebLingoScheduler`): the bytecode is unchanged | Proposed |
| Classes holding a value | From classes extending `Boolean`, `Integer`, `Number` and `String` (§3.7): these four are classes written in WebLingo, whose constructors give the instance its value, `class Number { constructor(value = 0.0) { hold(this, value); } }`, `hold` being theirs alone (`Hold` in the bytecode). `new` on one of them gives a plain value, the one given or its kind's first (`0`, `0.0`, `""`, `false`), an integer given to `Number` becoming a binary64, and a string given to `Integer`, `Number` or `Boolean` read as its literal, as `parse` reads it (from 14 November 2026); any other kind is WL3001, and more than one argument WL2006. A class extending one holds a value of that kind, `null` until its base's constructor gives one; a class holding a value that declares no constructor passes its arguments on to its base's (as every class does, §3.7). Arithmetic is on the values held: its result is an instance of its left operand's class, if that holds a value, else of its right's, holding the result, its constructor not run, with that operand's fields; a result not of that class's kind (an integer's class divided, giving a binary64) is a plain value (approved 14 November 2026). Unary `-` and `+` keep the class; comparisons and `==` compare the values held; `!` gives a plain Boolean; and a condition, `&&` and `||` take a Boolean an instance holds. An instance holding a value has its fields, then its class's methods, then its value's members (a string's `length`), and is indexed and visited by `foreach` as its value is. It is written `Celsius:21.5`, a string it holds quoted (`Name:"Anna"`), unless its class overrides `toString()`; it is not a key (WL3001). From `Function`: `Function` is written so too, `constructor(f = function () {})`, holding a function, a built-in or a bound method; `new Function(f)` is `f` itself, and `new Function()` an instance of `Function` holding a function that does nothing and gives `null`, written `Function:function`; an instance holding a function is called as that function, its class's methods and fields beside. `Task` and `Module` stay sealed, for now (§9, "Standard classes extended, parts", part 3), and `Class` did until 14 November 2026, `Bytes`, `BytesBuilder`, `DateTime`, `Json` and `Html` until 18 November 2026 (§3.7, "No sealed classes"), `Bytes` and `DateTime` then classes holding a value too, `new` on one of them making what `Bytes.from(…)` and `DateTime.utc(…)` make (`WebLingoMembers.TryNewBytes` and `TryNewDateTime`), and their classes' sources, as `Regex`'s, `class Bytes { constructor(...parts) { hold(this, new Bytes(...parts)); } }`. A built-in class's static methods the runtime gives, `Integer.parse`, `Bytes.from`, `DateTime.utc`, `Json.parse`, `Html.url` and `Task.all` among them, are a class's too when it extends that class (`WebLingoMembers.Reaches`), as a base's static members are; and binding a built-in class while a built-in class's source is bound, as a spread binds its own, leaves the source still the prelude's, `hold` its own (`WebLingoBinder.BindBuiltinClass`). From converting strings: `parse` is a static method of `Integer`, `Number` and `Boolean` alone, built in, as `setMethod` is, which a static member of their own of that name would hide (`String.parse` is WL3006); it takes one string (WL2006, WL3001). The string is its kind's literal as WebLingo writes one, a number's with `-` or `+` before it, and nothing around it, no space either: an integer's decimal digits, leading zeros allowed (`"+007"` is 7), WL3002 for one outside int64 and WL3001 for anything else; a number's digits, a fraction's with digits on both sides of the point, an exponent, or both, or `Infinity` or `NaN`, the binary64 nearest, WL3001 for one too large for a binary64 or anything else (`"10"` is `10.0`); a Boolean's `"true"` or `"false"`, exactly. A class holding a value reads a string so too (`new Celsius("21.5")`) | Proposed |
| Operators | From operators (§3.7): `operator` is not a keyword. `static operator +(a, b) { … }`, in a class or an `extend` block, declares an operator, a static method named `operator+`, which no program can name otherwise; a class may declare `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=`, each with two parameters (WL1001 otherwise), but not `==` or `!=` (WL1001), `==` staying identity for instances (§3.7, "Equality"), nor unary `-` and `+`. An operation calls one when either operand is an instance, or a collection, of a class that declares it, or whose base does, its left operand's class first, with its operands as arguments, as a call of the operation's instruction, whose result is the operation's; a value that is not an instance (`5`) has no operators. The VMs find one where their own arithmetic and comparisons give up, so that an operation on numbers costs nothing more | Proposed |
| Extensions | From `extend` blocks (§3.7): `extend` is a keyword. An `extend` names its class by a name or a dotted name, which is evaluated where its block begins, as a base is: it may name the block's classes and functions, and what is declared around the block, but not the block's variables (WL2001); a value that is not a class is WL3001 there. Extensions run in their order once the block's classes are made and their static fields set, each then setting its own static fields in their order. An extension's methods have `this`, but no `super` (WL2009); it cannot have a constructor (WL1001); `destructor()` is `dispose()`, as in a class (WL2011 for both); its names are its own's once (WL2002), its static members' apart from its methods'. A method it gives replaces the class's own of that name, in the class's disposal too; a static field it declares is the class's own, beside a base's of that name, `null` until its value is set, and replaces a static method of that name, as a static method replaces a static field. A class gathers its methods from its bases anew when they are next looked up after an extension has run, so that its instances and subclasses, made before or after, have the new ones. Once an `extend` has run, a value that is not an instance has its kind's class's methods, before its own members (so `extend Array { push(v) { … } }` replaces arrays' `push`), or `Object`'s if the program has not made that class; `null` has none, but `Object`'s `toString()` and `equal()`; and extending `Class` gives every class methods, after its static members (`P.describe()`). `write` writes a value that is not an instance as before, even when its class's `toString()` is extended, which only `x.toString()` calls; and so does an interpolation (§3.5, the owner's decision, 17 November 2026). In the bytecode, `Extend` (register: A, the class, B, an entry of the module's class table; stack: A, the entry, popping the class), an extension being an entry without a constructor | Proposed |
| Classes made while the program runs | From making classes (§3.7): a function declaration or expression may take `this` as its first parameter, its receiver, without a default; `this` anywhere else in a parameter list, a method's included, is WL1001. Such a function is bound as a method of no class: `super` in it is WL2009, and a property's `field` does not reach into it; its arity counts its receiver, so `describe(p)` for `describe(this, prefix)` is WL2006 at compile time. A call that has a target, a method's, a bound method's or `new`'s, gives a function with a receiver that target; any other gives it its first argument: a function value's, a static member's, and a field's (`p.g(q, 3)`, where the field `g` holds such a function, runs it on `q`). A function without a receiver given as a method runs without one. `new Class(name, base)` takes at most two arguments (WL2006): a name, a string or `null` for none (WL3001 otherwise), and a base, a class, or `null` for `Object` (WL3001 otherwise, a sealed one included). `removeMethod(name)` takes one argument (WL2006), a string that is a name (WL3001), and gives whether the class had a method of its own of that name, as `m.remove(k)` gives whether the map had the key; `"constructor"` removes its constructor, the class then acting as one that declares none, and `"destructor"` its `dispose`. A class so made has no constructor until `setMethod` gives it one: `new` on it passes its arguments on to the nearest base that declares one, as any class's does, and with none takes none (WL2006), and `super()` in a class extending it runs nothing. An unnamed class is written `class`, its instances `class {x: 1}`, and a message calls it "an unnamed class". `Point.setMethod(name, f)` takes two arguments (WL2006) and gives `null`; its name is a string that is a name, ASCII letters, digits and `_`, not first a digit (WL3001 otherwise), so that no program names a property's accessor or an operator so; `f` is a function (WL3001 otherwise, a built-in, a bound method and a class included); `"constructor"` gives the class its constructor, and `"destructor"` its `dispose`, as in a class. It is a built-in method of every class, a program's own or a built-in one (`Integer.setMethod("twice", f)` gives integers a method, as `extend` does), which a static member of its name hides, and which, read without a call, is WL3001, as `a.push` is. In the bytecode nothing is new: a function with a receiver is marked `HasThis`, as a method is, and `MakeClosure` may make one, the verifiers no longer requiring that it make no method; `new` on `Class` goes the way `new Integer(…)` goes. From names (§3.7): `x.__instanceOf` is a value's class, its kind's for one that is not an instance (`5.__instanceOf` is `Integer`), so that a program reading it gets every kind of value's class, and `null` for `null`; `Point.__className` is a class's name, `null` for an unnamed one, and `Point.__parent` its base, `null` for `Object`. They come before a program's own members of those names, which they therefore hide; `__className` and `__parent` are a class's only (`p.__className` is WL3006 on an instance). Assigning `__className` a string names or renames a class, and `null` unnames it (WL3001 otherwise); `__instanceOf` and `__parent` cannot be assigned (WL3001). A class's text, and its instances', follow its name as it is when written. From properties and constants given while the program runs (§3.7): `setProperty(name, getter, setter)` and `setStaticProperty(name, getter, setter)` take three arguments, and `setConstant(name, value)` two (WL2006), each giving `null`; a getter or a setter is a function or `null` (WL3001 otherwise), and `null` for both leaves the property with neither, as if it had none. A property so given is a class's methods `get:name` and `set:name`, as a declared one is, so that a class's own replaces its base's, reaches its subclasses, and is hidden by a method of its name, and a static one its static methods of those names. A constant is a static field of the class itself that cannot be assigned (WL3001), through the class or a subclass; `setConstant` replaces a static field or method of its name, a constant's value included. `static const name = value;` in a class or an `extend` block declares one, and so does `const name = value;` there, static as C#'s `const` is (the owner asked that `m.PI` read it, which a static member does through an instance), its value set as a static field's is, by the class's `setConstant`, called by a name no program can declare, so that a static member of the program's own named `setConstant` does not hide it; `static let` stays WL1001. Assigning a static method, through a class or a subclass, replaces it, on the class that has it, by a static field holding the value. From metaclasses (§3.7): `Class` is a class written in WebLingo, `class Class { constructor(name = null, base = null) { hold(this, [name, base]); } }`, which `new Class(…)` does not run; a class may extend it, and `Task` stays sealed. `new` on a class extending it makes a class, unnamed and extending `Object`, whose class is that metaclass, and runs the metaclass's constructor on it, whose `super(name, base)` names it and gives it its base, as `new Class(name, base)` does (WL3001 for a name that is not a string or `null`, a base that is not a class, a sealed one, or the class itself or one extending it); a metaclass without a constructor passes its arguments on to `Class`'s. A class a metaclass made has, after its own static members, its metaclass's methods, `setMethod` and the other built-in methods coming after both, so that a metaclass may replace them; its `__instanceOf` is its metaclass, and `is` tests it against the metaclass and its bases, so that it `is Class`; any other class's `__instanceOf` is `Class`. From one implementation (§3.7, 14 November 2026): a class's declaration is made as `new Class(name, base)` makes one, then given its constructor, its methods, a property's getter and setter among them, its static methods and its static fields, in that order, each by what `setMethod`, `setStaticProperty` and assigning a static member use (`WebLingoHeap`'s `TryMakeClass`, `GiveConstructor`, `TrySetMethod`, `TrySetStaticMethod` and `TryDeclareStatic`), and its static constants by `setConstant`; an `extend` block gives its members so too. A metaclass's base that is not a class, or is sealed, is therefore reported as a declared class's is (`… cannot be extended: only a class can.`, `The class Task cannot be extended.`); and a static method that replaces a constant, an extension's, leaves no constant behind, so that the static field assigned in its place may be assigned again | Proposed |
| Properties | From properties (§3.7): `property`, `get` and `set` are not keywords: `property name {` begins a property in a class or an `extend` block, `static` before it for a static one, and a method may still be named `property`. A property is a getter, `get { … }`, a setter, `set(value) { … }`, or both, each at most once: methods named `get:name` and `set:name`, which no program can name otherwise, so that a class gathers, inherits and replaces them as it does its methods. A property without either is WL1001, and so is a setter that does not take one parameter; an accessor cannot be `async`. A property's name is a member's, as a method's or a static field's is: a property and a method, or a static property and a static field, of one name in one class or extension are WL2002. Reading `x.name` finds an instance's fields, then its class's methods, then its static members, then its properties; a class's static members, then its static properties; and a value that is not an instance its kind's class's properties after its methods, as an `extend` gave them. Assigning `x.name = v` finds a property before a field, so that an instance never has a field of a property's name, and a class's static field before its static property; a property without a setter is WL3001, and reading one without a getter WL3006, `x?.name` too. Calling a property, `p.area()`, is WL3001: it is read, then what it gives called. An assignment's value is the value assigned, whatever the setter does with it: a setter's `return;` ends it, and `return value;` in one is WL1001. `field` is the property's field, an instance's for a property of instances and its class's for a static one, named `field:name`, which no program can name otherwise, `null` until assigned, in a function inside the accessor too; a variable named `field` declared in the accessor means that variable there, while one declared around the property does not reach into it; a static accessor of an `extend` block has no `field` (WL2001). A static property is reached through its class, not through an instance. `super.name` reads the base's property through its getter. In the bytecode nothing is new: `GetMember`, `SetMember` and `GetSuper` call a getter or a setter as a call of their instruction, on the property's target, its result where the instruction's goes, a setter's (the value) to the value's register on the register VM, and dropped on the stack VM for an assignment whose value is not kept | Proposed |
| Modules | From static imports and exports (§3.9): `import` and `export` are keywords, and `from` one only in an import. An import takes a module's instance, `import db from "lib/db.wlg";`, names it exports, `import { f, g } from …;`, or both, `import db, { f } from …;`; its names are constants (WL2003), a name it takes read as the module's export each time it is read. Imports and exports stand at a file's top level (WL1001 elsewhere), and `return` does not (WL2008). A file sees the built-in classes, but none of another file's names. A file an import names is found by the host, compiled with the program, and run as an `async` function of its own, once: its instance is made where its function begins, before its imports run, so that a cycle gets it as it is so far, and an export read before its module has set it is WL2004; the program's built-in classes are made before its imports run. A file that cannot be found is WL2015, at its path. A module's instance is written `module lib/db.wlg`, is a `Module`, cannot be assigned (WL3001), and has no member it does not export (WL3006); a module that imports itself gets its own instance. The CLI finds a file from the importing file's folder, adding `.wlg` to a path without an extension, and names it by its path from the current folder, with `/` between its folders, the program's own by the path it was given; diagnostics and runtime errors name the file they are in (`lib/db.wlg(4,12)`). In the bytecode, a module holds a table of its files (`WebLingoUnitInfo`: a name, a function, and the names it exports), the program's first; `RegisterUnit` (register: A, a file, B, its function's closure; stack: pops it), `MakeModule` (register: A, the destination, B, the file, C, the first of D registers holding its exports' cells; stack: A, the file, B, the count, popped) and `Import` (register: A, the destination, B, the file; stack: A, the file, pushing the instance, or the task of running it, which the code then awaits); and each function names its file, if it is not the program's. From standard modules: an import whose path begins with `std:` names a standard module, which the compiler makes itself, never from a file: `std:system`, whose exports are the host's built-ins (`write`, `writeLn` and `delay` under the CLI), its instance made where the program begins, before its imports run; any other is WL2015. Every module still sees the built-ins without importing `std:system`. In the bytecode, a standard module is a file without a function, its name beginning `std:`, its `MakeModule` given the built-ins' values. From parameters: `param` is a keyword. A module's `param` statements come first, before its other statements, imports included; `param` anywhere else is WL1001. Each is a constant of the module (WL2003 when assigned), its default an expression bound before it is declared, in the module's scope, evaluated when its import leaves it out; its value is set where the module's function begins, after its instance is made and before its imports run, so that an import may give its own module's parameters on (`import box from "box" { size: n * 10 };`). An import's values are written as a map literal's entries after its path, each key a name or a string, a key given twice being WL2002; a module with parameters is always given a map, `{}` when none are written. Two imports give the same values when each parameter's are equal, as `==` compares them, a value left out counting as different from one given, even its default. The program's own file may declare parameters, which its host gives values when it starts, each a string (`weblingo run script.wlg name=value`), its entry point taking them as one map, as a module's function does; a value for none of them, or a parameter without a default left out, is WL2016, which ends the execution as it starts (exit 3). The CLI takes each `name=value` after the script's path, the name a name, given once, the value everything after the first `=`; an argument that is not one is a usage error (exit 2), and `check` takes none. A host gives the values as `WebLingoExecutionContext.Parameters`, and a module lists the program's own parameters (`Parameters`), its entry point then taking one parameter, as both verifiers check. When it runs, an import's values are checked again: a value that is not a map is WL3001, and a key that is not one of the module's parameters, or a parameter without a default left out, WL2016. In the bytecode, a file's entry of the table of files lists its parameters, each a name and whether it has a default, and its function then takes one parameter, the map of their values, which its code reads with `has` and indexing; `Import` takes the map (register: C, its register, with D 1; stack: B 1, the map popped), which a file with parameters is always given, as both verifiers check, and the VMs keep each file's instance for each set of values (`WebLingoModuleTable`). From dynamic imports: the compiler knows `Module.import(path)` and `Module.import(path, values)` when `Module` is the built-in class, a program's own `Module` being called as any class is; it takes one or two arguments (WL2006 at compile time). A member's name after a point may be any word, a keyword included (`Module.import`, `a.if`), while a declaration's name may not (`static import() { … }` is WL1001). The path is a string (WL3001 otherwise), found from the importing file's folder by the host's loader, as a static import's is; one it cannot find, or a host without a loader, is WL2015, when it runs. A file the program has already is not compiled again: the import gives its instance for those values, as a task already complete, or runs it, its call giving the task. Any other file is compiled then, with the files it imports, as an import: a program of its own, whose top level takes the values, imports the file with them, and gives its instance; a file that does not compile is a runtime error of its first diagnostic's code and message, which a catch may catch. The import is then joined to the running program: its functions, constants, built-ins and classes added to the program's, and its instructions' indices moved to where they now are; its built-in classes are the program's where the program has made them, so that `x is Object` holds across them, and else its own, which become the program's; and a file it imports that the program has is the program's, so that every file runs once for each set of values, whichever way it is imported. Its values are checked when it runs, as a static import's are at compile time (WL3001, WL2016); a file with parameters given none is given an empty map. The task completes with the instance once the file has run, or faults with the exception its top level throws. In the bytecode, `ImportDynamic` (register: A, the destination, B, the path, C, the values with D 1; stack: A 1 with values, popping them and the path, and pushing the task); a module compiled as an import is marked `IsImport`, its entry point taking one parameter, the values, as both verifiers check; and a VM given a loader (`IWebLingoModuleLoader`, `IWebLingoStackModuleLoader`, `WebLingo.Bytecode`), which the compiler implements (`WebLingoModuleLoader`) and the CLI gives its VM, joins such a module itself | Proposed |
| `foreach` | From M0.9.4 (§3.5, §3.6): `foreach` is a keyword, and `in` is one only in a `foreach`'s parentheses; `const` or `let` in place of `var`, or another name in place of `in`, is reported and read as meant, for one diagnostic. The collection is evaluated once, before the loop's variables exist, so `foreach (var x in x)` loops over an outer `x`. The loop keeps the collection and its position in two hidden locals, followed by its variables, which a closure captures each in a new cell on each iteration. A string is visited by Unicode scalar value. A collection that cannot be looped over is WL3001, at the collection | Proposed |
| Members and indexing in the bytecode | Register: `GetMember`, `CallMethod` (its target and its arguments in consecutive registers), `GetIndex`, `SetIndex`, `NewArray` (its elements in consecutive registers) and `NewMap` (each key, then its value, in consecutive registers); stack: `GetMember`, `CallMethod`, `GetIndex`, `StoreIndex`, which may push the value back, a `GetIndex` operand (register D, stack A) being 1 for `?[`, `NewArray` and `NewMap`, and from M0.9.5 `NewSet` (its values in consecutive registers, or on the stack). From M0.10.1: `MakeClass` (a class of the module's class table, its methods' closures made as their captures say, from M0.10.2 with the class it extends: register C if D is 1, stack popped if B is 1), `GetSuper` (from M0.10.2: a class, `this` and a name, giving the base's method or constructor bound to `this`), `New` (register: the class and the arguments as `Call` takes them; stack: the class below the arguments) and `SetMember` (stack: which may push the value back), a `GetMember` operand (register D, stack B) being 1 for `?.`; a module holds a class table, each class with its constructor and its methods, and a function a flag, `HasThis`, for a method, which both verifiers check: a class's functions are methods, and `MakeClosure` makes no method. From M0.9.4, `ForEachNext` (register: the exit, the collection's register, with the position and the items in the registers after it, and the item count; stack: the exit and the collection's local, with `ForEachNextEntry` for a key and a value), which the verifiers treat as a conditional jump whose way out writes the position only. From M0.10.3, `Dispose` (register: A, the resource's register; stack: the resource, popped), a `using`'s disposal, which does nothing for `null`, and which the verifiers treat as a read; each VM runs an instance's dispose methods in turn in the frame of one call, the first as that call's, each next one as the last returns. From default values, `JumpIfGiven` (register: A, a parameter's register, B, the target; stack: A, the target, B, the parameter's local), which jumps when the caller gave the parameter, and each function's `OptionalCount`, how many of its last parameters may be left out. From typed catches, `IsInstance` (register: A, the destination, B, the value, C, the class; stack: pops the class, then the value, and pushes a Boolean), and a module names its kinds of values' classes, by `WebLingoValueClass` (`WebLingo.Core`), and its runtime errors' classes, by their codes. From M0.10.7, `ToText` (register: A, the destination, and B; stack: pops a value, pushes its text), and a module names its `Object` class, whose `toString()` a VM keeps as every value's. From M0.10.6, `Throw` (register A; stack: popped) and `Rethrow` (register A, the exception, and B, where it was first thrown, an int64 naming a function and an instruction; stack: both popped), and each function's handler table (§5.4's table-driven regions): ranges of its code, innermost first, each with its handler's instruction and the two registers, or locals, its handler receives the exception and its origin in, and on the stack VM the stack's depth there. The verifiers treat every instruction of a range as able to go to its handler, with what is written before it. A jump out of a protected range (`break`, `continue`, `return`) runs the `finally` bodies and disposals it leaves, copied there, outside the ranges they belong to; and a module names its `Error` class, which a VM keeps when it makes it. From M0.10.4, a module's class table gives each class its static methods, functions without `HasThis`, and its static fields' names, which the verifiers check; `MakeClass` makes the static methods' closures too, and `GetMember`, `SetMember` and `CallMethod` on a class reach its static members. From M0.11.1, `Await`, and each function's `IsAsync` (see "Asynchrony"); and from `extend` blocks, `Extend` (see "Extensions"). A member's name is a string constant, which each VM resolves to a member once, when it loads the module; the semantics are `WebLingoMembers`' (`WebLingo.Runtime`), the same for both VMs | Proposed |
| Use before declaration | A name used before its declaration, or in its own initializer, means the outer declaration, or is undefined (WL2001, at compile time) if there is none (§3.1) | Approved |
| Evaluation order | Left to right: operands, then arguments; an assignment evaluates its value before it stores it | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Instruction model | An in-memory register instruction model, not WLBC (§5.3, §8.4): an operation and up to four integer operands A to D, unused ones 0 (`WebLingoBytecodeContracts.cs`); a module holds its constants (int64 and binary64, kept as IEEE bits), the built-ins it imports by name and arity, and one function, with a source span per instruction and the names of its locals for runtime messages | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| WLBC's layout | From M0.13.1 (§5.3, "As built"): fixed-size fields little-endian. A 32-byte header: `WLBC`, the major and minor version (0 and 1), the VM (1 register, 2 stack), flags (0), the sections' count, the directory's offset and the file's length. A directory of 32-byte entries: the section's kind, 0, its offset, its length, its CRC-32 (IEEE 802.3's, as ZIP's) and 0. Counts and lengths as unsigned LEB128; every other integer, an operand, an index, an arity or an int64 constant, as signed LEB128; a binary64 as its 8 bytes; a string as its UTF-8 bytes' count and the bytes, a lone surrogate as its three bytes (WTF-8). The sections: 1 the module, 2 constants, 3 functions (the entry point first; each its counts, flags, source, native call, captures and instruction count), 4 code (each function's instructions, an operation's byte and its operands, four for the register VM and two for the stack VM, unused ones 0), 5 classes and units, 6 handlers, 7 source positions (each function's spans and its locals' names, then a table for each file of the line and column of each position its spans start at, the program's own first). The writer writes the same bytes for the same module | Proposed |
| Registers | A function's locals take registers 0 to n − 1 and its temporaries the registers above; at most 65535 registers (§5.3), more is a compile-time error (WL4002) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Operations | `LoadConstant`, `Move`, `Negate`, `Plus`, `Add`, `Subtract`, `Multiply`, `Divide`, `CallBuiltin` (arguments in consecutive registers, §5.4), `Jump`, `Return`; from M0.7.2, `LoadNull`, `LoadBoolean`, `Not`, `Equal`, `NotEqual`, `Less`, `LessOrEqual`, `Greater`, `GreaterOrEqual`, `JumpIfFalse` and `JumpIfTrue` (whose condition must be a Boolean, WL3001), and `CheckBoolean` (the right operand of `&&` and `||`); from M0.7.6, `NewCell`, `BoxRegister`, `CopyCell`, `LoadCell`, `StoreCell`, `LoadCapture`, `StoreCapture`, `MakeClosure`, `LoadBuiltin`, `Call` and `ReturnValue`, and a module holds its functions besides its entry point, each with its parameter count and its captures | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Verification | Besides §5.5: unused operands must be 0; no path may run off the end of the code; every register, local or temporary, must be written before it is read on every path, which is what keeps a variable from being read before it has a value (§3.1); a function whose check needs more than 16 million words of state or 100 million steps is rejected rather than verified slowly. The bounds are arbitrary, and are constants that are easy to change (`WebLingoBytecodeVerifier.MaxStateWords`, `WebLingoBytecodeVerifier.MaxSteps`), as is the register limit (`WebLingoFunction.MaxRegisters`) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Compiler output | The compiler verifies what it generates, and reports a module that fails as an internal error (WL4001), with no module | Proposed |
| Runtime values | `WebLingoValue`, a kind and a 64-bit payload, 16 bytes (§4.1's target, met): null, Boolean, int64 and binary64 (§8.4); scripts write `null`, `true` and `false` from M0.7.2 | Proposed |
| Heap | Strings are the first objects, then closures, cells and, from M0.9.2, arrays, and from M0.9.3 maps, whose units are their element or entry count, which grows and shrinks with them, within the budget: a value of kind String holds a handle into its execution's heap (`WebLingoHeap`), never a .NET reference (§4.1); the heap is the execution's own (§4.3), and is collected by WebLingo's nonmoving mark-and-sweep (§4.2) from its roots, the VM's registers and constants. A string's text is kept as a .NET string for now (UTF-16; §3.3's UTF-8 representation is still proposed). Its size is counted in units (a string's UTF-16 length, plus 16 per object): a collection runs at twice what was live after the last one, at least 1M units, and the budget is 64M units (`WebLingoResourceBudget.MaxHeapUnits`), beyond which an allocation is WL3003; all of these are constants that are easy to change | Proposed |
| String operations | `+` of two strings concatenates them; `==` and `!=` compare their texts, code unit by code unit; a string is written as its own text | Approved (12 November 2026, §3.5), but for the comparison, which is proposed |
| Mixed arithmetic | An int64 meeting a binary64 is converted to binary64 (`1 + 0.5`, and `10 / 4 + 1` is 3.5) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Binary64 results | IEEE 754 throughout: `1 / 0` is `Infinity`, `-1 / 0` is `-Infinity`, `0 / 0` is `NaN`; only integer arithmetic raises an error (WL3002). `Infinity` and `NaN` are written as keywords (see "Binary64 literals") | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Invalid operands | Arithmetic on anything but numbers is WL3001; once strings exist, `+` also concatenates them | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| `write` | `write(value)` writes the value as text, with no line break; `writeLn(value)` writes it and a line break (LF); each takes one argument and gives `null`; the host (the CLI) registers them, and they are implemented once in `WebLingo.StdLib`. A line break can also be written as text, once string literals exist | Approved (12 November 2026); `std:console`'s since 20 November 2026 ("The console", below) |
| Text of a value | `null`, `true`, `false`; an int64 in decimal digits; a binary64 as the shortest decimal that reads back as the same number, always with a decimal point or an exponent (`5.0`, `3.5`, `0.1`, `-0.0`, `1E+21`), or `NaN`, `Infinity`, `-Infinity`; never culture-dependent (`WebLingoValueFormatter`) | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| Linking | The VM verifies a module, then links its imports to the host's built-ins by name and arity; a missing one stops the module before it runs (WL2001) | Proposed |
| Runtime errors | The first runtime error stops the execution, and is reported with the source span of the instruction that raised it; output printed before it stays | Proposed |
| Budgets and cancellation | An execution has an instruction budget (exceeded: WL3003) and a register budget (a module needing more is not run: WL3003); the VM looks at the host's cancellation signal every 1024 instructions (WL3004); a WebLingo-owned signal, not .NET's `CancellationToken` (WL-P007) | Proposed |
| Built-in failures | A built-in that fails, even by throwing, is a runtime error (WL3001) | Proposed |
| CLI | `weblingo run <file>` compiles and runs; `weblingo check <file>` compiles and reports, without running; `weblingo --version` prints `WebLingo M0.6 (pre-alpha)`, `WebLingo 0.20.0-pre-alpha` from 18 November 2026 ("Versions", the register). Diagnostics go to standard error as `file(line,column): ERROR CODE (ExceptionClass): message` (approved, 19 November 2026). Use the class of the thrown `Error` instance when available, or the built-in class assigned to the code. Omit the parentheses for a diagnostic without an exception class. Display `ERROR` in bold on terminals that support it and as plain text when redirected.
