Exit codes, each with one meaning: 0 success, 1 compilation errors, 2 a usage error, 3 a runtime error, 4 a file that cannot be read. `write`'s lines end in LF on every platform | Approved (12 November 2026) |
| WLBC on the command line | From M0.13.2 (§5.3): `weblingo compile [--register|--stack] [--scheme …] <file.wlg>` writes `<file>.wlbr` beside it for the register VM or `<file>.wlbs` for the stack VM. Source defaults to the register VM. `weblingo.config` can choose a different default, and `--register` and `--stack` override it, `--vm register` and `--vm stack` their aliases (the owner's request, 16 November 2026), and prints nothing; `weblingo run` and `check` accept `.wlbr`, `.wlbs` and legacy `.wlbc` files. The header selects the VM and must agree with any VM-specific extension and any configured VM, and `check` reads and verifies; `weblingo disasm <file.wlbc>` writes its bytecode as text (`WebLingoDisassembler`, `WebLingoStackDisassembler`). A `.wlbc` names its files from the program's folder, the program's own by its name alone, so that it holds none of the host's paths; `run` names them again from the `.wlbc`'s folder, the program's own as the file beside it, and loads the native modules it names, as its compiler had. A `.wlbc` that cannot be used (damaged, of another version, another VM's, or failing verification) is `<file>: ERROR WL4001: …`, exit code 1, as compilation errors are; one that cannot be written, exit code 4, as a file that cannot be read. `wasp` has none of this (§5.1) | Proposed |
| WLHI's HTTP contracts | From M0.14.1 (§6.7): a request's path is as its host decoded it, from `/`, without the query; its query is as sent, after the `?`, not decoded, empty without one; its method as sent. A response's status is 200 until set, 100 to 999; writing its body commits it, and so does completing it, its status and headers then fixed. A header's name is an HTTP token (RFC 9110, section 5.6.2), compared without regard to ASCII case, and may have several values, in order, joined by `", "` when read as one; a header's value is visible ASCII, spaces and tabs only, so that no CR or LF can end it early (header injection), and nothing depends on an encoding; a change that breaks a rule, or is made to fixed headers, is refused, and says why. Bodies are bytes, read and written synchronously until M0.14.7. From M0.14.7.1, `Abort()` ends a response unfinished, as a broken connection would, so that its client sees its body incomplete. From M0.14.7.4, a request's body is read as it arrives, `ReadAsync` calling back once it has read, and `Read`, which waits, read only on a host's own thread | Proposed |
| The host's core | From M0.14.2 (§6.12): an application's routes are made when it loads, from its root's files, a path compared as it is, its case included, on every platform; `index.wasp` is its folder's path with and without its closing `/`; never routed are files and folders whose names start with `.`, `.wlbr`, `.wlbs` and legacy `.wlbc` files, and at the root `wasp.config`, `weblingo.config` and IIS's `web.config`, whatever their names' case, since Windows finds them so, nor a page or a `.wlg` by its own name, nor one whose path has a character no portable file name has, `\`, `:` or a control character, which its includes and imports, named from it, could not be found from (17 November 2026; until then, on Linux, a route that always failed); a page or a `.wlg` wins over a file of the same path. A page is compiled when first requested, not when the application loads, since an include or a layout is a `.wasp` too, which does not compile alone; once on each worker, each worker's pages its own, so that no state shared between workers changes and nothing needs a lock (WL-P013); kept, with its components, each compiled when first rendered, for the generation's life. Its includes, layouts, components and imports are found from the root for a path starting with `/`, from the including file's folder otherwise, never outside the root, and named from the root, so that an error names no host path. Its output is written as UTF-8, its `Content-Type` `text/html; charset=utf-8` unless it set another (from M0.14.7.1, "Output sent as it is written"); a page that does not compile or fails is a 500, every error a line `file(line,column): error CODE: message`, escaped, in development only; a path without a route is a 404 page. A request runs on the site's configured VM (register by default), on the worker its host gives, numbered from 0; the host gives the environment, its built-ins and its native modules | Proposed |
| Executions that wait without a thread | From M0.14.7.2 (§6.6): `WebLingoExecutionContext.Suspends` has a VM return, rather than wait on its worker's thread, where its scheduler would wait, for an operation off the worker or a timer on the host's clock, a timer due already, or in virtual time, completing as before; its result `Waiting`, neither success nor failure, with `WaitMilliseconds`, the time until its next timer, -1 for none; the VM keeps the execution, its frames, its heap and its count of instructions, nothing being on its stack then, and `Resume()`, on both VMs (`IWebLingoVirtualMachine.Resume`), goes on through its scheduler, which runs the work posted, looks at the cancellation signal, and may wait again; its heap's host objects are released only once it ends. Without `Suspends`, a VM waits as before, so that `weblingo` and `wasp run` are unchanged. `WebLingoApplication.Handle` takes the request's own scheduler host, and with one gives a request that waits as the outcome `Waiting`, whose `Resume` gives its next outcome. The ASP.NET Core adapter's `WebLingoWorkerPool.Serve` gives each request a scheduler host of its own, its blocking work on .NET's thread pool and its posted work held for its worker, whose post queues the request's resumption on a queue of that worker's own, taken before the queue of new work every worker shares, as are a timer's at its time and the client's going; a worker with 64 requests waiting takes no new work until one ends; disposing of the pool cancels the requests that wait. A client that has gone ends its request's serving without an error. Tried with `wasp serve` on Windows: 12 pages each awaiting a 500-millisecond timer, on 8 workers, all ended by 513 milliseconds, a quick page asked for meanwhile answered at once | Proposed |
| A request's body | From M0.14.7.4 (§6.12, "Streaming"): the ASP.NET Core adapter no longer reads a request's body before its page runs, but gives it as it arrives (`IWebLingoBodyReader.ReadAsync`), so that a page that reads nothing of it never waits for it. `Request`'s `text()` and `form()` each give a task (`WebLingoScheduler.TryWaitForHost`, its result made on the worker) that reads the whole body, off the worker, once, then keeps it, so that both, and either again, give the same. `text()` decodes it in the charset its `Content-Type` names, one the portable core's own codec reads (`WebLingoTextCodec`, without .NET's `System.Text.Encoding`, which is adapter-only: UTF-8, UTF-16, little-endian or big-endian, US-ASCII and ISO-8859-1, by the web's names for them, `latin1` among them), UTF-8 if it names none, what is no character U+FFFD, and another charset WL3001. Its fields, and a query's, are decoded by the core too, a `%` without two hexadecimal digits after it kept as it is. The body's reading hands its end over to the worker through the scheduler host's `Post`, the core taking no lock. `form()` reads only a body sent as `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, whatever its charset parameter, WL3001 otherwise, its fields decoded as a query's are, UTF-8, a name given twice having its last value, a name without `=` an empty string. A body that cannot be read, its client gone, faults the task, WL3001. From M0.14.7.5, `read()` gives a task of the body's next piece, bytes, as it arrives, up to 16 KiB, or `null` at its end; a body read in pieces cannot then be read whole (WL3001), and one read whole is given by `read()` as one piece, then `null`; a `read()` before the last one's piece has come is WL3001. WebLingo sets no limit on a body's size: the web server's does (Kestrel's 30 MB by default). Tried with `wasp serve` on Windows: a form posted to a page, and 5 MB of text posted to a handler | Proposed |
| Bytes | From M0.14.7.5 (§3.2, "Bytes"): a kind of value, `WebLingoValueKind.Bytes`, its heap object a byte array nothing changes after it is made, a unit for every eight bytes; its class, `Bytes`, the last but one of the classes of values, so that a module compiled before it still loads (the verifier takes 12 to 14 of them), `new Bytes(value, charset)` making what `Bytes.from(value, charset)` makes, and a class extending it holding bytes (from 18 November 2026, §3.7, "No sealed classes"; sealed until then, `new Bytes()` WL3001), and extended as any other (`extend Bytes { … }`). `b.length`; `b[i]`, an integer, `b?[i]` `null` outside them, WL3005 with `[`; `b[i] = v` WL3001; `b.slice(start, end)`, `end` left out the end, WL3005 for a range not within them; `b.text(charset)`; `Bytes.from(value, charset)`, of a string, encoded, an array of integers from 0 to 255, or bytes, which are themselves, a charset only with a string; `Bytes.concat(parts)`, of an array of bytes and integers from 0 to 255, each copied once; and `Bytes.fixedTimeEqual(a, b)`, which compares every byte, never stopping where they differ, its time given away only by their lengths, which are not secret. Charsets are the core's codec's (`WebLingoTextCodec`), UTF-8 by default. `==` compares contents; bytes are no key of a map, nor a value of a set, WL3001, and not visited by `foreach`, nor joined by `+`, for now. `write` writes `Bytes:[104, 105]`, in a collection too. `BytesBuilder` is native (18 November 2026, "Native BytesBuilder"), a kind of value of its own, as bytes are (`WebLingoValueKind.BytesBuilder`), in place of a class written in WebLingo (`WebLingoPrelude`), which kept an array of pieces and joined them with `Bytes.concat`: a buffer of bytes that doubles as it must, from 64 bytes, charged a unit for every eight bytes of it as it grows, within the budget (WL3003), and given back whole when collected; `add(value)`, of bytes, an integer from 0 to 255, or a string, encoded as UTF-8, at its end, giving the builder, anything else WL3001 (`'add' takes bytes, an integer from 0 to 255, or a string, not …`, where the class written in WebLingo named `concat`); its `length`; and `toBytes()`, its bytes so far copied into new bytes, which no later add changes, so that building n bytes from any number of pieces costs O(n). Its text is its bytes so far, `BytesBuilder:[104, 105]`; `new BytesBuilder()` makes one, and takes no argument (WL2006); and a class extending it holds a builder, from 18 November 2026 (§3.7, "No sealed classes"), as a class could extend the one written in WebLingo, its class sealed in between (WL3001). Measured against that one: §5.6, "Bytes built from pieces". Hexadecimal and Base64 (17 November 2026), by the runtime's own codec, `WebLingoBinaryText`, which `std:crypto` writes its passwords' hashes with too: `b.toHex()` two lower-case digits for each byte, and `Bytes.fromHex(text)` two digits for each, in either case; `b.toBase64()` RFC 4648's standard alphabet, padded with `=`, and `b.toBase64(true)` its URL-safe one, `-` and `_`, unpadded, since a URL would escape `=`; `Bytes.fromBase64(text)` the standard alphabet, its padding required, and `Bytes.fromBase64(text, true)` the URL-safe one, padded or not; `""` no bytes; nothing else, white space included, the text then WL3001, quoted in the message, as `Integer.parse`'s is; the argument a Boolean, anything else WL3001 | Proposed |
| Dates and times | From 16 November 2026 (§3.2, "Dates and times"): a kind of value, `WebLingoValueKind.DateTime`, held in the value itself, no heap object, its instant in milliseconds since 1970-01-01T00:00:00Z and its offset in minutes sharing its 64 bits; its class, `DateTime`, the last of the classes of values, after `BytesBuilder`, so that a module compiled before it still loads (the verifier takes 12 to 15 of them), sealed, so that `new DateTime()` is WL3001, but extended as any other. Its instants and its local times are within the years 1 to 9999, its offsets within 14 hours, as .NET's `DateTimeOffset`'s are, WL3001 beyond them. Made by `DateTime.now()`, the wall clock's instant with the server's offset at it; `DateTime.utcNow()`; `DateTime.local(year, month, day, hour, minute, second, millisecond)`, the server's zone's, and `DateTime.utc(…)`, those after the day optional, 0 when left out, each part an integer within its range (WL3001), months and days from 1, a time the zone skips or has twice taking its standard offset; `DateTime.fromUnixMilliseconds(ms)`, UTC; and `DateTime.parse(text)`, ISO 8601 as a date's text writes it, `yyyy-MM-ddTHH:mm:ss.fff` with `Z` or an offset, the seconds and the fraction optional, or without a zone, the server's time, or a date alone, its midnight, and `DateTime.parse(text, format)`, by .NET's format, its zone its `z`, `zz`, `zzz` or `K`, the server's time without one, UTC where `K` finds none. Its properties `year`, `month`, `day`, `hour`, `minute`, `second`, `millisecond`, its time on its own clocks, `dayOfWeek`, 0 for Sunday, `dayOfYear`, `offset`, in minutes, and `unixMilliseconds`; its methods `addYears(n)` and `addMonths(n)`, integers, the day kept, or the month's last, `addDays(n)`, `addHours(n)`, `addMinutes(n)`, `addSeconds(n)` and `addMilliseconds(n)`, integers or numbers, rounded to the millisecond, half away from zero, each keeping the offset, as .NET does; `toUtc()` and `toLocal()`, the same instant at UTC's offset or at the server's zone's; and `format(format)`. `==` compares instants, whatever their offsets, as .NET's `DateTimeOffset` does, and so do `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=`; `b - a` is an integer, milliseconds; `+` and the other arithmetic are WL3001; dates are no key of a map, nor a value of a set (WL3001). Its text, `write`'s, `toString()`'s and `<%= %>`'s, is `DateTime:` and ISO 8601's, its milliseconds only when it has any, `Z` for an offset of 0; its formats are .NET's, standard and custom, with the invariant culture, `d.format("q")` WL3001 for one .NET refuses. Without a host's wall clock, `now()` and `utcNow()` are WL3001; without its zone, the server's is UTC | Proposed |
| Assignment's operators and choosing a value | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): the parser reads `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `%=`, `??=`, `++`, `--`, `?`, `??` and an `if` in an expression's place; the binder writes them with two expressions of its own, a conditional and a sequence, and with temporaries, variables of names no program can write, which hold an element's target and index, a member's target, a postfix's old value and a `??`'s left operand, so that each is evaluated once; both VMs run them with their jumps, no instruction of their own added. `?[` written together is an optional index, never a conditional's | Proposed |
| `switch` | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): `switch`, `case` and `default` are words of their own only where a `switch` stands, so that a program's names are left as they are. The statement is the `if`s it means, its value held once, so that `break` and `continue` in a case act on the loop around it, as in an `if`; the expression the conditionals it means, as tight as C#'s, after the unary operators and before `*`, needing a `_ => …` arm (WL1001 without one). A class's pattern stands alone in its case or arm (WL1001 beside others); a name before an arm's `=>` is a value, not an arrow function's parameter | Proposed |
| Decimals | From 16 November 2026 (§3.2): a kind of value, `WebLingoValueKind.Decimal`, .NET's `decimal` in a heap object, immutable; its class, `Decimal`, the last of the classes of values (the verifier takes 12 to 16 of them), a class of values as `Number` is: `new Decimal(x)` of an integer, exactly, of a number, converted, or of a string, read as `Decimal.parse` reads it, a sign, digits and a point; `new Number(d)` and `new Integer(d)`, the latter only of a whole decimal within 64 bits (WL3001 otherwise); a class may extend it. A literal, `1.5m`, its digits within a decimal's range (WL1004 beyond), is a constant of a kind of its own, its digits as text, which a VM reads when it loads the module. `+`, `-`, `*`, `/` and `%` of two decimals, or of a decimal and an integer, give a decimal; with a binary64, WL3001; a result beyond a decimal's range WL3002; a division or remainder by 0 WL3013. `==` compares amounts, `1m == 1` true, a decimal never equal to a binary64; `<` and the others order them, with an integer too. Its text is its fewest digits and `m`, never in an exponent's form; an interpolation formats it by .NET's numeric formats. Decimals are no key of a map | Proposed |
| Time zones | From 16 November 2026 (§3.2, "Time zones"): `d.toZone(name)`, the same instant at the zone's offset then, and `DateTime.inZone(name, year, month, day, hour, minute, second, millisecond)`, 4 to 8 arguments, those after the day optional, as `DateTime.local(…)` but in the zone named, a time the zone skips or has twice taking its standard offset, as the server's zone's does. A zone is found through the host's, `IWebLingoTimeZone.Find(name)`, `DotNetTimeZone` asking .NET's `TimeZoneInfo.TryFindSystemTimeZoneById`, Windows' zones on Windows, IANA's names converted to them by ICU, the operating system's elsewhere. A name is looked for only when it is written as IANA writes them, ASCII letters, digits, `_`, `-` and `+`, its parts between single `/`, at most 64 characters, so that Windows' own names, with spaces, are not, alike on every server, and no name reaches the platform as a path; a name written otherwise, or one the platform does not know, a name that is not a string, and a host that gives no zone are WL3001, the last as `now()` is without the host's clock. A date holds its offset, not its zone: one moved by its `add…` methods keeps its offset, as before, though the zone's clocks change meanwhile, `toZone` again giving the zone's offset then | Proposed |
| Rest parameters | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): `...name`, `...` a token of its own, the last parameter of a function, a method, a constructor, an arrow function, in parentheses, `(...xs) => …`, or a function with a receiver, after any with defaults; it has no default (WL1001), and no parameter follows it (WL1001). Each call makes it a new array. A call gives at least the parameters before it but those with defaults, WL2006 otherwise, `'f' takes at least 1 argument, not 0.`, at compile time for a function called by its name, as for any other. In either VM's bytecode, a function with one counts only the parameters before it, says that it has one, `HasRest`, written in WLBC as its flags' 16, and takes it into the local after them by its first instruction, `TakeRest`, and nowhere else (the verifiers); a call that gives more arguments than the parameters, which the VMs check already, gathers those after them into an array before the frame is made, held as a root until `TakeRest` takes it, and `TakeRest` without them makes an empty one, so that a call of any function takes the path it took before (the benchmark's `objects` and `calls` against `main`'s, alternately, five rounds, and the stack VM's `calls` seven more, within the machine's noise, where a first build, which looked for a rest parameter on every call, made the stack VM's `objects` 2.5 percent slower); a native member has none | Proposed |
| Spreading | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): `...values`, with `...` the token rest parameters have, an argument of any call, a method's, `new`'s and `super(…)`'s included, or an element of an array's or a set's literal, or an entry of a map's; anywhere else WL1001, and in `Module.import(…)` and a component's render, whose arguments the compiler reads as written, too. Elements and arguments are evaluated in order, spread or not. A value spreads as `foreach` visits it: an array, a set, a string, or an instance by its `iterate()`, anything else WL3001; into a map, a map's entries, a later key's value replacing an earlier one's, anything else WL3001. A call with a spread argument has its arity checked when it runs (WL2006), and gives at most 65,535 arguments (WL3001). As built: the binder makes a literal with spread elements a call of a class written in WebLingo, under a name no program can write, whose static `array`, `set` and `map` visit their parts, the plain elements grouped in literals of their own, its functions native, so that an error in one is reported where the spread is; and a call's spread arguments one such array, which three new instructions of each VM, `CallSpread`, `CallMethodSpread` and `NewSpread`, put where the call takes its arguments, from the array's register on, or on the stack in its place, the frame's registers or slots grown where they are too few, as the stack VM grows them for an operator's call, then running the call's own instruction's code on a rewritten instruction held in a field, so that the dispatch method gets no new locals; values put beyond the function's own registers or slots stay in its frame's array until something else is put there, which keeps them from being collected meanwhile, as an operator's operands are. The benchmark's workloads but `io`, on both VMs, against `main`'s, alternately, three rounds, and the stack VM's `loops` and `templates` five more, were within the machine's noise. | Proposed |
| String methods | From 16 November 2026 (§3.5): in the runtime, which both VMs share. Searching compares UTF-16 code units, ordinally, never by culture. `indexOf(search, from)`, `from` optional, 0 to the length (WL3005 outside), gives where `search` first starts at or after it, `from` itself for an empty one, else -1; `lastIndexOf(search)` where it last starts, the length for an empty one, else -1. `slice(start, end)` is as bytes' is, `end` optional, WL3005 for a range outside the string, never counting from its end. `split(separator)` keeps empty parts, an empty separator splitting it into its Unicode scalar values. `trim()` and the others remove what .NET's `char.IsWhiteSpace` calls white space. Case changes each UTF-16 code unit as the invariant culture does, `"Straße".toUpper()` being `"STRAßE"`. `replace(old, new)` replaces every `old`, left to right, none overlapping, an empty `old` WL3001. `repeat(n)` takes an integer, 0 or more (WL3001 otherwise); `padStart(width, pad)` and `padEnd` a width in Unicode scalar values and a pad, a space unless given, repeated and cut at a scalar value, an empty one leaving the string as it is; a result that would not fit in the execution's memory is WL3003 before it is built. `separator.join(array)` takes an array, its strings as they are, `null` as nothing, its integers, numbers, decimals, Booleans and dates as `write` writes them, anything else, an instance of a class of values among them, WL3001, its text being its `toString()`'s, which a program gives first; an array's own `join(separator)` comes with the array methods. JavaScript's `substring` is not provided, its meaning differing from C#'s From 18 November 2026, `trim`, `trimStart` and `trimEnd` take 0 or 1 arguments (WL2006 otherwise), a string (WL3001 otherwise), its characters each a scalar value, a surrogate pair one character, never its halves, so that `"😀b".trimStart("😃")` trims nothing; `removePrefix` and `removeSuffix` take 1, a string, compared ordinally, code unit by code unit, as `startsWith` and `endsWith` compare, `""` removing nothing; each gives the string itself where nothing is removed. Both are `WebLingoMember`s, `RemovePrefix` and `RemoveSuffix`, beside the strings' others | Proposed |
| Array methods | From 16 November 2026 (§3.6): `insert(i, v)`, `i` from 0 to the length, and `removeAt(i)`, which gives the element removed, change the array, as `push` and `pop` do, a position outside it WL3005; `indexOf(v)` and `contains(v)`, or `includes(v)`, compare by `==`'s own comparison, a class's operator aside; `slice(start, end)` is as a string's and bytes' are; `concat(other)` takes an array; `join(separator)` a comma unless a separator is given, as JavaScript's, its elements written as a string's `join` writes them; `reverse()` gives a new array. These are the runtime's, which both VMs share. Those that take a function, `map(f)` or `select`, `filter(f)` or `where`, `reduce(f, initial)`, its `initial` required, `find(f)`, `null` when none is found, `any(f)` or `some`, `all(f)` or `every`, and `sort(compare)`, are written in WebLingo, an extension of `Array` the compiler adds to a program that names one of them, on anything, its kind being known only when it runs, so that each calls its function as the VMs call any function, given the element alone, its functions native, an error in one reported where it was called. A function that gives a task, an `async` one's, is awaited, the method then giving a task of its result, which the program awaits, `await a.map(load)`; an error the method itself raises after awaiting one, a predicate's result that is not a Boolean, is reported where the method was called too, its call no longer on the stack but kept by its frame from the end of its first run (17 November 2026; until then, at the program's end). `sort(compare)`, stable, a merge sort, orders by `compare(a, b) < 0`, never awaited, or without one by `<`, decimals and dates included, which `<` orders too. JavaScript's and C#'s other names are aliases where their meaning is the same: `includes`, `select`, `where`, `some` and `every`; JavaScript's `splice`, and LINQ's `Aggregate` and `First`, whose arguments or meanings differ, are not. The warning, WL2017, is for `x.sort();` or `x.reverse();` as a statement, whatever `x` is | Proposed |
| Regular expressions | From 16 November 2026 (§3.6): a kind of value, `WebLingoValueKind.Regex`, a .NET `Regex` in a heap object, immutable; its class, `Regex`, the last of the classes of values (the verifier takes 12 to 17 of them), a class of values as `Decimal` is, which a class may extend. `new Regex(pattern, flags)`, the flags a string of letters, none unless given, `i`, `m`, `s`, `x` and `n` (.NET's `IgnoreCase`, `Multiline`, `Singleline`, `IgnorePatternWhitespace` and `ExplicitCapture`), each once, always with the invariant culture and never compiled to CLR code; a pattern or a flag that is not one WL3001. `r.test(s)` or `r.isMatch(s)`; `r.match(s)` or `r.exec(s)`, the first match, an array of its groups' text in .NET's numbering, the whole match first, a group that took part in none `null`, or `null` for no match; `r.matchAll(s)` or `r.matches(s)`, an array of them; `r.replace(s, replacement)`, every match replaced, by .NET's substitutions, `$1`, `${name}`, `$0` and `$$`; `r.split(s)`, as .NET splits, its captured groups among the parts; a string's `replace`, `split`, `match` and `matchAll` given a regular expression do the same; `r.pattern` and `r.flags`. A match exceeding its time limit raises a catchable `TimeoutError` (WL3010). By default it is one second. The owner approved a per-run override in milliseconds on 19 November 2026. `weblingo run --timeout <ms>` (source, register bytecode or stack bytecode) and `wasp run --timeout <ms>` set a positive integral value up to 2,147,483,646. The VM reads the limit from its execution context when creating the heap. Its text is `Regex:` and `/pattern/flags`; `==` is identity; it is no key of a map | Proposed |
| JSON | From 16 November 2026 (§3.6): `Json`, a class of no kind's, after `Regex` among the classes of values (the verifier takes 12 to 19 of them, `Html` since 17 November 2026), `new Json()` a plain instance, and a class extending it reaching `Json.parse`, from 18 November 2026 (§3.7, "No sealed classes"; sealed until then, `new Json()` WL3001), as `Html`'s. `Json.parse(text)` reads RFC 8259's JSON, in the runtime: an object as a map, a later key's value replacing an earlier one's; `\uXXXX` escapes, a surrogate pair joined, half of one WL3001; a number too large for a binary64 its infinity, as .NET reads it; nesting at most 512 deep; anything else, leading zeros, a trailing comma, text after the value, WL3001, naming the line and the column, as diagnostics count them. `Json.stringify(value, indent)`, `indent` 0, compact, unless given, up to 10 spaces, each element and entry then on a line of its own and `": "` after a key, writes a map as an object, its keys strings or integers, written as their digits; an array, or a set, as an array; a string escaped, its quotes, backslashes and control characters, the rest as it is; an integer, a number, as `write` writes it, so that `5.0` reads back as a number, and a decimal, without its `m`, as JSON's numbers; a date as its ISO 8601 text; an instance of a class of values as the value it holds; a collection of a class extending `Array`, `Map` or `Set` as its kind; and another instance as its fields, as `write` writes them. A class's `toJson()`, if it has one, gives what is written in its instance's place, the VMs calling it as they call a `toString()` for `write`: the prelude's `Json.stringify` gives `toJson(value, indent)`, a name only the prelude has, a new instruction of each VM, `ToJson`, which builds the text with `WebLingoJsonBuilder`, as `ToText` builds `write`'s with `WebLingoTextBuilder`, both through `IWebLingoTextBuilder`. A number that is not finite, a function, a class, a task, a module, bytes, a regular expression, a key of another kind, and a value that contains itself, a `toJson()` that gives its instance among them, are WL3001, reported where `stringify` was called. The benchmark's workloads but `io`, on both VMs, against `main`'s, alternately, three rounds, were within the machine's noise, `templates` 5.2 ms against 5.3.. From M0.20.2 (18 November 2026), `Json` is `std:json`'s, a standard module the compiler makes itself (`WebLingoStandardModules`), whose instance's one member is the built-in class, made where the program begins as every built-in class is, and which a program has only by importing it, `import { Json } from "std:json";`, or `Module.import("std:json")`; `std:html`'s `Html` likewise, which a page's own escaping, `Html.js(x)` in a script, uses without an import, as the built-in classes' own sources use each other | Proposed |
| Network access | From 16 November 2026 (§7.9): `std:http`'s `get(url, headers)`, `post(url, body, headers)` and `request(method, url, body, headers)`, `headers` and `body` optional, a method's name any letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`, sent in upper case, each a task of a map, `"status"`, an integer, `"headers"`, a map of each header's name in lower case to its value, a header sent more than once its values joined by `, `, `"body"`, its text in the charset its `Content-Type` names, UTF-8 unless it names another the core knows, and `"bytes"`; a body to send a string, sent as UTF-8, or bytes, and headers a map of names to strings, a header .NET does not let a request have WL3001; a redirect given as it is, not followed, so that a script reaches only hosts it names; a request waiting at most 30 seconds to connect and 100 seconds in all, and a body at most 64 MB, read whole. `std:net`'s native class `Socket`, made by its factory, `Socket.connect(host, port)`, a port from 1 to 65535, a task of the connection; its `read()`, a task of the bytes that have come, at least one and at most 64 KB, `null` once the other end has closed; its `write(data)`, a string, sent as UTF-8, or bytes; and its `dispose()`, which closes it, once. An address that is not an `http` or `https` one is WL3001, and a host the application may not reach a `NetworkPermissionError`, WL3017 (17 November 2026, in place of WL3001); a host that cannot be reached, a connection refused or broken, a request that takes too long, and a body too large are a `NetworkError`, WL3014, its message .NET's. `weblingo`'s scripts reach any host (`WebLingoCommandLineProgram.ReachesAnyHost`), `weblingo.config`'s `"network"` read but not used; `wasp`'s, under `wasp run`, `wasp check` and `wasp serve`, and the web host's, only those its `wasp.config`'s `"network": { "allow": [...] }` lists, a name or an address, compared ignoring case, any port, none without the setting. A host is checked as the script names it, in `DotNetNetwork`, where the connection is made, and the addresses it resolves to are not, so that a name the list allows may resolve to any address (§7.9's DNS rebinding); TLS is verified as .NET verifies it | Proposed |
| Database access | From 16 November 2026 (§7.11): `std:db`'s class `Database`: its factory `open(nameOrConnection)`, a task of a connection, by a name the configuration gives, without a colon, or by a connection string, a provider's name, a colon, and what the provider reads; `query(sql, values, options)`, a task of an array of maps, each from a column's name to its value, a later column of the same name replacing an earlier; `execute(sql, values, options)`, a task of the rows changed, 0 for a statement that changes none; `begin(options)`, `commit()` and `rollback()`, a transaction's steps, each a task, a connection's transactions not nesting; `transaction(work, options)`, written in WebLingo, which begins, awaits `work()`, and commits, giving what `work` gave, or rolls back and throws on; `provider` and `serverVersion`; and `dispose()`, once, a transaction still open rolled back. A statement's values are a map of names, ASCII letters, digits and `_`, to `null`, Booleans, integers, numbers, decimals, strings, bytes and dates, each `@name` in its SQL, any other value WL3001; its options a map, a statement's `{ timeout: 30 }`, in seconds, an integer, 0 or more, and a transaction's `{ isolation: "serializable" }`, `readUncommitted`, `readCommitted`, `repeatableRead`, `serializable` or `snapshot`, any other option WL3001, and one the provider does not take WL3001 where it is used. A connection's operations run one at a time, a second waiting for the first, those a script starts without awaiting them in no defined order (the owner's answer, 17 November 2026, §7.11). SQLite's values come back as integers, numbers, strings and bytes, a column declared a decimal (`DECIMAL`, `NUMERIC`, `MONEY`) read as a decimal, a Boolean (`BOOL`) as one, and a date (`DATE`, `DATETIME`, `TIMESTAMP`) as one where its text reads as a date, a time without an offset the server's; a decimal and a date go in as SQLite's text. SQLite's connection string is a file's path, or `:memory:`, or SQLite's own, which holds an `=`; a relative path in a configuration's is from its folder, in a script's from the process's current folder; a file that is not there is made; and the provider pools connections. A name no configuration gives, a provider WebLingo does not have, a connection string that is not one, a closed connection, a value the SQL names but the map does not give, and a transaction that is not open are WL3001; the database's own failures, a statement it refuses, a file it cannot open, are a `DatabaseError`, WL3015, its message the provider's. `weblingo.config`'s and `wasp.config`'s `"databases"`, each a name and a connection string, are read by `weblingo` and `wasp` alike, and a script's connection string is allowed under both, capabilities (§7.13) not yet limiting either. The packages, in `WebLingo.Data.DotNet` only: Microsoft.Data.Sqlite 10.0.15 and Microsoft.Data.Sqlite.Core 10.0.15 (MIT), SQLitePCLRaw.bundle_e_sqlite3, .core, .provider.e_sqlite3 and .lib.e_sqlite3 2.1.13 (Apache-2.0), the last holding SQLite itself (public domain) for each platform. From 17 November 2026, SQL Server's provider, `sqlserver`: its connection string SqlClient's own, passed on as it is once SqlClient has read it, a keyword it does not know, or one without a server, WL3001; SqlClient's managed network layer on Windows too, its switch (`UseManagedNetworkingOnWindows`) set before it first connects, its native one, Microsoft.Data.SqlClient.SNI.runtime, excluded from the build (the owner's decision, §7.11), so that a server is reached by TCP/IP, never by its shared memory; its values as their types say, integers from `TINYINT` to `BIGINT` as integers, `FLOAT` and `REAL` as numbers, `DECIMAL` and `MONEY` as decimals, `BIT` a Boolean, text strings, `VARBINARY` bytes, `DATETIMEOFFSET` a date with its offset, `DATE`, `DATETIME` and `DATETIME2` a date on the server's clocks, and anything else, `UNIQUEIDENTIFIER` and `TIME` among them, its text; a statement's values typed by SqlClient from their kinds, an integer a `BIGINT`, a string an `NVARCHAR`, a date a `DATETIMEOFFSET`, `null` untyped; and its packages, Microsoft.Data.SqlClient 7.1.2, with Microsoft.Data.SqlClient.Extensions.Abstractions and .Internal.Logging 7.1.2, Microsoft.IdentityModel.Abstractions, .JsonWebTokens, .Logging, .Protocols, .Protocols.OpenIdConnect and .Tokens 8.16.0, System.IdentityModel.Tokens.Jwt 8.16.0, Microsoft.Extensions.Caching.Abstractions, .Caching.Memory, .Options and .Primitives 9.0.21, Microsoft.Extensions.DependencyInjection.Abstractions and .Logging.Abstractions 10.0.0, Microsoft.Bcl.Cryptography, System.Configuration.ConfigurationManager, System.Diagnostics.EventLog, System.Security.Cryptography.Pkcs, System.Security.Cryptography.ProtectedData and System.Threading.RateLimiting 9.0.21, and Microsoft.SqlServer.Server 1.0.0, all MIT | Proposed |
| Native classes' methods written in WebLingo | From 16 November 2026 (§3.9), for `std:db`'s `transaction`, which calls a script's function and awaits what it gives, as a native function cannot: `WebLingoNativeClass.DeclareWebLingoMethods(source)`, methods as a class's body declares them, which reach the class's native members through `this`. The host reads them when it loads the module (`WebLingoNativeClassInfo.TryParseMethods`), a source that does not parse, that declares anything but methods, or a method whose name another member has failing the module; the binder binds them with the class's native members, as native functions, so that an error in one is reported where it was called, after it has awaited too, its frame keeping its call from the end of its first run, when the scheduler resumes it with no caller below it (17 November 2026; until then, such an error, `transaction` given what is not a function, was located by the method's own positions in the caller's file); and a `.wlbc` holds them as it holds any class's methods | Proposed |
| Backpressure | From M0.14.7.3 (§6.12, "Streaming"): built in the compiler and the host, the VMs unchanged but for M0.14.7.2's waiting, rather than in the VMs as the owner's question supposed. A built-in a compiler's host declares `Writes` (`WebLingoBuiltinDeclaration`), the template compiler's writers, `write` and `writeLn`, called as a statement where the program can `await`, at a page's, a layout's, a component's or a module's top level or in an `async` function, is followed by `if (full()) await drained();`, two built-ins no program can name (`WebLingoTemplateWriters.Full` and `.Drained`), a template's own writer giving whether the output is full itself, `if (write(x)) await drained();`, so that a page's write costs no second call; so a page waits only when its output is full, `await` otherwise never running, and no other task running at a write; in a function that is not `async`, where it cannot wait, a write's output is kept until the page next writes where it can. `full()` is true while the output (`IWebLingoWaitingOutput`) holds more than its client has taken, which only a page's being sent does: its response's body full (WLHI's `IWebLingoBodyWriter.IsFull`, a write never waiting, `WhenReady(timeout, ready)` calling back once it can take more, or once the timeout has passed, its host keeping the time, so that the portable core keeps no timer); `drained()` gives a task the host completes then (`WebLingoScheduler.TryWaitForHost`), for which the execution waits as for an operation off its worker. The ASP.NET Core adapter's response is full while four pieces of its body wait to be sent. A wait for the client longer than the send timeout (`WebLingoApplicationOptions.SendTimeout`, `wasp.config`'s `"sendTimeout"`, a number of seconds more than 0, 30 by default) cancels the request, its response aborted and a line in the host's log, `A page's client took nothing of its output for 30 seconds, and its request was cancelled: file(line,column): error WL3004: …`. Tried with `wasp serve` on Windows: a 40 MB page sent whole to a client that reads, and, `"sendTimeout": 1`, cancelled for one that never does. On the benchmark's `templates`, measured against the work before it, alternately, three times each (16 November 2026), the check costs nothing beyond this machine's noise on the register VM, and up to a tenth on the stack VM, a write running one more instruction; and M0.14.7.2's waiting changed no other workload's time beyond the noise | Proposed |
| Output sent as it is written | From M0.14.7.1 (§6.12, "Streaming"): a page's output is kept until it reaches 64 KiB of UTF-8 (`WebLingoResponseText`), then its response committed, its `Content-Type` set unless the page set one, and the output kept sent; after that, it is sent each time 16 KiB more is kept, and the rest once the page has run, a surrogate whose pair may follow kept for it. A page that fails before then is a 500, as before; one that fails after has what it wrote sent, then, in development, its error, as a 500 page shows it, in `<pre>`, escaped, and its response aborted; in both environments, the error is a line in the host's log, `A page failed after its response was sent: file(line,column): error CODE: message`, which `WebLingoApplicationOptions.Log` takes, and `wasp serve` writes to its output, as it does its reloads. A status or a header set after then is WL3001. The ASP.NET Core adapter's response (`WebLingoStreamingResponse`) passes its commit, its body's pieces and its end or abort from its worker to the request's own task, which sends them as they come, at most four waiting, so that a write beyond them keeps the worker waiting until the client has taken one or gone, until M0.14.7.3, after which a write never waits, the page waiting for room instead ("Backpressure"); a body that comes in one piece is sent with its `Content-Length`, any other in chunks, and an abort closes the connection | Proposed |
| The request and the response in scripts | From M0.14.3 (§6.12): `Request`, `Response` and their headers' class, `Headers`, are native classes of the host's core (`WebLingoHttpObjects`), made in a program as a native module's are, under names no script can write, so that a page's own class of that name hides nothing and no script makes one; the compiler's `HostParameters` give the program's own file's `param request;` and `param response;` the instance the host's built-in makes, given their classes, as a `const` set where the statement stands, after the imports, so that an import's parameters cannot be given them; a component's or an import's parameter of those names is its caller's, and a page without them has neither. Their instances and headers are kept by the heap's lasting roots (`AddLastingRoots`) for the execution's life, which nothing else reaches them by. A request's members: `method`, `path`, `scheme` and `host`, strings as WLHI gives them; `query`, a map of its names to their values, decoded (`+` a space, `%xx` a byte of UTF-8), a name given twice having its last value, as PHP's `$_GET` has, the same map each time it is read; and `headers`. A response's: `status`, an integer from 100 to 999, `headers`, and, from 20 November 2026, `write(value)` and `writeLn(value)` ("The console"). `Headers`: `h[name]`, a header's values joined with `", "`, a missing one WL3007, as a map's key, `h?[name]` then `null`; `h[name] = value`, a string, in place of its values; `has(name)`, `add(name, value)`, and `remove(name)`, giving whether it was there, as a map's does; names compared without regard to case; a request's never changed, a response's only until it is sent, a wrong name or value, or a change they cannot take, WL3001. A request's body is read with `text()` and `form()` from M0.14.7.4 ("A request's body"). A route handler's function runs as a page that imports its file and calls it, `await handler.get(request, response);`, compiled once on each worker for each function, named `file#function`; what a `.wlg` exports is read from its text, once on each worker, a text that does not parse being taken for a handler's, so that its errors are the 500's. The methods are `GET`, `HEAD`, `POST`, `PUT`, `DELETE`, `PATCH` and `OPTIONS`, each the function of its name in lower case, `HEAD` calling `get` when there is no `head`, its body left for the host to drop; any other method is a 405, whose `Allow` lists those the handler answers, and a `.wlg` exporting none of them a 404, a module rather than a handler. A handler's output, `response.write`'s (`write`'s until 20 November 2026, "The console"), is its body, its `Content-Type` `text/html; charset=utf-8` unless it set another, as a page's; and a 404, 405 or 500 has only its own headers, any a page set before it failed removed. From 16 November 2026 (the owner's answer): a layout's or a component's `param request;` and `param response;` are module parameters whose default is the host's instance, so that its page need not give them, and may give others, the compiler's check of a layout's values, and of an import's, not asking for them; and a program's own host parameters are made before its imports run, its built-in classes first, so that a page gives them to its layout, which a page's layout statement, an import, read before they were made, as WL2004, or, when nothing captured them, as bytecode that failed its verification (WL4001) | Proposed |
| The ASP.NET Core adapter | From M0.14.4 (§6.1, WL-H009): `WebLingo.Hosting.AspNetCore`'s `UseWebLingo` ends an ASP.NET Core pipeline, under IIS through the ASP.NET Core Module or on Kestrel. A request's path is its path without the application's base (`PathBase`), so that an application under an IIS virtual directory has the routes it has at a site's root; its body is read whole before it runs, and its response sent whole after, until M0.14.7. It runs on a pool of the adapter's own threads, `WebLingoWorkerPool`, each a worker, since a VM keeps its thread while it runs, so that it never holds one of ASP.NET Core's; a client that goes away cancels it. A file the application leaves to its host is sent with the content type ASP.NET Core gives its extension, and an extension it does not know is a 404, as ASP.NET Core's own static files are | Proposed |
| `wasp serve` | From M0.14.5 (§6.12, §8.4): `wasp serve [--root <folder>] [--port <port>] [--scheme <name>=<folder> …]` serves the application at its root, the current folder by default, a relative one from the current folder, or, under IIS, from the site's, the folder the module starts it in, out of process, the module's `ASPNETCORE_IIS_PHYSICAL_PATH` being the application's id there, `/LM/W3SVC/2/ROOT`, not its folder (found 18 November 2026), and the folder that variable names only where it names one, on `localhost`, port 8080 by default, until it is stopped; under IIS, which it knows by the ASP.NET Core Module's `ASPNETCORE_PORT` or `ASPNETCORE_IIS_PHYSICAL_PATH`, on the port the module gives, the module running it out of process, as a site's `web.config` names it. Its environment is development locally and production under IIS, unless the root's `wasp.config` names one, `"mode": "development"` or `"production"` (`"environment"` until 18 November 2026), a setting only `wasp serve` reads. It has a worker for each processor, each of which loads `std:system` and the native modules on its own. A page's import of a path starting with `/` is from the application's root under `wasp run` as under `wasp serve`, as an include's is, so that a page imports the same files whichever command runs it | Proposed |
| Installing WebLingo on a server | From 16 November 2026 (§6.9): `publish.ps1`, in the solution's folder, publishes `wasp`'s project, whose output holds `weblingo` too, which `wasp` references, with their libraries and `std/`, into one folder, `publish/WebLingo` by default or the one `-Destination` names, both needing .NET's runtime, which the ASP.NET Core Hosting Bundle brings to an IIS server with its module. On Windows, the installation's folder is `C:\Program Files\WebLingo`, which a site's `web.config` names by its full path, `processPath="C:\Program Files\WebLingo\wasp.exe"`, since the module's documentation does not say that it expands environment variables there; installed elsewhere, a site's `web.config` names that folder. A running `wasp` keeps the installation's files in use, so that an update stops the sites first. A site's `web.config` runs `wasp serve` without `--root`, the site's folder being its root, and has the module write `wasp`'s output, when `stdoutLogEnabled` is true, to `C:\inetpub\logs\WebLingo`, outside every site, in files named after the site (`stdoutLogFile="C:\inetpub\logs\WebLingo\example1"`), so that it is neither served nor a change to the application's files, which would reload it (§6.5), its reload then written to the log in turn; whoever enables it creates the folder and gives the site's application pool write access to it. Tried by running the installed `wasp` as the module starts it, with its variables, in the example site's folder; not yet under IIS | Proposed |
| WebLingo's installers for Windows | From 18 November 2026 ("Minimal WebLingo Installer: Windows", the owner's answer: two zips, one with .NET's runtime and one assuming it is installed): `Installers/Windows/package.ps1` publishes `weblingo`'s project for Windows on x64, `win-x64`, self-contained and framework-dependent, without its symbols and SqlClient's resources in other languages, each into a zip, `WebLingo-<version>-win-x64-self-contained.zip`, about 37 MB, and `…-framework-dependent.zip`, about 3 MB, which needs .NET 10's runtime; each holds, at its root, the folder `WebLingo`, `weblingo` and the standard modules, `install.wlg`, `windows.wlg` and `weblingo.config` from `Scripts`, and `readme.md`, a human's steps: unblock and unzip it, then, as administrator, in its folder, `WebLingo\weblingo.exe run install.wlg`, which copies `WebLingo` into `C:\Program Files\WebLingo` and puts that on the machine's `PATH`, once. `wasp` is not in them, WASP's installer adding it; x64 only, for now; not signed | Proposed |
| WASP's installers for Windows | From 18 November 2026 ("Minimal WASP Installer: Windows"): beside WebLingo's zips, `package.ps1` makes one for WASP of each kind, `WASP-<version>-win-x64-self-contained.zip`, about 12 MB, and `…-framework-dependent.zip`, under 1 MB, for a machine with WebLingo installed from the zip of the same version and kind, its folder `WASP` what `wasp`'s publish has that `weblingo`'s has not, or has otherwise, ASP.NET Core's libraries among them, and `wasp-needs.json` the SHA-256 of each file of `weblingo`'s it shares, beside `iis-install.wlg`, `add-wasp-site.wlg`, `windows.wlg`, `iis.wlg`, `weblingo.config` and `readme.md`. As administrator, `weblingo run iis-install.wlg` checks the installation's files against `wasp-needs.json`, a WebLingo of another version or kind refused; turns IIS on, unless `appcmd` is there, through DISM, with `IIS-WebServerRole`, `IIS-WebServer`, its common HTTP features, HTTP errors, logging, request filtering and its manager; installs the ASP.NET Core Module unless IIS's global modules have `AspNetCoreModuleV2`, from the Hosting Bundle that Microsoft's release metadata for .NET 10 names, its latest, `releases[0]["aspnetcore-runtime"]["files"]`'s `dotnet-hosting-win.exe`, downloaded with `curl.exe` and checked against its SHA-512 by `certutil`, then run quietly, with `OPT_NO_RUNTIME=1 OPT_NO_SHAREDFX=1` for a self-contained `wasp`, `/repair` where the module's file is there without IIS's having it, as for a bundle installed before IIS, and IIS restarted; and copies `WASP` into the installation, the pools of the sites that run its `wasp` stopped meanwhile. x64 only, for now; not signed | Proposed |
| Installers for x86 | From 18 November 2026 (the owner's request, the same day, for zips to download, nothing installed for x86 on the development machine): `package.ps1` makes each of WebLingo's and WASP's zips for x86 too, `win-x86`, for 32-bit Windows, and for 64-bit Windows's 32-bit programs, such as a site using a COM component made for 32 bits only, which a 64-bit process cannot load; `install.wlg` and `iis-install.wlg` install a zip in WebLingo's folder for its architecture, read from its program's `.deps.json`, `C:\Program Files\WebLingo`, and for x86 on 64-bit Windows `C:\Program Files (x86)\WebLingo` (`windows.wlg`'s `architectureOf` and `programFiles`), whichever `weblingo` runs them; the Hosting Bundle given its 32-bit runtimes for a framework-dependent `wasp` for x86; and `add-wasp-site.wlg` checks the `wasp` the site's `web.config` names, rather than one folder's. The `PATH` takes WebLingo's folder after any other, so that where both are installed, `weblingo` by its name is the one installed first. Tried on x64 Windows: the x86 zips' `weblingo` running every example as x64's does, COM and SQLite among them; not on 32-bit Windows, and not installed. And for arm64, Windows on ARM (the owner's request, 19 November 2026), installed as x64's are, in Program Files, built here, its programs and SQLite's library ARM64's, but run nowhere, this machine being x64 | Proposed |
| Reloading | From M0.14.6 (§6.5, WL-T007): a new generation is healthy when its routes can be made, and published then; its pages are compiled when first requested, as the first generation's are, since an include or a layout is a `.wasp` that does not compile alone, so that a page a change breaks is a 500 in the new generation, its error shown in development. A burst of changes, the files unchanged for 250 milliseconds after it, makes one generation, and two reloads never run at once; a change the watcher's buffer cannot hold reloads too. A change to `wasp.config` (its schemes, its environment) needs `wasp serve` started again. Generations are numbered from 1; `wasp serve` writes a line for each reload, and for one that fails, which keeps the generation served | Proposed |
| Pages | From M0.12.1 (§13, "Contracts"): the template compiler (`WebLingoTemplateCompiler`, `WebLingo.Templates`) reads a page into the WebLingo parser's tokens (`WebLingoTemplateParser`), which the WebLingo compiler compiles as a script's, for either VM: the parser and both compilers take tokens, or a parsed tree, from another reader, and the lexer may be given the delimiter that ends its code, as `%>` ends a block, where it stands as a token would. Each run of the page's text between blocks is a call of a built-in that writes it as it stands; `<%= x %>` and `<%- x %>` are calls of built-ins that write `x`'s text escaped, or as it stands, both named so that no program can name them (`<%=>` and `<%->`, `WebLingoTemplateWriters`, `WebLingo.Core`), which the template compiler declares and a host that runs pages registers (`WebLingoTemplateBuiltins`, `WebLingo.StdLib`). A block's mark follows its `<%` directly (`<% -x; %>` is a statement). A `<%#` block, which holds no code, ends at the first `%>`. A block without its `%>` is WL5001, at its `<%`; `<%= %>` and `<%- %>` without an expression are WL1001, at their `%>`, and a `;` in one (`<%= x; %>`) is WL1001 too. A line whose blocks are all `<% … %>` and `<%# … %>`, any number of them, the rest of it spaces and tabs, writes nothing, its line break included; a block that spans lines is one line's. The page's line breaks are written as they stand, CR LF, LF or CR. A page is its program's own file: its `param` statements come first, before its text, as a module's do, and it imports modules from its folder, as a script does. `wasp` (`WebLingo.Wasp.Cli`) has `weblingo`'s commands, parameters, diagnostics and exit codes, sharing its code (`WebLingoCommandLine`, given a `WebLingoCommandLineProgram`); `wasp --version` prints `WASP M0.12 (pre-alpha)`, `WASP 0.20.0-pre-alpha` from 18 November 2026 ("Versions", the register) | Proposed |
| Context-aware escaping | From M0.15 (§13, "Context-aware escaping"): the template parser reads the page's HTML through its text (`WebLingoHtmlContext`, `WebLingo.Templates`), a port of Go's `html/template`'s transitions and its `contextAfterText`, which rewrites none of the text, unlike Go's, which removes comments and escapes a stray `<`, and ends a script, a style, a title or a text area at its end tag wherever it stands, as browsers do, where Go's ignores one in a script's string; an attribute's value is read with its numeric character references, and the named ones of ASCII's punctuation, decoded; an attribute's kind by Go's table and rules (`WebLingoHtmlAttributes`, `WebLingo.Core`), `on…` a script, `style` a style, a name holding `src`, `uri` or `url` a URL, after `data-` or a namespace's prefix is taken off, `xmlns:…` a URL; a script whose `type` is not JavaScript's or JSON's is HTML's text. Each `<%= %>` calls a writer of its escaping (`WebLingoTemplateWriters.EscapedAs`), `<%=>` for HTML's text and a quoted attribute's plain value, as before, any other named by its escape and its quoting, `<%=url,quoted>`, 36 in all, which the template compiler declares and a host registers, the web host making them when a page names them, so that each is a one-argument text built-in whose instance's `toString()` the VMs run, and neither VM changed; the escapes are the runtime's (`WebLingoHtmlEscaping`, `WebLingo.Runtime`). HTML's text, a title's, a text area's and an HTML comment's, and a quoted attribute's value: the five as entities (Go writes nothing in a comment). An unquoted attribute's value: also spaces, `=`, `` ` `` and `+` as numeric entities, an empty one `zWebLingoz`. An attribute's name: a plain attribute's, of letters, digits and `-` (Go: letters and digits), in lower case, else `zWebLingoz`. A URL: at its beginning, a scheme, the text before a first `:` that no `/`, `?` or `#` comes before (Go: no `/`), other than `http`, `https` and `mailto`, in any case, `#zWebLingoz`, then normalized; after it, normalized, percent-encoded as UTF-8 with upper-case digits (Go: lower), its reserved characters and escapes kept, `'`, `(` and `)` encoded; in its query or fragment, all but letters, digits and `-._~` encoded; a `srcset`'s source checked so and normalized, its descriptor letters, digits, `.` and spaces (Go: no `.`), else `#zWebLingoz`. A script's value: what `Html.js` gives, which the binder calls, the built-in class's whatever the page names `Html`: `Json.stringify`'s text with `<`, `>`, `&`, U+2028 and U+2029 as escapes, a number or a keyword between spaces, so that `null` is `null` there, where elsewhere `<%= null %>` writes nothing, nothing being no value in a script; an error, a function given, reported at the `<%=`. A script's string: JavaScript's escapes, control characters, the quotes, `` ` ``, `&`, `+`, `/`, `<`, `>`, `\`, U+2028 and U+2029; a template literal's also `$`, `{` and `}`; a regular expression's also its specials, an empty one `(?:)`; a script's or a style's comment: nothing. A style's value: its CSS escapes decoded, refused if it holds `"'()/;@[\]`{}<>`, a NUL, `--`, or names `expression` or `mozbinding`; a style's string: CSS's hexadecimal escapes, a space after one that a hexadecimal digit or a space follows, its scheme checked at its beginning, as a URL's. After a script's value, a `/` divides. Between blocks, the code's braces are followed (the parser has its tokens): a `{…}` ends where the HTML stood at its `{`, but for an `if`'s branches and its `else`s, which end where each other's do, and an `if` without an `else`, whose branches end where it began, joined as Go joins branches, a URL's part or a script's `/` unknown where only they differ, an error where a value or a `/` then stands; a loop's body is not read twice, as Go reads it, but must end where it began. Text after a header without braces, `if (…)`, `else`, `do` or `=>`, must leave the HTML where it was; `renderComponent` and a layout's `renderBody` must stand in HTML's text; a page must end there, a component and a layout being pages. A function written in a page is read where it is written, not where it is called; `<%- %>` and `write` are taken to leave the HTML where it was; an exception that leaves a block midway is not followed. Each such fault, and HTML Go refuses, is WL5008, the first of a page only, the HTML after it unknown. `Html` is a class of no kind's, after `Json` among the classes of values (the verifier takes 12 to 19 of them), sealed, `new Html()` WL3001: `escape`, `url`, `urlPart`, `jsString` and `css` the runtime's, a value's text as `write` writes it, but for an instance's `toString()`, `null` as `""`; `js` written in WebLingo, with `Json.stringify`, its functions native, so that an error is reported where it was called | Proposed |
| Includes | From M0.12.2 (§13, "Contracts"): `include` is not a keyword. In a `<% … %>` block's code, the name `include` followed by a string literal is an include, wherever it stands (one in a loop is read once, its code run on each iteration), a `;` after it (WL1002 otherwise); `include` anywhere else is a name like any other, and `.include` a member. The template is read in its place, its text and its blocks as a page's, with its own lines; the page's imports are its imports, found from the page's folder, its text standing in the page. Each file's positions are its own among the program's (`WebLingoTemplateFiles`), the page's first, then each template's after the one before, with one position between, so that a diagnostic, at compile time or at run time, is located in its file, which it then names, as a module's does (`WebLingoTemplateFiles.Locate`); a template included twice is read once, and its code compiled at each include. The template compiler finds templates with the host's resolver (`IWebLingoSourceResolver`, the interface that finds modules); `wasp`'s (`WebLingoTemplateFileResolver`, `WebLingo.Wasp.Cli`) finds a path starting with `/` from the root, any other from the including file's folder, adds `.wasp` to a path without an extension, names a template by its path from the current folder, with `/` between its folders, and the page by the path it was given; a template outside the root is WL5002 ("it is outside the application's root"). A template that cannot be found or read is WL5002, at its path; a cycle WL5003, at the path that closes it, naming the files in it; includes nested more than 64 deep, or reading more than 16M characters in all, each include counted, WL5004 (`WebLingoTemplateParser.MaxIncludeDepth` and `MaxIncludedLength`, arbitrary and easy to change). Names that differ only in case, on a file system that ignores it, are different templates, so that a cycle through them ends at the depth limit. `wasp`'s `--root <folder>` comes right after `run` or `check`, before the page | Proposed |
| Components | From M0.12.3 (§13, "Contracts"): `renderComponent` is a built-in the template compiler declares, which the compiler knows (`WebLingoBuiltinDeclaration.RendersComponent`): a call of it, `renderComponent(path, values)`, with one or two arguments (WL2006), is a dynamic import (§3.9) that runs its file anew each time, its call giving the task of running it, which completes with the component's instance; read without a call it is WL3001, and it is no export of `std:system`. Its path is a string (WL3001), found by the templates' resolver (`wasp`'s, as an include's is: from the rendering file's folder, the root for a path starting with `/`, `.wasp` added to one without an extension); code that a template includes renders from the page's folder, its text standing in the page. A component is a page, compiled as an import when it is first rendered (`WebLingoTemplateCompiler.Components`, a `WebLingoModuleLoader` that reads a file with the template parser), and kept; its `param` statements are a module's parameters, which each render gives and checks, as an import's are, a key that is not one of them, or a parameter without a default left out, being WL5006 (naming the key, where a module's error names its kind); one that cannot be found or read is WL5005, and one that does not compile is its first diagnostic's code and message, both where it is rendered, which a catch may catch. A component does not see the variables where it is rendered; it may include templates, and render components, itself among them, as deep as calls may nest (WL3003). What it writes goes where the page's goes, as it runs: one not awaited writes until its first `await`. A runtime error in it, or in a template it includes, names that file (`WebLingoTemplateSources`, which holds the files of the page and of each component). In the bytecode, `ImportDynamic`'s flags (register D, stack A) are 1 for values, plus 2 for a component's render, as both verifiers check; a VM finds components with a loader of their own (`Components`, beside `Loader`), and its module table runs such a file without keeping its instance (`WebLingoModuleTable.RunAnew`) | Proposed |
| Native modules | From native functions (§3.9; §9, "Native modules", part 1): a scheme's name is ASCII letters and digits, a letter first, at least two of them, so that a drive letter is never one. A configured scheme's import path, `native:sqlite`, names a native module, but `std:`'s, which the compiler makes itself until part 3; the module's name is a file's in the scheme's folder, without `/`, `\`, `:` or a leading `.`, its assembly that name with `.dll`. An import path ending in `.dll` names a native module too, by its assembly's path, from the importing file's folder, the assembly being in a configured scheme's folder itself, not below it; any other path, an unconfigured scheme's included, is a file's, as a script's import is. A native module is named `scheme:module` however it is imported, so that it is one module, loaded once. Its assembly is loaded into the host's context (`Assembly.LoadFrom`), its one public class implementing `IWebLingoNativeModule`, not abstract, with a public constructor that takes nothing, made and asked to register its exports; a function's name must be a name, its arity 0 or more, each name given once. A module that cannot be found, loaded or registered is WL2015, saying why, at compile time, or at run time for `Module.import`. A function is one of the host's built-ins, named `scheme:module.function`, which no script can name, its failure, even an exception, a runtime error (WL3001, naming it), as any built-in's; its arity is checked as a function value's is, when it is called. The configuration in the program's folder, `weblingo.config` for a script and `wasp.config` for a page (`WebLingoCommandLineProgram.Configuration`), holds only `"schemes"`, and a site's its settings too (§6.12), each a name and a folder, a relative one from the file's folder; anything else, or a file that cannot be read, stops the command (exit 4) with what is wrong. `--scheme name=folder`, given any number of times between `run` or `check` and the file (for `wasp`, with `--root`), names a folder from the current one, over the file's scheme of that name; one malformed, or given twice, is a usage error (exit 2). In the bytecode, a native module is a file without a function, marked `IsNative`, whose `MakeModule` is given its functions' built-ins, as a standard module's is; the verifiers allow it no parameters. The compiler takes the host's native modules as `WebLingoCompilerOptions.Native` (`IWebLingoNativeResolver`), and the command lines give `WebLingoNativeModuleLoader` (`WebLingo.Cli`), which registers each module's functions in the execution's built-ins as it loads it. From standard modules as native modules (§9, "Native modules", part 3): `std:system` is `WebLingoSystemModule` (`WebLingo.StdLib`), installed as `std/system.dll` beside each command line's program (and the tests', which reference `WebLingo.Cli`); a command line loads it before it compiles, from the `std` scheme's folder, and gives its functions, by their own names, as the host's built-ins, which every module sees and `std:system` exports, so that another `system.dll` there replaces them; one it cannot load stops the command (exit 4) with why. A native module may register a function that writes text (`TextFunction`), which a VM gives an instance's `toString()`, as `write`'s argument; and a native function reaches the execution's output through the heap (`WebLingoHeap.Output`, which a VM sets from `WebLingoExecutionContext.Output`), where `write`, given no output of its own, writes; with none, it is WL3001. A host that is not a command line, a test's or the benchmark's, may still register the standard built-ins itself (`WebLingoStandardBuiltins`). From `std:tasks`: a standard module other than `std:system` is a native module of the `std` scheme, imported as any other is, one that is not installed being "there is no such standard module" (WL2015); `std:tasks` is `WebLingoTasksModule`, in a project of its own, `WebLingo.StdLib.Tasks`, since an assembly holds one module, installed as `std/tasks.dll`; its `all`, `any` and `timeout` are `Task`'s, the same tasks and the same errors | Proposed |
| The import taught | From M0.20.1 (§3.9, "The standard library"): a name that is not defined is WL2001, `'x' is not defined.`, unless the binder finds what it may have been meant as, which ends the message instead: a name in scope it differs from only by case, a local's, a built-in class's or a host's built-in's, `'Count' is not defined; did you mean count?`; else an export, a function or a class, of a standard module the host has, of that name, or, failing one, of a name it differs from only by case, the first in the modules' order by name, `'environment' is not defined; did you mean Environment, from std:env?`. The host's native resolver lists the standard modules (`IWebLingoNativeResolver.StandardModules`), the modules in the `std` scheme's folder, each loaded, but `std:system`, whose functions every program has, only when a program has a name that is not defined, so that a program that compiles loads none it does not import | Proposed |
| `std:system`, imported | From 20 November 2026 (§3.9, "The console: `std:console`"), `std:system`'s one standard name is `delay`, `write`, `writeLn` and the console's reading `std:console`'s ("The console", below); what follows describes it as M0.20 built it. From M0.20.3 (18 November 2026, §3.9, "The standard library"), `write` and `writeLn`, and from M0.20.4 `delay`, only by an import, `std:system`'s, `std:io`'s from M0.20.3 to M0.20.4: `std:system` is a standard module the compiler makes itself (`WebLingoStandardModules.Functions`), exporting the host's built-ins, those its implementation gives (`std/system.dll`, `WebLingoSystemModule`), which another may replace with others; every program imports it implicitly (`WebLingoStandardModules.IsImplicit`), its names, `write`, `writeLn` and `delay`, and any other the host gives, in its scope without an import, as before M0.20.3, and an import of them allowed, meaning the same. A name an import takes of a standard module's built-in, `import { writeLn } from "std:system";`, means that built-in (`WebLingoLocalSymbol.ImportedBuiltin`), called directly and checked as a built-in every program had was, `writeLn(x)` a `CallBuiltin` as before; the module's instance, `import system from "std:system";`, has them as its members. A standard module the compiler makes, `std:system`, `std:json`, `std:html`, `std:bytes`, `std:datetime`, `std:regex`, is made where the program begins, nothing of it running, and its static import gives its instance at once, not awaited, so that a program that imports only such modules is not `async` for it, as it was not when every program had their names; the import costs a program a few instructions where it begins, its own module's, a load of each built-in it exports, the standard module's and its import (six on the register VM, nine on the stack VM, for `std:system` with three built-ins). From M0.20.4, `std:bytes`, `std:datetime` and `std:regex` export the built-in classes of the kinds of values no literal makes (`WebLingoStandardModules.Classes`), whose names the binder hides from a program that does not import them, as `Json`'s, the classes made as before, so that a value of the kind keeps its members. The compiler's own tests call a test's built-ins, `print` and `printLn`, which their options declare | Proposed |
| The console | From "Console module", part 1 (20 November 2026, §3.9, "The console: `std:console`"): `std:console` is `WebLingo.StdLib.Console`, a native module installed as `std/console.dll`, whose functions are the standard library's (`WebLingoWriteFunction`, `WebLingoWriteLnFunction`, `WebLingoWriteErrorFunction` and the reading's, in `WebLingo.StdLib`), registered as any native module's are, `write`, `writeLn`, `writeError` and `writeErrorLn` text built-ins, so that an instance's `toString()` runs as for `write`. They write to the execution's output and its errors (`WebLingoExecutionContext.Output` and `ErrorOutput`, the heap's), `weblingo`'s standard output and standard error, each written as it is, UTF-8 on Windows too ("Text written on Windows"); an execution with no error output gives `writeError` WL3001, as `write` without an output, and a read without a console an `IOError`, `'readLine' reads a console, and this program has none.` A program has them only by importing them: `write(x)` without the import is WL2001, `'write' is not defined; did you mean write, from std:console?`, and `Writeln` suggests `writeLn` from `std:console`; `import { writeLn } from "std:system";` compiles, as an import of any name a module may not export does, and is WL3006 where it is used, `The module std:system has no member 'writeLn'.` A host that gives the console's functions as its own built-ins (`WebLingoStandardBuiltins`), as a test's does, gives them to every program, `std:system` exporting them, as it exports any built-in the host gives. `wasp`'s loaders refuse the module (`WebLingoNativeModuleLoader.Refused`, `WithoutConsole`), its import WL2015, `The module 'std:console' cannot be imported: a site has no console: a page writes with its own write and writeLn, a handler with response.write and response.writeLn.`, and no name not defined is suggested from it. A page's own `write` and `writeLn` (`WebLingoTemplateWriters.Write`, `WebLingoPageWriteFunction`): the template compiler declares them in place of any `write` and `writeLn` its host gives, for templates alone (`WebLingoBuiltinDeclaration.InTemplates`), which the binder lets only a template's code see, the files the template compiler read as templates (`WebLingoCompilerOptions.IsTemplate`), a page, its includes, a layout and a component, never a module it imports, nor a handler's file, `std:system` exporting neither; each writes the value's text where the page's text goes, as `write` does, `null` as `null`, where `<%= null %>` writes nothing, and gives `null`, a call of either as a statement waiting for the output as before ("Backpressure"). `response.write(value)` and `response.writeLn(value)` are methods of `Response` written in WebLingo, the value's text made as an interpolation makes it, `null` as `null`, so that an instance's `toString()` runs, then given to the response's own native `__write(text)`, which takes a string, a name a program's members are unlikely to take, as `__className`'s; a host's native class may have methods written in WebLingo, as a native module's may. A statement that calls a member named `write` or `writeLn` with one argument, where a compiler's host declares the output's wait, as the template compiler does, and the program can `await`, is followed by the wait, as a page's `write` is, so that a handler's writes wait for a slow client as before (§6.12, "Streaming"); a script's own `write` methods under `wasp` wait so too, nothing else changing. The CLI gives `wasp`'s programs no console (`WebLingoCommandLineProgram.HasConsole`). Neither VM changed. From "Write Aliases" (20 November 2026): `print` and `printLn`, registered with `write`'s and `writeLn`'s functions, text built-ins as they are. From part 3 (20 November 2026): `weblingo.exe`'s manifest (`WebLingo.Cli/app.manifest`) says `consoleAllocationPolicy` `detached`; `weblingow.exe` is `WebLingo.Windowed.Cli`, a Windows application, its program `weblingo`'s (`WebLingoMain`), which `wasp`'s project references, so that one publish holds the three programs. A command line has a console where it has a console window, or a standard output or error a file or a pipe takes, as its parent gave them, as for a debugger's or a test's pipes; else, on Windows, its text is kept, and its writers and input are switched to a console made for it, `AllocConsoleWithOptions`'s new window, `AllocConsole`'s on earlier Windows, when it first loads `std:console` to run a script, by its import or a dynamic one, from source or bytecode, for `run` and `test` but never `check` or `compile` (`WebLingoNativeModuleLoader.Loaded`, `WebLingoConsoleSession.Ensure`); a script that imports it and fails to compile shows its errors in that console. Where it never had one, a failure, a nonzero exit code, shows its errors, or else its output, in a message box titled with the program's name, an error's icon; a success shows nothing. In a console window of its own, as before, a key is waited for at the end (`WebLingoConsoleWindow`). From part 2 (20 November 2026): `Console` and `ConsoleColor` are native classes of `std:console` (`WebLingoConsoleClass`), through the platform's `IWebLingoConsole`, `DotNetConsole` on .NET, given an execution as `WebLingoExecutionContext.Console`, the heap's, by `weblingo` and never by `wasp`. A colour is a string, `ConsoleColor`'s static properties giving the names, so that a colour read compares with either, and a property set to another WL3001. A key read is a map, `{ key, char, shift, alt, control }`, `key` .NET's `ConsoleKey`'s name in lower camel case, `null` for one .NET does not name, and `char` `null` for none; `readKey`'s argument, `intercept`, `true` for a key not shown, as .NET's; `readKey` and `read` tasks, read off the worker, and `readSync`'s and `read`'s character a Unicode scalar value, a surrogate pair read whole, through the console's input (`IWebLingoConsoleInput.ReadCharacter`). `beep(frequency, duration)` both or neither; `moveBufferArea`'s last three optional, the character first, its colours the console's where left out. `Console`'s writing is written in WebLingo, a value's text as an interpolation makes it, so that an instance's `toString()` runs, given to its own `__write` and `__writeError`; `std:console`'s `read`, `readSync`, `readKey` and `readKeySync` are `Console`'s alone, not exported by name. A member without a console is an `IOError`, WL3011, `'Console.title' needs a console, and this program has none.`, and so is one the console cannot do, its output a pipe's; one the platform has not a `PlatformError`, WL3022, its message saying what is Windows's alone; a value outside what the console takes, a cursor beyond its buffer, WL3001.
